“He who fights with monsters should look to it that

he himself does not become a monster.”

Nietzsche, “Beyond Good and Evil”, Aphorism 146 (1886).

Who.

You.

What.

  The FILTER.

Say that again?

  The FILTER enables the User to experience new worlds; extra sensory
  perceptions; new realms; the event horizon of human conception
  bridging into worlds long-promised by the soothsayers and liars of
  yesteryear in their prayers of utopia while virtual in appearance, yet
  not and real all the same.

When.

Now.

Why.

Because life is but a dream, dreamt upon the prayers of the generations
now cold, but still scolding you for how wasteful you’ve been up unto
this point.

  Welcome to Project FILTER. The last thing you’ll remember before the
  world melts around you.

 

The FILTER. 

A simple device. Accepted by the host. Integrated into the body. The
benefits multiplied and the advantaged and gainfully recognized. This
device enables the User to experience new worlds; extra sensory
perceptions; new realms; the event horizon of human conception. The
worlds while virtual in appearance, yet not and real all the same.

What seems as just the mechanical thump of a helicopter rotor to the
eardrum of the User; is so much more to the perceptions of cerebellum.
But the helicopter was there. And yes, the User did hear it. And yes,
the Filter heard and recognized both. Both both did not occur at exactly
the same moment. One fifty years prior amongst the rice paddies of South
Vietnam - or maybe the racist plains of southern Alabama - and then
called for effect and the other just a stimulus during an appropriate
moment. You simply do not know. So sit down and read. Buckle your
fucking seat belt and wait for the cow to kick you in the chest.

Oh, you are not wearing a seatbelt? Then hold onto something.

Loading…

Three,

Two.

One.

Enjoy the fucking ride.

Sad, you were one of my favorite assholes,

Shards of light and flashes roll across your visual receptors.

Your brain is taken over.

The pulse of light floods your every sense.

No more rent.

No more car note.

No more feeding the cat.

Really, a cat. You let your life become dictated by an animal that
thrived on the concept of food, shitting into a sand box, and all for a
few moments of rewarding solace. Yeah, I agree they are pretty
tolerable.

Yeh, I guess I am the asshole here.

That cat was pretty boss.

Feel free to write its name here…

________________________

I’ll wait.

This is important metaphysical bullshit that cannot be replaced.

Done?

Good.

Now, fuck that hairball hacking up fur-clown.

He’s no better than the shit you passed via the natural contortions on
the western acceptable means - I am talking about a fucking toilet.

Remember your last really good shit? Good. Because it was worth it. And
now, for the rest of the story.

Via a molecular and physical bond the Filter manipulates the User’s
perceptions of the world and real world interactions by manipulating
their senses while their actions affect what is real in a direct and
often destructive way. Manipulation is merely a manner by which an
objective is reached. Like Plato, the cave wall portrayals a most
believable lie.

True immersive experience invigorates the blood and pulse of the human
condition, more real than reality; because it is. The FILTER uses the
user.

A simple illustration, the User loads an assignment protocol.

An empty room.

The User wanders around an empty room.

Undead mongrels appear before the User.

Gunshots from a rifle appear; rhythmic, sustaining, and satisfying.

The undead meet a violent end. Satisfaction hormones and pulses find
their way to the sensory receptors of the User's’ brain.

To the Users’ every sense, the he believes what he sees is merely a
simulation and his mind enjoys all the stimulus and excitement.

Visceral and bloody. Jets of black blood spray in the air. The
Murder-smells of effluence, gore, human blood fill his nostrils. The
coppery metallic smell of fresh blood only butchers, murderers, and
killers know first-hand. The sticky splash of blood on his naked skin is
real, but unreal as interpreted through the Filter’s protocols and
algorithms. It has to be this way. If the User really felt the hotness
of real blood his consciousness might reject the experience,but somehow
the experience is acceptable if it is not real and ignored as some
simulation.

But the User does not know that the room is filled with real people and
his game is murder.

He does not know he is the killer.

Every blast from his gun displays the gory destruction of an undead
monster, but his game delivers real bullets to the skulls of the victims
in the room he entered.

From within the deep bowels of a computer simulation; these truths
spring forth. The terrible reality of all those digital bits and bytes.
The sickening truth of the clicks and clanks - pulses? - of every
depravity of the “modern” human mind.

This is the truth.

The one thing we cannot excuse ourselves from.

The very thing which forces us to face the terrible truth behind our
eyelids.

We are savage.

We are not forgiving.

We are nothing better than the animals from which we spring.

Pause.

Dear Reader, this is a most uncomfortable fact beyond the presumption of
our cordial perception.

But a fact we must accept.

Truth hurts.

And the human condition is designed - if nothing else - for pain.

that are a violation of the criminal laws of the United States

State...“

Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Act of 1978, Sec. 101.

 

1 - Protocol: Wildfire

Databases syncing; sensors indicate target at residence - in pocket.
License plate confirmed on car parked outside is targets’ - 25 meters
from his townhouse. Last GPS ping from his secure-link triggered as it
linked with his home wireless router. I’ve got his webcam on his laptop
monitoring from his bedroom - nothing on screen. His television has a
webcam; rooting now to transmit static images.

###

[]

The soon-to-be late-Speaker of the House Baker’s Chief of Staff, Reggie
Watts, lay couch locked in his living room as the implanted secure-link
rattled behind his right eye just next to his caged brain. The rattling
and pings only further resonating against Reggie’s hangover.

The room dark with blackout curtains.

Wood flooring dulled form lack of polish.

A black and white cow skin area rug lies underneath a chipped
second-hand coffee table.

No pictures or art on the walls; Reggie wanted to get his deposit
back...some day.

Text messages and notes, phone calls and pings flooded Reggie’s visual
display.

He hated the implant. It was an electronic shackle around his optic
nerve constantly rattling and tethering him to reality.

In the past, he joked about taking a drill to the side of his head.
These jokes, while dark, were usually encouraged by three-or-more rum
and cokes and inspired by the delusion felt at the end of a
stress-filled day; but always with a grain of truth at the core.

Suddenly an urgent message overpowered all the others.

It was a message from a dead man. But not yet dead to the reader, our
Protagonist, Reggie.

The sender - Representative Baker, Reggie’s boss... the now late public
servant, Rep. Baker.

Although Reggie did not know of his demise, Rep. Baker was at this very
moment dying, if not already settled into the final stage of mortality
of his sweet repose, and like Henry the Eighth, he gave his honours to
the world again.

In a most final curtain call.

The constant buzz and ping from the mechanism behind his eye did more
than rattle Reggie’s nerves, they had begun to affect his focus and
effectiveness. As a result, Reggie was now finding a deficit in his
vacation time and taking more and more personal days off from his duties
as Chief-of-Staff to Speaker Baker.

This did not go unnoticed by the Representative.

Reggie had just taken the whole weekend - unheard of within the
Washington DC Beltway universe - to unplug his secure-link and ignore
the political world.

Inside the DC machine, Saturday’s are just another work day. The
political animals cannot help but find their fingers tickling the keys
of a keyboard and their minds engaged in a tilt against the windmills of
political sophistry while the rest of the world finds reprise from the
confines of these mortal and capitalistic coils.

Or better yet a booze-infused brunch with your significant other.

Generally, Sunday’s are off limits for political animals - but only if
the morning talk shows don’t produce anything juicy enough to warrant a
flurry of wonky emails that will very likely be the primary scuttlebutt
in the marbled halls of the Capitol Hill offices come Monday morning.
God forbid some Senator or top aide drops a drunken or otherwise
inebriated twitter rant. Then it was just another workday.

Writer's Note: a Wonk is a un-clever little nickname given in a
backhanded manner to potential scurvy patients who spend too much time
focused on the esoteric minutiae of policy and law making rather than
finding a real reason to interact with reality. Frankly put, they find
solace and logic in books and papers rather than fight the insanity of
the real world. Somehow they command the world with these stupid little
words and phrases.

Simply put, Wonks are more likely to understand the social and political
reasons for the sanctions against North Korea than be able to grasp how
electricity flows through the wiring in the wall into their laptops and
tablets that they are virtually melded too. Or for that matter, the
basic economics of the farm to table paradigm.

In short, too much theory and a deficit of substance.

Reggie had been saying he was taking time to focus on his health and
getting more exercise. Usually this excuse afforded little pushback from
employers inside the political machine because a healthy employee means
they have the stamina to focus on highly technical concepts for more
extended periods. If they hit the gym they are better at sitting at a
desk and hammering out reports, research, and drafts. Besides, buried in
the back of this logic was a pure concept that work kills the worker if
there is no relief.

[]

In truth, Reggie had not crossed the threshold of his gym in months,
instead he learned the local liquor store owner’s first and last name.
Moreover, the store owner was an immigrant from Sudan with a family here
in the states and about two years from becoming a naturalized citizen.
The liquor store owner knew Reggie’s name. He also knew, Reggie paid in
cash for Puerto Rican rum.

Reggie also became closely acquainted with his neighborhood pot
supplier. Cannabis has been decriminalized at the state level but
remained illegal at the federal level for almost five years inside the
District, so getting a bag of pot was not illegal at the state level,
but federal employees and staffers had to still know a guy who knew a
guy that would still be discreet in the age of “free shareable” pot.

Reggie's pot guy was named Kevin. He drove for three rideshare companies
and delivered groceries for a fourth. But if you knew the tell-tale
signs and the right code words, like Reggie did, you could figure out
that for an extra $80 he'd give you a mixtape of his favorite music that
he played in his car.

This is also another trait of a Beltway-animal. These creatures tell you
little about themselves but observing one can glean dividends in
proclivities and tendencies. In short, there is a reason you always keep
forty dollars in cash in your wallet and another forty dollars in your
shoe. One is for the bottle at the beginning of the night and the other
is for when you are jostled awake by the cabbie at four-thirty in the
morning and he is demanding you pay him for doing you the favor of
driving you to the home address you scribbled on the back of a business
card you wrapped the forty dollars around at the beginning of the night.

Reggie’s hoodie had a stain from miso soup from Friday night, but also
potentially a little vomit from the bottle of white rum he polished off
by late-Saturday morning. Either way, the three-day wear on the garment
was enough to indicate to the liquor store owner that the
early-afternoon Saturday purchase of booze was not simply a restock but
rather a continuation of the previous night’s journey.

Even the bulletproof-glass could not hide Reggie’s halftime break on his
three-day booze-stink. The only hope he had was a half hour under hot
water and solid six-hour coma to metabolize the liter of alcohol he
plowed through since 2:45 pm last friday afternoon.

The facade of a Chief-of-Staff to a Congressman was crumbling. It was
not the job demands. Honestly, a robot could take on the scheduling and
juggling demands of Reggie’s position. It was the social demand that
took they unreasonable toll.

[]

It was the back room deals. The moments that we never on a calendar or
included in a note or follow-up.

It was he darkness from which the movements of government take hold of
whole lives. and change the routes of others.

Below the Capitol in the rootcellar of democracy, where all the bodies
are buried the gears of this country grind away the sacrificed and the
damned.

A man is only suspect to hold so much before his boundaries are met. And
Reggie was facing not only the demand of his position, but also the
pressures of his skin tone along with the prejudices of his position.

No excuses, this was his reality. He had to be twice as good at his work
with zero mistakes than his contemporaries. His boss was in the majority
party for this term and the pressure to keep his soon-to-be re-election
campaign could not withstand any bumps in the road or bridge collapses
in the future.

But here is where the off-the-record meetings in the bowels of the
capitol stopped taking up time. The Speaker was losing his grip. Not
only on the party, but also on reality.

And it was Reggie that took the brunt of the toll from his Bosses
lapses. Holding up the facade of incompetence to assuage the embassament
of your boss - when it not your incompetence, but rather his inability
to grasp a calendar notification and invite grows more and more tiresome
each day, the side eyes grow more and more while one’s prospects for
employment beyond the coattails of a aging and mentally slipping
poltician in denial of their mortality.

In short, Speaker Baker could not afford to have his name attached to an
unpopular bill or any hint of scandal so close to the primary back in
their home district - not because of the politcal blowback, but more
because every news outlet and grudge holding political animal in the
District to pounce and offset the whole chessboard. The pressure grew
heavier on Reggie’s shoulders.

Nearly every ringing phonecall started to grate on Reggie’s senses. The
pressure to keep no constiuent complaint or - more importantly - major
donor from feeling slighted. This meant the staffers answered every
phone call and complaint with dignity and respect, because to have a
octogenarian suddenly feel slighted by some snot-nosed Capitol Hill
intern would find its way into the letters-to-the-editor for the local
paper and might find traction onto the local talk-radio power-hour. Or
worse, the Blue-hair NIMBY with a trust fund or a generational stock
portfolio.

Even one news-cycle of bad light in the political cycle is enough to
push approval ratings and polls numbers down. If two or three points,
then it might be recoverable with some good attentiveness from the
Speaker. But if more than five points, then the risk of losing the seat
to a rabid base is very real. If the Representative loses his seat, then
his staff - twenty professionals in all - will lose their jobs and
meager salaries. So the threat of immediate career ending conduct rings
with every phone call, email, and fax that washes into the office.

It was only further complicated with some voices on the other end of the
phone refusing to speak with “that colored fella” or even worse,
refusing to speak to anyone but “that colored fella.”

It was worse yet for Reggie when the slips and public missteps of his
boss started to get notided by other Officials. Then the other staff
started to talk. Worse than high school - it was a high-school that
actually meant something, because this high school made rules and
churned through bodies like a slaughterhouse factory farm and once you
caufgght a break in the halls of Congress, you either cashed the chips
in and took the paydays and lobbying suites - Or yu long-hauled it and
took the time to build a stock portfolio that looked like your bosses,
with a few “gifts” from a well-placed donor or two, and then you don’t
mind the low staffer pay when you have the retirement plan of a Senator.

Worse, you have to hide and in politics it is often best to be obscure,
but when you are the only black man standing next to the Representative
at the press conferences you tend to get noticed.

Like all of us, Reggie was only human at the end of the week.

After years of facing the chagrins and prejudices of other ivy-league
staffers. It was Reggie who bore the controls focused on the challenges
at hand.

Reggie was too honest for the grime of the Hill. He didn’t take the tips
and try to swindle a few bucks around the donaitons accounts. The sad
fact was, Reggie was too honest for this line of work. Honesty gets used
up and shat back out on the Hill.

Reggie loved his work on the Hill, but he felt like a fish out of water.
He never compromised his integrity or accepted any bribes. He always
tried to do what was best for his constituents and the country. But that
made him a target for the sharks and the snakes who lurked in the
corridors of power. They exploited his honesty and made him look naive
and foolish. Reggie was too good for this dirty game. He deserved to
work on meaningful projects that would make a difference in people's
lives.

Reggie was good at his job, when his job was focused on public service
and working on legislation that mattered to people back home.

But then the bills crossed his desk that simply stank of filthy money.
They crossed with the imprint of th epaper clips still fresh on the
paper - the checks long slipped away into another’s pocket.

The implant rattled behind his eye; invading his personal space. The
ping stabbed Reggie in the eye and echoed in his mind. This time the
thought was different but the same. The same but new, and still the
same. Something off about the frequency of the light and the sound it
made in his mind. But then he thought that could also be the three
quarters of a bottle of rum swirling in his bloodstream.

The pulses and messages made Reggie’s face twist and twitch. His right
hand trembled. And he snapped to his feet. The message from the
late-Rep. Baker flashed across his eyes.

Reggie:

I’m not going to give in to these quasi-BLACKMAIL tactics.

I’m not elected by those fat-cat shitheads.

I’m ELECTED by MY CONSTITUENTS. If they want to go to war on issues that
I know the folks back home are going to burn my ass to the ground on if
I double-back, then they can have a WAR.

I’ve fought one before. And I’ve got plenty of fight left in me.

FILE THE DAMN PAPERS.

LEAK THE DATA.

I DON’T CARE WHAT THOSE BASTARDS SAY.

The typing in ALL-CAPS was a trait of the Late-Speaker’s and a pet-peeve
of Reggie’s. The “papers” were documents and a form to be filed with the
local board of elections officially changing the party affiliation of a
candidate currently running for office in the State of Florida. They
also included a memorandum from Rep. Baker that outlined the “gifts” and
“donations” he had received over the years from various lobbying groups:
gun rights advocates, tech firms jockeying for the latest defense
contract, and banking groups that wanted to keep a thumb on the scale of
the latest versions of laws that regulate how much money they could
squeeze out of the world. All of them highly suspect and arguably
illegal.

Late-Speaker Baker’s “old-man bravado” was the final element of Reggie’s
annoyance at this moment. The gut-fire that Rep. Baker felt was his
right to buck the system and in turn place his entire staff and
contingency at the mercy of the political tides and fallout that were
driving in from the horizon. This “Damn the torpedoes” strategy was
wholly desirable for a dramatic speech on the House floor, but rarely
desirable when one’s mortgage or monthly bills came due. Reggie had
tried to respectfully debate the pro’s and con’s with his mentor and
boss, but the old-man bravado won out with strong pointed challenges and
statements such as, “Reggie, if I can’t depend on you to do as I
instruct then I can’t depend on you in the trenches. I keep the doors
open here and if you can’t accept that, then you can find another line
place to spend your time.”

For the Beltway outsiders among you, this was either a deft and shrewd
political move of certain political suicide which essentially scuttles
the career of not only the candidate but also any of his staffers as
turncoats are not viewed favorably in DC.

It also meant that as a 30-year veteran in the halls of Congress, (soon
to be former-Speaker) Rep. Baker was no longer chained to the political
machine that raised him; unfortunately it also meant his vote and
influence was now extremely less effective because the old partisan
friendships he enjoyed before were now the opposition. Essentially,
political divorce, not common in Washington, DC.

The papers were stored in the office digital-cloud immediately
accessible by the late-Rep. Baker’s heads of staff.

Sensitive documents to say the least, but more so the end of the road
for so much effort toward a better future - now pawned for political
gamesmanship.

Hours, days, nights, weekends… endless discussion and debate concerning
the future-relevance of Rep. Baker’s political career and the future
employability of nearly every member of his staff. Reggie worried about
the kids on the staff that didn’t have a rich dad to buy another
internship. He worried about the kids that came from his law school - a
4th tier school focused on public service over the corruption that had
plagued the republic for generations since Nixon was long dead.

Those kids would have trouble enough pivoting to another progressive
staff, but worse, they faced guilt-by-association from “working for a
turncoat like Baker.”

Hating the work you do comes in any profession. But hating what you have
to do becuase you know it is going to cut a legion of throats alongside
your own is barely numbed by the bottle Reggie chose.

Reggie knew exactly what to do - the papers had to go public.

A quick message from his office account to a reporter for any of the
Hill rags or even the Washington Bulletin would do.

He wiped the air in front of him to mute the messages, pings, and emails
flashing in his eyes. He moved with purpose. To the bathroom.

Water filled his hands with cool wetness and splashed on his face.

The coldness always stunned the secure-link momentarily.

Nothing too wild, like a stutter in the resolution lines of an old UHF
television signal, but this time it stuttered a little longer than
normal.

###

“Movement. He’s up. Don’t know where. Next webcam image in three
seconds.

“Got it.

“He’s waving his arms. I’m trying to get a resonating laser on his
window or a microphone to open up inside.”

“Nevermind the microphone.” A second voice in the chat room ordered.
“Load protocol WILDFIRE and execute.”

###

His secure-link jolted back into sync. A song began to play in his head.
The Pixies, Where Is My Mind. It was an old song. Ancient by today’s
standards, but one that Reggie was always fond of. He had not heard or
thought of it years, but it was clear as rain and playing in his mind.

Suddenly his secure-link went dark with a snap behind his eyes.

Reggie’s knees buckled. He was suddenly half-blind. Like one of his eyes
just switched off.

His heads up display - gone.

Unread emails - gone.

Unread text messages - gone.

All the news alerts about Rep. Baker - gone.

Reggie stumbled out of the bathroom. His bare feet on the cool tile
floor. His head dizzy without the buzz of data. Suddenly a searing pain
shot through his brain. White hot and burning behind his right eye. It
felt like it was getting hotter. Because it was.

The number ten appeared in the field of view in Reggie’s right eye. He
saw it vividly bright white against a grey shaded background forming a
blind spot in the center of his right eye field of vision - a dead zone.

Nine.

Eight.

What is it counting down for? Reggie’s mind raced.

Seven.

The heat behind his eye was growing, like a battery getting too hot from
overuse.

Six.

Too hot.

His eye was twitching at a dizzying rate.

Five.

Batteries can...explode.

His mind flashed to moment of a memory where a middle school teacher
demonstrated an overcharged battery casing a flash, explosion, and a
fireball under a ventilation hood, for science.

Four.

Oh. Fuck. The Science was now in Reggie’s skull.

Three.

A spoon in a half-eaten bowl of cereal lay on the kitchen counter.

Two.

Pop goes the weasel. The spoon jammed between the eyelid and the round
circumference of the eyeball.

The white and blood-veined orb suddenly found itself a captive between
the socket and the cold silver of a common cereal spoon.

A quick flick of them wrist with the pull of a string of his forearm
muscles.

The number disappeared from Reggie’s sight along with half of his field
of vision and pain rushed in to fill the void.

The unsettling quiet when there once was noise filled Reggie and
then...a sudden searing and real pain filled his senses.

A pain - warm and terrible - with the pulse of a panicked drummer behind
a rapid snare drum. The beat of his blood pounded in his veins and ears.

Reggie had just torn out his own eyeball.

For what?. A sober part of his brain asked.

There was no answer.

The only thing that remained was the heat of his eye - a memory in the
cold vacuum of his skull.

With his remaining eye, Reggie watched his detached eyeball arch through
the air.

He did not dare to blink.

His right hand cupped his empty eye socket and blood spurted between his
fingers.

The eyeball still arching across the room.

The spoon clattered and spattered flecks of Reggie’s blood across the
ceramic tile floor.

His eye socket filled with blood, loose tissue, and searing pain from
the torn optical nerve and muscle.

The spoon could have been a cleaner one, Reggie thought as his tattered
senses raged in mind.

Reggie’s eyeball lay on the cold tile floor of his kitchen, inched form
the laminate flooring of the faux-hardwood flooring of his hallway.

The secure-link - a small corpuscle of circuit-board and wiring sparked,
popped, and fizzled with crackles of electricity and flash of a
polymer-lithium battery arching and exploding just behind the
circumference of his eyeball.
The light green of his former eye’s iris stared back at him. It was at
this moment Reggie suddenly wondered would he ever be able to look at
his mother in in the eye? He was always told, he got his eyes from his
mother.

And now it stared at him from the kitchen floor. Hazel. White and veiny.

That would have burned up my frontal lobe. It would have killed me.

Reggie’s skin flushed pale. He felt clammy. Cold and sweaty.

Would...have...killed me.

Suddenly Reggie heard the sizzle of the air against his ear drums; stark
reality surrounded him.

He reached for a hand towel and pressed it to his bleeding and now empty
eye socket. He fought the sensation of passing out and braced himself
against the kitchen counter. He pulled his first-aid kit from below the
kitchen sink. There was a bottle of ready-to-patch synth-skin inside.
Reggie used the whole bottle to fill his eye socket... normally patch a
cut with just a squirt - like dressing on a hot dog with mustard. It
hurt because it was binding to the nerve endings and adhering to the
wounded skin. It burned like a hot poker to Reggie’s face as the last of
the pressurized contents sprayed into his eye socket and onto his face.

The filling sensation did not escape his perception.

The synth-skin sanitized and closed the wound. He was not going to bleed
out. He would survive this self-inflicted madness.

However, the blood loss was too much. The pressure in his veins dropped.
Reggie felt the coagulation beginning to occur from his wound -
solidifying in his eye socket. The searing pain reduced to a dull ache.
Reggie was not going to die today and he fainted. The reassuring
sensation rolled over him; he was not going to die today.

Reggie crumbled into a heap on his kitchen floor.

###

“Do we have confirmation?” Operator No. 1 barked into his headset and
whipped his head around to face the other Operators in the room.

“I have confirmation,”another Operator responded. “The destruct protocol
was delivered, installed and began running,”

“I had the countdown as well,” A third operator admitted. “The process
was completing and then. Pop. Fizz. Connection lost. Jackson, do you
have anything on the emergency bands? A fire in the sandbox?”

“Nothing, not even a puff of smoke.” Jackson responded. “Henry? You said
a ‘pop’ then a ‘fizz.’ What did you mean?” Jackson asked.

“I meant what I said. The protocol was delivered. The Install caused a
slight reverb in the resolution display then began running. The target
should have shrugged it off.”

“Send me the code. I want to see it.” Operator No. 1 barked at Henry and
Jackson. A report registered on his heads up display. He swept it into
his reader and code flashed before his eyes. “Fucking sloppy coding!
You’ve got shit laying all over the place in this!” he sucked his teeth
and lobbed a javelin-like stare at his subordinate Operators.

All the operators had numbers assigned, but they always broke protocol
and used their real names. Sometimes first, sometimes last names.

got an assigned parking space, even though he never learned to drive.
One of his pet-peeves was sloppy coding. Also Peter Janowski preferred
to be called OpOne - a homage to the hacker handles of yesteryear.

open! He saw the Goddamn countdown!”

Washington Post, The Switch, January 29, 2015.

 

2 - Vision Systems

Deep in the bowels of building outside of Washington D.C. inside of
which we find a thing. A legal fiction on paper labeled Vision Systems.
But what it truly is are thousands of FILTERS are online, connected and
monitored.

The every eyes of the blank stare you find behind every social media
post and poignant statement you find some solace via the bits and bytes
of the worldwide internet.

This is your echochamber. Where all your internal screams come to drown.

This is your sustenance. Where all your emotions feed and starve.

This is your reality. Where all your dreams go to live and die.

Before you continue... Dear Reader, I beg you, disconnect.

Set this down.

Ignore the siren’s call.

Plug your ears with Odysseus's wax.

Tie yourself to the mast and go down with history.

If you venture further…

Only wickedness this way comes.

You ignore me.

And now a harbinger follows the echo of my warning.

Now.

Here, it comes.

The FILTER.

It commands and programs.

You visualize only the tip of an iceberg of conspiracy and the contrived
perceptions of mankind.

These simple lies stretch across the world; shaking the very core of
your world to the very core of you never understood as the boundary of
civility and barbarism.

The game unloads in digital bits and blocks around him, step by step

He steps toward the street and hails a cab. One pulls up and a door

"Seventeen hundred block of K Street, please.” He asked the driver. The

The cab rolled through the intersection as a firetruck and ambulance

His constituents have been voting for him for the past 25 years because

4 - The Digital Underground 

The operators kept their command rooms dim. Not for dramatic effect,
their white eye-screens worked better in dim rooms. Instead of one eye
taking the brunt force of a heads-up display, Operators had both eyes
plugged into their systems, the network, and essentially the backbone of
the whole information grid.

At the command of a synapse, the whole online accumulated knowledge and
capability of the modern world could be called to bear on the task at
hand. Those tasks could be any aspect of the surveillance state. One was
was compiling the metadata of targets and collating their contacts
within that data. The three jumps from one contact was similar to the
six-degrees from Kevin Bacon. It was not hard to connect anyone person
across the globe when given enough data. Even if that data is me
coincidence, the data does not like when a classified briefing requires
an actionable outcome - i.e. a drone strike on a desert target halfway
across the world.

The operators were kept separate from the executives. One never
trespassed from one level to the next; only the information. The white
shirted executives never saw the oceans of source material and data in
which the operators bathed. But the check against this branch was known
as the failsafe. Just in case these “white hats” decided to wear black
one day. With such access and high functions these systems also required
extreme attention to detail - so much the Operators still used physical
keyboards, bluetooth connected, no wires. Coding was still faster and
easier when the process went through human hands.

Operators were assigned three to a room. They checked in and out, but
always three to a room. Four eyes against two. This meant nothing, but -
like the failsafe button - it was a comfort to brass and executives
upstairs.

A message flashed on OpOne’s eye-screen. “Debriefing Required. IRL”. In
Real Life. OpOne hated when the military and executive types tried to
use coder-speak. This meant he had to check out of the Operator Room,
which meant disconnection from the other two Operators - a complicated
and disruptive event including a power down and disconnection of his
links currently running. This also required either hot-transfer or power
down of any ongoing User-connected operations - data gleaning, network
sniffing, etc. In essence, a huge pain in the ass.

OpOne always got headaches when he left his operating room, which is why
he did not like to leave. It was not like the brass and executives
couldn’t just video chat with him, or just look down through the
observation windows from their floor - one flight above.

As he rubbed his eyes, OpOne glanced upwards to see a man in a grey suit
watching him from the observation window. The man shifted and folded and
unfolded his arms. The overhead light obscured his face. It was Clemens.
Chief Executive Officer of Research-Development-Deployment and
Execution - “CEO-Ready” - OpOne called him. All the Operators called him
that. Acronyms were commonplace in the military-industrial-complex.

“You couldn’t just wait for the report I was writing?” OpOne shouted
upwards.

"CEO-Ready" tapped his gold and silver watch; it glinted in the light,
he turned and walked away from the glass.

“Fuckin' bullshit face to faces.” OpOne swiped his hand at a grey panel
by the door. “Operator Number One, checking out. Alpha. Bronx. Charlie.
Uno. Dos. Tres.”

A well known and ancient Jackson Five song instantly triggered in
OpOne's mind.

The remaining operators groaned as their systems timed-out and their
protocols ground to a halt. Without the third Operator, the other two
were off-line; another failsafe to prevent a rogue Operator from
syphoning of data or plot a coup.

This directive was one of those in-house policies written into things
like employee manuals. Normally they are annoying bullshit that keeps
someone from accomplishing a simple task by requiring oversight and
redundancy. Before secure-link, FILTER, and a number of other
productivity applications, this - as well as the employee manual - was
usually tossed into file 13, a desk drawer, or used to steady the
underside of a wobbly end table. After the tech innovations, these
things, when a rule was triggered by the program, froze all activity
until the logic engine of the program allowed work to continue.

OpOne slipped his hand into his thigh pocket of his operator-flight-suit
and withdrew a thin pair of dark sunglasses. He whipped them to his eyes
as the pulse from fluorescent lights began to beat on OpOne's pale skin.

It took 36 seconds to reach the elevator at the end of the hallway. Just
as he tapped the call button, OpOne received a message in his
secure-link. "Coming? Awaiting your presence."

The elevator was built into the center spire of the panopticon-like
inner building. This wagon wheel design was horribly inefficient, but it
offered the best possible perspective for the executives in the center
spire to literally keep an eye on the operators on the spokes of the
spokes. This particular building featured an outer-shell similar to the
Epcot Center - a grey geometric orb. This wasn’t just a shell it housed
the processing power and communication links to the outside world. The
inner skin of the shell was photoreactive and could be programmed to
simulate daylight, even a night time sky. Most of the operators and
executives left this building only every three or four days and to keep
suicide rates low, simulated-natural light was needed.

###

Clemens tapped his foot, not for any reason, it was like breathing for
him - his natural state was in frentic motion.

There was another man standing in the room with Clemmens. The
blacks-skinned man was clad in an Air Force dress blues uniform with all
the assorted metal, loops, and colored ribbons. The overwhelming number
of them gave instant gravitas to his presence. His name plate read
"Robinson." He was once called "Jackie" as a joke in Officer Training
School. His black skin a target early in his Air Force Officer career.
Jack Robinson would have made his rank sooner if he had not broken his
fist on some redneck’s weak teeth and jaw.

“He keeps us waiting on purpose,” Clemens muttered.

Robinson acknowledged this with a huff slightly parting his lips.

An electronic lock buzzed and a white door swung open, OpOne strolled
through it.

A debrief-operation report flashed into Clemens’ and Robinson’s
secure-links. They began scanning the information.

OpOne leaned against a blank eggshell shaded white wall and resisted the
urge to ping the network. It was a nervous tick of his. He didn’t
because he was waiting for…

“Where’s the drone footage? The sandbox audio and video? We had ‘bugs’
in there, didn’t we?” Clemens never finished reading a whole report
before peppering OpOne with questions that were answered toward the end
of every report ever generated by his operations. If he had ever really
absorbed anything about OpOne’s designs and research, he would have
known calling them drones and bugs was insulting. Drones and bugs were
like rocks and spears compared to the full potential of the FILTER.

“This is a flash-prelim report,” OpOne began to answer. “The spiders
still haven’t gathered…”

“Spiders!?” Clemens never absorbed any of the coder/operator speak that
flowed in this type of operation.

“He means this isn’t finished. It’s a rough draft,”Robinson interjected.
“You rushed it out of the factory.”

“Ding! Tell’em what he’s won, Johnny!” OpOne snapped his fingers and
rolled a pointed finger at Robinson.

“Cut the shit, Operator,” Robinson refused to call OpOne by his handle
or even his real name.

“I haven’t been able to render the video feeds or compile the audio yet.
Each of the systems we have in the sandbox are feeding through six
different databases with nine different contractors answering to
multiple layers of encryption and various systems! Two of which are now
in stasis because you guys wanted a face to face. So, sorry, I haven’t
been able to get that wrapped up with a pretty pink little bow in the
thirty minutes it has been since the operation ceased,” OpOne took a
breath. “Besides, none of the ‘aforementioned’ are even supposed to know
we are skimming that shit off their systems,” OpOne even took the time
to do the “air quotes” thing that was a pet peeve of Clemens.

“So, when should we expect a full report?” Robinson asked.

“I had my operators gleaning that information when I was dragged up
here. They had to stop because I left the room. Directive 411.”

“So when?” Robinson asked a second time.

“The gathering should be done by this afternoon. If we’re lucky and
don’t get sniffed out by someone being overzealous with the bandwidth
protocols or server loads on the outside.” OpOne calculated how much
time he wanted to sleep, but also how much time his operator team would
want as well. “I think we could have a full autopsy report ready before
noon tomorrow.”

“Noon!” Evidently, Clemens did not sleep. “How much data are you
gleaming?”

Evidently, you don’t know the proper use of the verb “to glean,” OpOne
thought. “It’s not the data, it’s the…”

“How about we get pinged with a bloody copy, when it’s ready? Then the
final by mid-morning,” Robinson said as he unfolded his arms and walked
toward the only exit of the room.

“Understood.” OpOne had hoped sleep would have been a good reward for a
successful operation. Clemens and Robinson exited the room.

“Job well, done, OpOne. Take the night off.” He jested. “No treason
charges for you.”

###

Compiling data over a vast subnet tunneled under, through, and woven
into the information grid takes time.

Any operation takes time to set up. A sandbox is used - a secured arena
for the operation to take place. In this sandbox assets are placed. The
User, the Target or objective, and protocols are installed. Setting this
up is the easy part.

Culling the information and gleaning the data from the systems that are
manipulated in order to monitor and effectuate the mission is difficult.
OpOne’s spiders - search and retrieve programs - were slipping in and
out of servers and datapipes retrieving logs and scouring databanks for
the remnants of the Wildfire protocol. The information feeding in was a
trickle of data that pooled between OpOne and his operators.

“We have the energy spikes registered when the secure-link sparked,”
Henry chirped.

“I’ve got the sound from his ambient electronics in the house,” Jackson
added. ”He still had a cordless landline plugged into a voice over IP!
We’ve got a full PRISM transcript being machined right now.”

OpOne was at an impasse. He could either wait for the computers at Fort
Meade to churn the ambient data into a transcript and then slip a spider
into it to copy out the data or have the spider piggy-back the process
in real time. One was safe, but took longer. The other would show an
unreasonable server load that an NSA tech would likely ignore. It had
been a busy day, what with a stock market tumble just after the news
broke about a Congressman dropping dead in DC. Just another ripple in
the digital world.

“Screw it, my eyes are burning,” OpOne growled. “Slip in the spider on
the ambient sound and pull it in real-time. It’s 7:30 on the East Coast,
let’s hope the tech is eating a burrito or jerking off.”

And the spider-programs shot through the fiber-optical connections and
piggy-backed the processing. The server-loads spiked, but the tech was …
indisposed… no disruption noted.

“I’m not staying here all night, gentlemen,” OpOne barked.

“There’s a lot to pack in here, man,” Henry complained.

I need more eyes and bandwidth. OpOne was about to do something that
maked Clemens and Robinson nervous even when the mission was not
quasi-terrorism.

“Jackson, ping your man in the UK,” OpOne ordered. “I’m going to try and
get a hold of Uri.” OpOne’s contact list spun in his head’s up display
to an entry for Unit 8200/ISNU.

“Sir, are you sure about this?” Jackson hesitated sending the ping.

“Eyes and bandwidth, gentlemen. We will miss deadline unless we get our
friends to pitch in. Encrypt the data streams and just ask for
processing power. It’s night over there, they shouldn’t mind letting us
borrow some processors.”

OpOne executed a little-known and forbidden-to-use protocol he designed
into the system allowing his operators to “share” their protocols to
external systems and users. Geek-speak for letting them play with their
friends after curfew.

Jackson pinged a friend of his, who was not happy about the wake up
call. He lent a server farm in North London.

Uri never slept and was happy to throw the rendering and compiling into
his Kray-quantum server.

“It’ll be done in a couple of hours,” he promised and clicked off the
video chat with OpOne. He knew accessing the one of numerous quantum
computers in the US would have triggered a few too many red flags. OpOne
liked being able to avoid red flags.

They’ll have a bloody draft before midnight. OpOne was happy that he
would see his own bed tonight.

###

“Shit! SHIT! FUCK!” Clemens usually overreacted, but this time it was
warranted.

The Baker objective met; without incident. Translation: no problems,
they were able to assassinate a Representative in the United States
Congress with no fallout or blowback. There were no indications that
anyone suspected foul-play.

The report even included diagrams and three-dimensional animated models
based on the live feeds, ambient audio from the arena, and device
metadata retrieved. If one or two microphones are present in a room and
something making sound occurs, a detailed simulation can be generated
from a sonar-regeneration program readily available to the Operators.
It's a common tool used for many years. And OpOne liked to include
pictures in his reports.

The modeling of the shooting of the Representative reminded him of an
old conspiracy documentary he saw about the John F. Kennedy
assassination. Instead of three shots from an Italian rifle in the hands
of disgruntled marxist and the ensuing national catastrophe, we have a
simulated heart attack from one energy-bolt, an unknowing middle-aged
assassin who thinks he just “leveled-up,” and a minor blip in the stock
market.

OpOne suggested the company might think about putting in a few anonymous
shorts in the market - if well-placed they could offset the cost of
these operations.

OpOne already had a very robust trading portfolio, but was becoming
bored with the “predictability” of the market these days.

The secondary objective was not accomplished.

“The target survived and was at-large. The protocol inserted via
secure-link was ineffective in neutralizing this target.” The report
read. Translation: Reginald Watts was not silenced and remains at-large;
he survived the operation by digging his own eye out of its socket.

“Are you reading this?” Clemens barked into the air around his head as
he sat bolt up in his bed. His breaths were short and quick.

“I am,” Robinson sat in his home-office. He reached for a decanter
filled with a rich amber-colored liquid and poured three fingers worth
into a glass.

“What are we going to do?” Clemens was frantic. Executives always got

It was late afternoon. There was a orange glow coming from the drawn

He flicked his hand in a gesture that, had his secure-link not lay in a

It was fortunate that this command was not executed. Had it occurred

Moron.

Suddenly, he realized this was no accident. Rep. Baker hated the

Reggie’s mind flashed back to the speech.

This technology crosses a great divide. Builds a bridge into the very

Much of the news coverage and the public debate concerning this

Too late, a rule steeped in national security concerns quietly forced

“We gave them a Goddamn tunnel right into our brains!” Reggie exclaimed.

Perhaps it was shock. Perhaps it was the dull roar of pain pulsing in

Reggie understood he was not safe. This was not an accident. And he had

Everyone has one. If you live in D.C., you are an idiot not to have one.
A “Go-Bag.” A backpack filled with - among other things - some cash, a

He dove into his hall closet. Throwing coats, hats and sweaters into the

He walked back to this kitchen counter. Swiped a ziplock bag from the

The last thing Reggie wanted to do was cause a scene. He had to get to

Fuck it. Just put on a hat. Nobody looks anybody in the eyes anymore,

Jessica Powell was one of the smartest and most-gifted technical
specialists the Air Force had ever produced. Her Commanding Officer did
not believe her when she walked into his office three years ago and told
him she wanted a position on the Hill. She always wanted to work on
public policy. She saw a chance to do something as she always wanted.
Technical Information Specialists were in high demand on Capitol Hill
after the secure-links were mandated. Lawmakers do not normally adapt to

Reggie knew the protocol. The lower staff members would have been sent

His mind raced as the city traffic whirled around him. A dog without his

Rep. Baker kept a fax machine plugged in. It never received any, but it

“Fuck! Alright! Yes, I know the damn light is green! And, No! I don’t

He could not just walk into a Kinko’s and say, “Hi! ignore the pulsing

People see this fucking neighborhood in almost every damn spy movie -

Capitol Hill still had something that Georgetown did not have. Small

Strange, he thought to himself, both times I had to visit this

the butt of his 9mm. “I mean, Miss.”

“No worries, Officer.” She answered as she rose and set the papers and
files on the file-cabinet. “You didn’t startle me. I was just trying to
clean up a few things.” She lied as through her secure-link the
visualized data as it swirling around them. It was only forty percent
transmitted according to her heads-up display.

“I know, I’ve heard the news. I saw you check-in on the entrance log and
I know that it has been rather...well. I mean.”

“Yeah, well, he was a good Congressman. Loved working for him.” Jessica
did not know why the Officer was still standing in her office doorway.

There was a brief awkward pause.

Sixty-three percent uploaded.

“Well, can I help you with anything, Officer?”

“Davis. Ed....Davis. And no, I’m sorry, I didn’t mean to bother you.”

The data uploaded sputtered and sputtered with numbers jumping by two’s,
five’s, and six’s.

“Well, thank you officer for checking on me. I am fine. Just picking up
a few things.”

Seventy-five percent uploaded. Go along your way, Cop.

“Oh, ok. Well, if you’re on your way out? I could escort you if you
wanted. My parents are from Rep.,” He paused. “I mean, the late-Rep.
Baker’s district. They moved there from Queens. That’s where I’m from.
Then the Marines.”

“Oh,” Jessica sighed. “A constituent. Well, I am almost finished up
here, I guess.”

Eighty-three percent.

“I just have to get one more thing in order. I have to...to see if I had
that one file...yes, Here it is.” Jessica feigned a grin. “I just have
to make sure this is on one of the staffers desks for Monday.”

She took a file and waved it in the air as she walked past the Officer
and out of her office toward a random desk next to one of the printers.

Eighty-nine percent.

“Wow, your office does have a lot of paper,” the Officer commented.

“Well, Rep. Baker liked paper. He was a lawyer for twenty years before
he was a congressman.”

Ninety-six percent.

“Oh, I didn’t know that. That’s cool. He must have been a good one.
Shame he died.”

“Yep,” Jessica felt the knife shift in her sleeve. “Shall we?”

“Oh sure, ladies first.”

“I’ve got to lockup,” Jessica grinned.

The Officer sheepishly grinned and walked toward the front area of the
office.

Jessica pulled her office door shut. Ninety-nine percent.

The Officer was standing at ease in the hallway. One-hundred percent.
File Purge Complete. Checking File Integrity. She could walk away from
it now. The rest of protocol would reformat all drives and install
dummy-backups of the regular work files. None of the staff would notice
any difference on Monday.

“Ok,” Jessica chuckled. “You know, you really don’t have to walk me out.
I know my way around.”

“Not a problem at all, Miss.”

His chivalry was nauseating.

They walked in silence until they got to the stairwell that lead down
and to the back staff exit.

“Oh, you know. I think I met Rep. Baker once,” the Officer suddenly
confessed.

“Yeah,” Jessica half-acknowledged the Officer as he began to ramble and
then descended the stairs. “When?”

“I think it was when I first came back from Afghanistan.” The Officer
was young. But the last mission in Afghanistan had been completed for at
least three or four years.

“You don’t seem that old. Afghanistan has been said and done for a
number of years,” Jessica was suddenly suspicious.

“Well, a lot of things happen to a guy over there.” The Officer sounded
like he was confessing. “A lot of painful things. Takes a while to
rebuild.”

“You mean get over?” Jessica prompted.

“No, ‘rebuild’ is the right word.”

They reached the exit door and stopped and look at each other. Jessica
just had to swipe her wrist across the grey sensor box and the door
would open. But her curiosity was too great.

“What do you mean ‘rebuild’?” She instantly regretted asking this
question and not just swiping her wrist across the sensor.

“I need you to swipe out, Ms. Richland.”

“Huh?”

“Your sensor, swipe out.”

She raised her arm and heard the electronic lock buzz; the door popped
open.

“Oh, one thing,” The Officer interrupted her as she stepped into the
doorway. “Didn’t you think anybody else could see the data transfer?”

A black sack slipped over her head. A hand, outside the sack, over her
mouth. Multiple sets of hands and arms wrapped around her ankles and
wrists before she could kick or swing. Her knife clattered on the
cement. She felt the puncture of a needle into her outer thigh and
zip-ties looped over her wrists and ankles. The last thing she heard
before blacking out: “Target in pocket!”

CHILLYWATER

ECHELON

Reggie clicked on one at random, labeled: “PROJECT FILTER.” The screen

read like the liner notes of old vinyl progressive rock albums and

The death of a middle-eastern despot was originally depicted in the

Metadate form a series of seemingly random phone numbers that contacted

The signals intelligence proved a pattern so convincing that OpOne set a

He then set the systems to record the audio of the encrypted phone

The cellphone triangulation data was so precise they did not need to

Out of deference to the useful connection with the despots daughter in

The despot was dragged from hiding and presented to a mob forming in the

Reggie’s blood ran cold in his veins as his mind raced back to the

5 - The User

Adrenaline shot through his veins and he loved the rush. The dopamine
and other pleasure inducing hormones fired across his synapses. More
than a normal energetic high; this was part of the design. The FILTER
supplements and ultimately changes the brain chemistry via the use of
nanotechnology to encourage the production of dopamine and other
hormones when mission objectives are met and exceeded. In turn, the User
learns to and ultimately wants to complete the mission in order to
receive the reward, like a junkie seeking a fix and given the means...at
all costs.

Josh Hendrickson was a normal guy. He had a job that he did not truly
love. A wife that he once loved, but married life and their careers
faded thier mutual bliss. A house in the suburbs. Two cars, a barbecue,
savings accounts with five digits, a retirement plan. Not having
children and high salaries allowed for this last luxury.

They had carved out a nice niche for themselves. Jennifer, his wife, had
a good public relations position with a mid-sized firm with lots of
potential. She studied hard in school and her professional skills were
sharp. It also helped that she was a workaholic. Like many Professionals
in Washington, DC they focused solely on their work and personal lives
were a distant second.

Josh was a “digital” man. He loved technology. His time in the Navy gave
him plenty of exposure to high-level cutting-edge networks and
components and he was trained extensively in all of them that his short
career allowed. He tried out for the Navy SEALS and incurred a medical
discharge when ligaments in his right knee could not keep up. Honorable
discharge and because his commanding officer made a phone call, a cozy
job with a technology contractor for the Navy waited for him. He
flourished at first, but he missed the smells of the ships and
excitement of live fire drills - even the simulated ones. A year out of
the Navy, he found the monotony burdening him every day he swiped his
ID-chip at the front door of his office building. Then he was picked for
a special program - the FILTER Project.

It was high-level research and development. It excited Josh in ways that
felt unnatural to him, but he welcomed the change. In many ways it was.
He excelled in the aptitude tests and after implantation he sparkled.
His performance biometrics reflected this.

Two years ago the project was running full-force. Every expectation and
objective was being fully accomplished and exceeded. The participants
never met each other, but they competed against the other subjects via
anonymous briefing-boards. Josh’s employer encouraged his total focus on
Project FILTER. They gave him bonuses, stock options, and he wanted for
nothing. Every training mission he was assigned came with first-class
airline tickets, car service, and high-end hotels. His employer, Vision
Systems, could be very generous when profitability was increased and
project goals were exceeded by its best operatives.

Josh loved watching his old assignment’s video logs. This most recent
one almost gave him an erection. It was the stealth and undercover of
this one. Also, the location was truly exciting and torn from the
headlines of the day. He remembered reading about the representatives
heart-attack earlier in the day, but he never thought one of his aides
was a spy for the Chinese. He stopped her. He stopped the secrets in his
files from getting in the enemy's hands. Or so his debriefing session
informed him. The tires of the black van with government plates thumped
on the seams of the concrete on the Baltimore-Washington Parkway. They
were heading north, toward Fort Meade, Maryland.

###

There were other Users in Project FILTER who understood the veil between

Jax424 was one of these Users. He was a video game fanatic. Played and
beat - within a few hours or days - every game that his parents bought
him for birthdays and Christmas.

His dad once said, “You’re not going to be some couch potato. Every game
you play and beat means time at practice.”

Jax424 was not a “couch potato.” He played lacrosse - all-state and full
athletic and academic scholarship to a small private college in Northern
Maryland. Studied information technology. Graduated and then found work
for one of the numerous technology contractors the United States
Government had in its stable.

Technology was everything in the modern world. It brought instantaneous
communication around the worlds and streams of data flowed from every
living creature. Simply put,Data was everything. Jax424 played with data
at his job; at least that is what his online profiles said his
profession was. He was a network engineer/administrator. He is the man
that is on the other end of the phone when a hyperactive office manager
calls to complain that his email is not syncing or, “the internet is
running slooooow.”

Jax424 also had access to all the dirty secrets of the network and its
users. He knew that Charlie Johnson, an accountant on the fifth floor
liked to take a break around 3:30 every afternoon. Charlie’s break
involved looking up porn on his desktop computer via an incognito

Jax424 knew this vulnerability and would often mirror Charlie’s screen
around 3:33 pm. After seeing the images, Jax424 did not dare switch on

with the tracing information left behind on every machine that a human

visited, the messages sent and to whom, then what that person did and so

Charlie’s biggest vice was naked women on his computer screen in the

about - which he should have according to the by-laws. The CEO had many

That cellphone would then travel via the Metro system - Jax424 watched
as the distinct IP address of the cellphone moved and triangulated
through the underground tunnels of the city on the celluar phone
networks and pinging off the open public wifi networks along the way.

It surfaced in Chinatown and according to the speed was traveling in a
car down H street. It would turn after about ten blocks and follow 15th
street south toward the W Hotel.

Once, Jax424 switched on audio microphone to the company cellphone
assigned to the CEO after seeing this pattern and the CEO’s cellphone
followed a similar pattern from his office to the W Hotel.

He heard soft pillow talk.

A male voice and a female voice were discussing the latest episode of a
show about a singing competition for regular people.

Jax424 recognized the show and really liked the single mother waitress
from Arkansas, but it appeared that these two adulterers were found of a

A female voice from off camera inrupted the grunting noises, Is that the
new phone one with the cool camera features that can switch peoples
faces?

Charlie’s voice answered, Huh? The fucking camera? Really? Right now?

A female voice responded, Yeah! You wanna picture to think of me in your
office later, right?

Laughter, the camera moved revealing a Charlie’s face, then the view
changed to a naked woman on a hotel bed.

Say cheese! Charlie commanded.

Fuck me! The woman responded and juggled her round breasts in her hands.
A photo snapped and saved to the memory card of the phone and also to
the Jax424’s local hard drive.

Laughter erupted between them.

Let me try something! The woman said and snatched the phone from the
man. Okay, smile!

The camera switched to the man’s face and snapped a photo. The image
saved to the internal memory card of the phone and also a copy to
Jax424’s local hard drive. After few moments of staring at the woman's
concentrating face she grinned and flipped the camera back to Charlie’s
face. An image dropped onto Jax424’s local hard drive.

There! You’ve got my tits!

The image was that of the woman’s body with Charlie’s face imposed over

Oh hell, no! Charlie shouted and deleted the photo. The copy of Jax424’s

The camera switched around to show Charlie’s erect penis about to enter

After a few minutes, the camera returned to the ceiling view it began

video rendered on Jax424’s hard drive and the data began uploading to
Vision Systems private cloud.

Jax424 used his powers benignly. He looked away when their faces entered
the screen, at first. He only really needed to know their patterns - and

But Jax424 was eager to move into more advanced projects within the
company and eager to learn more about other branches of the company and

Jax424 was nervous he would not get accepted into these programs because

this concern with a quick hack into the Human Resources application
server and fixed a couple of misspellings. He received his promotion and
acceptance papers two weeks later - which he also saw approved a day

Jax424 was one of the first civilians to receive Project FILTER. Two
days after the bonding process, Jax424 never had to step into the server
room for his job duties. With a swipe of his hands Jax424 had every feed
and connection he needed to the entire information network of his
employer and every target he desired.

Vision Systems immediately saw a three-fold bump in productivity once
Project FILTER was implemented into its Information Technology
workforce.

6 - Paranoia

Time was meaningless when Reggie concentrated on something. His eye
scanned and absorbed the information pouring out of the laptop in front
of him. He did not realize that hours had passed since he last saw
Jessica.

Hunger growled in his belly and shook his attention away from the
glowing screen.

Damn, what time is it? The streetlamp shone through the grimy basement
window giving a soft glow to the room. Where was Jessica? I’m hungry.

Reggie slid the computer off his lap and stood. Stretching and popping
his back and shoulders. He stretched his legs and shook out the achiness
of hours of inactivity.

He ventured upstairs to the kitchen to see if there was anything to eat.

Does she even live here? Reggie wondered as he stared into an empty
fridge. The only items were three kinds of chinese mustard and two
containers of yogurt. The kind with probiotics.

He searched the cupboards, dishes, glasses, cold cereal - but no milk in
the fridge. To the kitchen drawers, nothing but chinese takeout menus
and fortune cookies. Reggie snatched a couple of fortune cookies from
the drawer and unwrapped one as he leaned against the counter.

Do I risk a phone call for a pizza? A brother’s gotta eat! Would they be
listening for my voice?

Reggie’s mind swirled with the day’s events and information he was just
reading. He split the cookie in two and tossed half of it into his
mouth. The fortune strip inside was half-exposed. He pulled it from the
cookie as he crunched the other half of the cookie in his mouth.

“Getting the right answers is only possible when you have asked the
right questions,” the fortune strip read.

Reggie always chuckled at how purposefully vague these “fortunes” always
were.

I can’t risk any communications. I’ll have to find something quick and
pay cash. Reggie remembered there was a carry-out place around the
corner, a memory from a previous visit to Jessica’s townhouse. They had
good noodles.

He would have to make it quick, he did not know where Jessica was or why
she was not back yet.

He threw on his coat and hat and started for the back door. Then he
heard a beeping and his blood ran cold.

Was there something left on? Where they tracking him? Was it an alarm?
Paranoia flushed his veins with fresh adrenaline. Reggie no longer felt
the hunger in his stomach.

He almost stumbled down the stairs to the basement. The laptop chirped
from the couch. He swept it into his hands. A message notification
wiggled on the screen. He clicked it instinctively, but then immediately
thought he should not have.

“They are watching. You are being tracked.”

Reggie’s guts went cold.

The message continued with another entry. “You must move, now. North.
Get to College Park. University of Maryland Campus. Watch for the sign.”

Reggie slapped the laptop shut. For a moment, he paused. Move, you
idiot. Even if this is a trap, you’ll get caught here for sure. It might
be Jessica.

He snatched the black bag Jessica kept this laptop in and slung it over
his shoulder. He grabbed his jacket, his car keys jingled in the pocket.
He could not waste a minute, but he could not move too fast - no
suspicious activity.

The streetlamps glowed in the dark of night as Reggie criss-crossed
through the streets toward the Baltimore-Washington Parkway.

 

7 - The Operators

It was a small room with a design was specially implemented just for
these types of secure hearings. No press ever saw the interior of this
room; no room in front of the committee bench for the photographers to
crawl around. The witness bench was a single row along the back of the
room immeadiately behind the table where Robinson perched himself.

Cell phones were checked with a Marine guard at the outside door. Then
the vistors - only if they held the right color badge and their name was
on the list in the Marine’s heads-up-display were admitted to a small
brightly lit entry room. This room shot a refreshing burst of air on the
entrant. It wasn’t to clear the glisten of humid DC sweat from their
brows, it was a quick burst of air to test for bombmaking materials. The
brightly colored lights were hidding the wide spectrum array of lights
that scanned the entrant for every possible material that wcould either
be a weapon or communications device - transmitting or recording. THis
was the last possible moment that any electronic transmission on any
radio frequency could possibly go out. Whether it was a text message to
a loved one saying, “wish me luck,” or it was a message to a handler on
the outside. Nothing further penetrated the walls after the door’s
electromagnetic seal disengaged and the foot thick concrete door slid to
one side.

This room within a room was a sarcophagus of concrete woven with special
shielding and copper mesh embedded into the walls to prevent
transmissions or signals from entering or leaving. As a by-product of
the sheilding and the concrete, it was also one of the safetest places
to be in the event of a nuclear attack; even a meteor hit was unlikely
to crack its shell.

Inside was where every interior designers dream was murdered in favor of
the brutally boring, plain, and stereotypical late 1970s government
hearing room. The brown and yellow curtains covered faux-windows frosted
and backlit to give the occupants the illusion of daytime and normallcy.
A few high back leather chairs with name plates of Senators infront of
them.

Lieutenant General Jack Robinson sat alone at table, a small microphone
at the center of the table in the hearing room of the Senate Committee
on Special Operations and Projects. SCOM-SOP. Robinson gave the
committee its own command code ward because it was easier to approach it
as something he controlled rather than an oversight committee compelling
him to stand and deliver.

At Robinson’s feet a briefcase; a prop for effect - visually inspected
by the Marine guard outside and electronically scanned by the sensors in
the entrance room. Both instances only a few papers detected. His
portfolio lay open before him, papers marked Top Secret and Classified
in red ink. But useless in reality, he carried all his notes and
briefing papers in his heads-up-display.

Robinson grinned to himself as he remembered getting his first
classified document earlier in his intelligence career. That was a long
time ago, long before the four stars crawled upon his shoulders. His
first classified document was a national security intercept from a
wiretap on a diplomatic cable sent from the Iranian Ministry of Foreign
Intelligence to a small consulate in Lebanon in late-October 1983.

He remembered the words in farsi with the onion paper overlay with an
english translation. The message was short and terse: Send the driver
with the package, praise be to Allah. The driver was a suicide terrorist
meant to drive a large truck laden with plastic explosives and oxygen
tanks. The target was the United States Marines Barracks in Beirut
Lebanon. The intercept itself bore a recieved date of 18-10-83; days
before the bombing occured. On its face the commands for the bombers on
the ground to ready themselves for paradise. The intercept appeared on
Robinson’s desk on Tuesday, October 25, 1983. The bombing occurred in
the early-morning the Sunday before.

Robinson took no pause but for a short gasp to acknowledge the 241
Marines and Servicemen killed in the blast. Bodies still being pulled
from the rubble as Robinson tucked the intercept into a report on the
event that found little relevance until an investigation found faults in
the military chain of command, but levied no accountability. No
accoutability for the myriad of reasons the intercept failed to hit the
desk that could have done anything to prevent it until 48 hours after
the bombing occured.

Back to the present with Robinson and his four-stars standing to
attention as Senators entered from the same entrance via the same Marine
guard. Smiling and laughing about a joke spoken on the other side of the
Marine Guard.

“Jack! Good to see you again,” Senator Richard Burchart was a two-time
incumbent from Missouri. He served in the Army and went to Harvard Law
School. Never set foot in a courtroom during his career as a lawyer.
Then he was tapped by a managing partner at a large firm in Missouri to
run for State Senate. He lost but then the U.S. Senator died in office
and the Governor - a former partner at Burchart’s firm - Burchart had
four years to prove that he could get elected. He did and the money that
went to the now late-senator went to Candidate Burchart. He has never
had a tightly contested election since.

“Good Morning, Senator,” Robinson answered. “How is Nancy?”

“She is well,” Burchart answered. “She’s heading up a
food-nutrition-access thing in Mizzou. Good for the local farmers and
schools from I’ve gathered.”

The other two Senators placed their portfolios next to their red-strap
sealed briefing packets at their positions and offered their hellos.
Robinson returned them.

“Okay, let’s get going,” Burchart said. “Where do we sit on our
projects?”

Robinson began to explain the status of various projects and referenced

and began to scan the pages as Robinson spoke.

Robinson spoke in plain and curt statements with short descriptions and
only the high probilities outlined. This was his nature he had honed to
sharp razor like blade he unshealted for these pro-forma “hearings.” The
hearings dictated by the laws that originated the matrix of programs and
commands Robinson oversaw were demanded by the laws enacted by Congress
as defacto oversight and engraved in the boilerplate of the complicated
words on the paper blessed by Congress and given life with signature of
the President of the United States. POssibly the only act that Robinson
was obligated to actually perform - these hearings were just the kabuki
dance required for the ticket to ride and Robinson - and his four-star

cadence.

“So these projects are going smoothly?” Burchart interrupted Robinson.
An interruption that Robinson expected.

“Yes, the warrants have been provided by the Foreign Intelligence and

“General Robinson,” Senator Chuck Ammerstand interrupted the General.
“The probable cause evidence proffered, has it been vetted and examined

Senator Ammerstand was a first-term Senator from New Hampshire. He was
elected on a platform of government oversight and reform. “Stand with
Ammerstand” his campaign slogan; and likely going to be the one he used
in his relection campaign. His electorate did in fact, stand with him,
and punched his ticket to Washington with sixty-percent of the vote in
the general election. In the elections these days three points was
considered a commanding victory. Six points is a mandate and ten gives
you room to stomp on some toes. And stomp Senator Ammerstand has in his
short time in Washington.

“Yes, Senator Ammerstand,” Robinson was used to this particular vein of

“Yes, yes,” Ammerstand responded. “But have these requests been reviewed
