structure?”

“We have held true to every requirement within the letter of the
enacting legislation.” Robinson had given this answer to Ammerstand a
number of times before, in previous closed door briefings and hearings.

“I understand that,” Ammerstand shifted in his leather chair and leaned
forward. “But that doesn’t…”

“Chuck,” Burchart interrupted. “We’ve read the reports. You have them
right there. Do we need to explore this?”

“Dick,” Ammerstand raised his hand with the palm toward Burchart. “We
have a responsibility to our constituents to diligently…”

“Yes, yes, I know. I’ve heard your speech about this before. I’m asking
if you have reason to get into the munitae. Right now. I’ve got a
luncheon to get to shortly and I’m sure the General has a busy schedule
to get to.”

Ammerstand sighed. “Ted? Ted!”

Theodore Chaulk was the third Senator in the room. His briefing papers
were left with the red strap still across the pcket; untouched and
pushed to the side of another set of papers he studied more earnestly -
polling information from Georgia. He was behind in very tight primary
race that would be coming to a head in two months. He was being outspent
by radicals in his own party six dollars to every one of his.

“Huh? Yes, I seen nothing troubling here,” Senator Chaulk answered.

Ammerstand sighed and slumped into the back of the leather chair. He
slipped off his glasses and rubbed bridge of his nose.

“Senator Ammerstand?” Burchart probed.

“I’ve got nothing further. I’m not going to get any answers today
anyway. Not here.”

“General? Nothing further to report, if not, I believe that is all we
require of you today. This hearing is adjourned. Thank you, Gentlemen.”

Robinson gathered his papers. He had not even scratched the surface of
the information he brought with him today. He never did. He slipped his
portfolio into his briefcase and started toward the door.

Senator Ammerstand called out to him, “General Robinson? A moment? Off
the record?”

Burchart paused at exit door and huffed. Senator Chaulk had already
barreled out of the room, his briefing papers remained - untouched and
bheind his nameplate in front of his chair. It was normal custom fo the
Senator’s to deposit their briefing papers into the shredder standing
sentry at the exit.

“Yes, Senator?” Robinson paused mid-way to the door.

“I thank you for the time you take out of your schedule to come here and
speak with us on these subjects,” Ammerstand tried to smooth any ruffled
feathers left in the room.

“I would not miss it. It is my duty, Senator,” Robinson answered.
“Anything further?”

Ammerstand paused and contemplated the fight he wanted to pick.

“Chuck? The General has a busy schedule and I’m sure you do too,”
Burchart’s voice was stern, as if he was chiding a young boy.

“Thank you, General. Nothing further.” Ammerstand felt his stomach drop
and an audible sigh escape his lips.

Robinson nodded to Burchart as the exit door slid open; Robinson passed
by with a quick slaute to Burchart.

Ammerstand whipped his overstuffed portfolio under his arm and turned
toward the exit door sweeping up Senator Chaulk’s packet to deposit in
the shredder. Burchart stood at the exit door waiting for him.

“Listen, Chuck,” Burchart said. “We have done our part. Our end of the
process.”

“Dick, I don’t want to hear this speech again,” Ammerstand curtly
responded as he jammed Senator Chaulk’s packet into the shredder.

“Well, you’re going to hear it again. And again. And twice tomorrow!”
Burchart thundered. “I’m tired of you trying to do whatever it is you
are doing or trying to do. I’m the chairman of this committee. I’m the
guarddog. It’s my signature that matters for anything that you think you
will try to accomplish with this kind of posturing. This is your first
time in the dance. I’ll give you that. But I’m not going to accept this
from one of MY committee members.”

The emphasis was on the word “my.” An emphasis that was not lost on
Ammerstand.

“Yes, Senator,” Ammerstand backed down. “Not trying to do anything, just
asking questions where I thought I should.”

“Son,” Burchart said smugly. “Get hot when there cameras on. When it
counts for your friends back home. No cameras here. And the air in here
is the best air-conditioned air you can get in DC.”

Ammerstand’s blood curdled in his veins. He hated this side of being a
Senator - he was never a good actor.

“Besides,” Burchart slid his arm around the shoulder of Ammerstand. “We
have an appropriations hearing next week and there are some bases and
projects on the list that are of interest to your friends back home.”

Burchart was right. There were no cameras to capture the rage. Nothing
to circulate to the voters back home and, more importantly, the donors
to Ammerstand’s next election campaign. Ammerstand came from a
predominately purple state and he would have to posture and dance the
dance for the money to fund his next election. Ammerstand knew he had
win another election if he were to gain any gravitas at these hearings.

###

The laptop chirped from inside the case on the passenger seat next to
Reggie as he drove north on the Baltimore-Washington Parkway. With only
one eye he did not dare try and pull it from the case with one hand. He
pulled off, and once stopped, pulled the laptop and opened it.

“Go to Greenbelt Metro. Park. Take train into DC.”

Reggie shoved the gearshift into drive and headed as directed. He pulled
off the parkway and found his way to the metro station. Once he parked
he walked to the station and stepped onto a train. The last one of the
evening. It was 2:30 in the morning. This was one of the last trains.
Wherever Reggie was going, he was not coming back to this station.

The door chimes sounded and Reggie entered a train car. The laptop
chirped.

“Chinatown. Exit to H Street. Wear the bag on your left shoulder.”

Reggie sat with his wounded eye to the glass. He drummed his fingers on
the laptop and slipped it back into the case. No need to look like and
easy mugging target this late in the evening, he thought.

It’s all uphill from here. He was too nervous to zone out like he and
every other local did while riding the Metro train; it was official,
Reggie was the “crazy-looking-dude” on the train.

###

Reggie stood in the glowing light of D.C.’s sad version of Times Square.
The bag on his left shoulder. He took a deep breath and waited. The
message did not say how long to wait and he did not dare open the laptop
on the street.

A car pulled up next to Reggie. A window rolled down. Reggie expected a
gun to emerge and his part in the play to come to an end.

“Get in.” A voice from the car said.

“What no codeword?” Reggie chuckled nervously.

“Fuck you, that’s my codeword. You want help or not?”

Reggie pulled the handle on the door and entered the vehicle.

The driver shifted the car into gear and drove. The direction did not
matter. The car drove. This was not a commute , it was an interview.

“How did you get that computer?” It was almost not a question - more
like an accusation.

“Jessica,” Reggie said. “She gave it to me then she sent something to
it. I haven’t seen or heard from her since. I’m worried about her.”

“Yeah, me too. She doesn’t drop off.”

The car continued northbound on 7th street. After 7th street turned into
Georgia Avenue, there were fewer streetlamps and more graffiti.

The silence was too much for Reggie, he had to speak.

“Where are we going?”

“To fix this.” The driver said. “She sent us something. We have to look
at it. Also, your bad eye is going to be seen. That’s gotta be fixed.”

“How?” Reggie looked at the driver. “I dug it out of my skull! You got a
magic eyeball machine somewhere? I’ve a quarter for the claw machine, if
you do.”

“You’d be surprised what I got up my sleeve. And I have the best arcade
machine collection. Besides, I’ve seen wounds like that one before,” the
driver said.

“Seen it before?” Reggie was astounded. “How? When?”

“Let’s just say, this ain’t no Uber ride and you’re not the first,” the
driver said smugly. “I’m Trevor. No need for last names.”

“Not the first?” Reggie questioned.

“Been there done that,” Trevor glanced at Reggie. His right eye gleamed
a metallic shine, glowing from the reflections of the night.

Reggie dared not press for details. He presumed Trevor’s story was
similar to his.

Trevor drove the car onto New Hampshire Avenue and into an alleyway
behind a series of rowhouses and pulled into a small carport.

“Let’s go. We don’t have much time.” Trevor yanked the keys from the
ignition and exited the vehicle.

Reggie followed clutching the bag in his arms. It was his meal-ticket.
Without it, he’s a crazy man left on the streets of D.C. to live a
miserable life or die.

Trevor entered through the basement doorway. A scanner clicked and the
door popped open. He disappeared into the blackness inside.

Reggie paused and thought, this could be it. The last doorway I walk
into. A dark room. Will it be a needle to his neck or knife through his
ribcage to puncture his lung?

“You coming?” Trevor called from the darkness.

“Yeah,” Reggie swallowed hard and stepped into the darkness ahead of
him. Curtains brushed against his face, he gasped then saw light split
the darkness. As he pressed forward, the curtains split and he was in a
room of computers and neon light. A surplus army cot sat along a bare
brick wall. Along the remaining walls was a series of large monitors and
Reggie heard the hum of computers.

“Shit!” Reggie exclaimed. “They’ll see us! They track everything!”

“Calm down,” Trevor said firmly. “This is off-grid, it’s a SCIF. Shut up
and take a seat. We’re in a Sensitive Compartmented Information
Facility. The CIA uses rooms like these to keep signals from getting in
or out. YOu’ve been in one before, right?” Trevor asked.

“Yeah, secure briefings,” Reggie confessed. “They always make us check
our cell phones at the door, usually with a Marine sitting at a desk
with a basket.”

“This one is custom,” Trevor explained. “I built it myself. The curtains
are copper shielded. I’ve got one wire going out. Only for shortwave
transmissions. Otherwise we are completely cut-off from the digital
world.”

Shortwave radio signals were impossible to manipulate with modern
technology. Spies used particular radio transmissions during the cold
war to send and receive signals and communicate around the world. New
technology did not allow for manipulation of the radio waves - physics
did not bend to the digital world.

“We’re safe here,” Trevor assured Reggie. “For now. Not for long.”

“How did you know how to …” Reggie began to ask.

“Find you? Get in touch?” Trevor pulled his jacket from his shoulders
and tossed it on the cot. “Jessica and I communicate via code. Spikes
and blips sent via radio waves and secure messaging channels. I got a
spike on a channel I monitor. With the recent news, I knew I’d get
something from her. Take a seat. We need to download and make backups.”

Trevor motioned toward a chair near a large glass monitor. Reggie obeyed
and felt his shoulders relax as his grip loosened around the bag.

“Let’s see what is so important.” Trevor held out his hand for the bag.
Reggie hesitated before he extended his arms and handed the bag to
Trevor. With the bag in hand, Trevor turned toward another desktop with
a set of glass monitors. A 9mm pistol hung from his belt in a holster at
the small of his back. Reggie had never fired a gun. This thought
triggered in his mind as he feared the sight of it.

Trevor flipped the lid of the laptop open. “Favorite ice cream, right?”

“Huh? Oh, um...yeah. With numbers and symbols instead of…”

“Vowels, yeah, that’s an old standard.”

An old standard? Who the fuck is this guy?, Reggie thought to himself.
The pain reliever was beginning to wear off and his eye socket began to
ache.

Trevor’s fingers tapped the keys and the screen glowed. “okay, we have
to get this whole thing ghosted, then we can examine what was pulled.”

“What about Jessica?” Reggie asked.

“I don’t know where she is.” Trevor confessed. “I expected you would be
her waiting for me at the Metro.”

Reggie’s mind raced with possibilities. She would have taken fifteen
minutes to jog back from the office. Reggie waited a full thirty
minutes. Someone must have got her. Oh God, what if she’s… Reggie could
not finish the thought. He gasped and cupped his hand over his mouth.

“If you’re going to be sick, don’t get any on my machines,” Trevor
pointed toward a wastebasket next to Reggie’s foot. “Use that, please.”

“We have to find her!” Reggie shouted with his hand dropping from his
mouth.

“I know, but we have to protect and analyze what she went after. So if
you’re gonna toss, toss in the basket. We’ll get to her, if we can.”

“Listen, here,” Reggie’s sudden defiance surprised himself. “I’m not
gonna toss and not gonna cool my heels or take a chill pill, whatever is
the phrase of the day. I’ve got to get her back. She could be…”

“Dead?” Trevor turned on his heels and faced Reggie. “Yeah, I know. She
knows. She knew what this assignment might entail. We are not going
anywhere until this information is duplicated and preserved. Then we
have to take care of your eye. Then we can search for her. So shut it.”

Trevor turned back to the laptop and finished his entry of cryptic
computer commands and pressed the enter key. The laptop’s hard disks
whirred with activity and progress bar filled the screen.

Trevor stepped away from the laptop to another desktop as the glass
monitor surged with activity and another progress bar began to fill up.

“Alrighty then, we have a few minutes,” Trevor sucked his teeth, turned
to Reggie. “Let’s take a look at that eye socket.”

###

It was not a painful experience, at least the cleaning of the wound. But
the at-home-surgery after the cleaning made Reggie pass-out. His eye
fluttered open. He laid on the cot. A green army blanket covering him. A
bandage over his wounded eye socket. He could not help but groan as the
dull ache registered in his brain.

“You’re up,” Trevor said cheerfully. “Congratulations, it’s a boy.”

“Very funny,” Reggie said as he felt the cool gauze covering his wound.
“What did you do exactly?”

“Prosthesis implantation,” Trevor said as he turned to his computer
monitors and examined streams of data being processed.

“You’re a fucking doctor, too?” Reggie huffed.

“I have two doctorates actually. But neither of them are in medicine.”
Trevor continued to examine his computer monitors. The data and
information on them processed and rendered at a dizzying rate.

“Did you at least use clean instruments?” Reggie’s concern was
warranted.

“Spit and elbow-grease.”

“Fucking hell, man!” Reggie began to sweat.

“Calm down.” Trevor turned away from his monitors. “What you have there
is grade A piece of the finest hardware not available to even the
highest bidders on a number of very ‘special’ buyers on a very
specialized marketplace.”

“The black market,” Reggie said flatly.

“‘Black’ is so old-fashioned. There are so many more shades of grey that
have wares for sale,” Trevor answered. “You’ve got what I’ve got.”

“Sounds like a disease,” Reggie said.

Trevor pointed his index finger at Reggie, then toward his right eye,
then back at Reggie.

“Some say diseases have a way of making people improve themselves. In
fact, the immune system is a remarkable way of improving the human
condition through sickness. So getting sick is actually evolution,”
Trevor said flatly. “I hope this encourages you.”

“What one of… whatever THAT is? And I thought you weren’t a fucking
Doctor!” Reggie remarked incredulously.

“Yep,” Trevor stood up and walked toward another set of monitors.
“Welcome to the club. Should be ready to power up by now.” He clicked a
few keys on a keyboard and hit enter.

Inside Reggie’s eye socket a hum stirred and he felt gyroscopes and
mechanical tendons and servos trigger. Further in Reggie’s head his mind
began to race and contort with self-doubt.

Jessica. Fuck. Where is all of this right now? What Am I doing? Why
didn’t I just file the fucking papers. Damnit, Jessica, why did you make
me fall for you? Here I am in the conspiracy-SCIF with He-Man here who
can evidently hack the fucking world AND implant shit into people's
heads. I’m gonna get an infection.

Jessica. I miss the last time we made love. I was just perfect. A moment
of weakness at the right time and there was a quiet nook on the stumble
home. Drunk. But aware enough to know it was love and not lust. She
kissed me first. Then a hand explored the front parts of my trousers,
from the inside. Giggles. God, I love the sound of her laughter. That
was the most intoxicating thing about you. I loved making you laugh.
Especially during sex. A beautiful joy between two people holding,
wrestling, and exploring each other. Soft breasts heaving with the
syncopated love making.

You taste like wine. Intoxicating.

Trevor stared blanking at Reggie. Frozen in shock.

“You. Yo...You can hear all of this, can’t you.” Reggie said sheepishly.

“Yeah,” Trevor responded. ”Even got the visualizations in your minds
eye, too. In living color.”

Blank stares between the two men. Shock then a cold panic rushed over
Reggie.

“Well, fuck you! Stay out of my room!” Reggie scoffed.

“Shut the door then!” Trevor answered. “I don’t need to hear your spank
bank recollection. Lock your shit down, soldier.”

“Fuck!” Reggie recoiled on the cot toward the wall. “Fuck! What the
fuck! If you can hear it then THEY CAN HEAR IT!! What did you do! Turn
it off! It will transmit! Is it transmitting!? They’ll blow my fucking
head off, dude!”

“Calm down,” Trevor darted across the room and threw his hands over
Reggie’s flailing arms. He was extremely strong and skipping the gym in
favor of the bottle had not done Reggie any favors in the physical
realm. “The Artificial Intelligence protocols in the implant is still
mapping your brain. Calm down. I’m going to let go, but...you have...to
calm...down.”

Reggie took a breath and shook his head in agreement then he stopped
cold and locked eye to eyes with Trevor.

“Mapping my brain?” Reggie stopped and stared at Trevor. “What the hell
is thing in my skull And WHY is it MAPPING MY FUCKING MIND?!”

“I’ve got the same model installed in my skull,” Trevor held his hands
up palms facing Reggie. “If it was going to blow up, I wouldn’t be
standing here right now. It’s mapping the synapses to figure out how
your mind thinks. What does what and what can it do better. It’s all
part of the A.I. in the software that helps you do so stuff better.”

“‘Do stuff’?” Reggie felt his fingertips press against his thighs.

“Calculations. Strategy. Access wireless networks in close vicinity.
Hack a server farm form the parking lot. Stream porn. Scan the White
House for unsecured comms. You’d be surprised what kinda sexy pics those
staffers trade in thier implants.”

“You mean that thing I lost my eye to that was counting down in my
head?” Reggie asled.

“Yeah, the implant you have now is ten generations ahead of the comms
you guys had. That thing in your head is like a Cray machine, a
supercomputer, at your command. You know what that...”

“I know what a damn Cray machine is.” Reggie attempted to stand. “I’m
not some punk kid, you ass.”

“I wouldn’t…” Trevor said as Reggie stood up then tumbled back into the
cot. “The implant hasn’t had time to to your motor skills and balance.
You’ll need a little longer to boot up.”

“Fuck!” Reggie exclaimed as he tried to shake his legs into working.

“Just give it a few moments. The A.I. is learning you just as much as
you are learning it.” Trevor cautioned. “I can see you’ve got about
fifteen percent left to process.”

“You can see?” Reggie gasped.

“Yeah, I can see it all,” Trevor paused and locked glances with Reggie.
“Don’t worry, you’re too pale for a black guy right now, bro. I’m
running diagnostics and systems checks. You’re almost done.”

“Get. The fuck. Out of...my skull!” Reggie stared daggers at Trevor.

“Just checking on my patient,” Trevor tried to assure him. “I’ve stopped
cataloging your memories. You really stole money from your mom’s purse
to buy comics from a kid on the school bus? Really? That was the first
time you stole? Do you even have those comics any more? Hulk? X-Men?
Spidey?”

GET. OUT. The thought shot form Reggie’s brain to his implant and to
Trevor;s implant and to his brain in nanoseconds.

“Alright,” Trevor spun away form Trevor and back to hius computers. “I’m
done. You’re mapped and should be up and running.”

Reggie felt his legs respond to his thoughts. And he slowly stood up as
he regained full control over his senses.

“Okay, I’m good,” Reggie assured Trevor.

“Take the bandage off,” Trevor commanded. “It will be a little
disorientating for a moment, but then, well, you’ll see.”

Reggie slowly pulled the bandage from his face. The stickiness of the
tape pulled at his moist skin. Light flooded his vision, then everything
came into focus.

“How’s it feel?” Trevor asked.

Reggie saw double, but not like you see after crossing your eyes.
Reggie’s double vision was crystallizing and honing into a
high-definition bitrate in one eye while remaining normal in the other.
The weight in his eye socket was also jarring and awkward.

“It’s like I’ve got a hand-carved billiard ball in my skull.”

“You’ll get used to that,” Trevor assured Reggie. “But wait until you
see the tricks it can do.”

###

Jessica’s eye fluttered open. Rhythmic beeps with bright fluorescent
light surrounded her. Her head was groggy within the drug haze.

“Good to see you’re awake, Ms. Powell,” A voice sounded over an
intercom.

Jessica did not respond. Her eyes focused to the light and she realized
she was strapped to table. But this table was not an ordinary table. It
had straps over the ankles, thighs, waist, and chest. Each of her arms
were strapped to separate wings of the table and extended out
horizontally, in a Christ pose. She was strapped to an execution table.

Her secure-link triggered and activated. A voice inside her skull
sounded, “We can begin now.”

###

Reggie was nervous.

“Just breathe and think,” Trevor encouraged. “It’s connected and making
connections at all times.”

“To what?” Reggie asked.

“Your brain.”

“What the hell?!”

“Just calm down, it’s encrypted and shrouded with a military-grade
firewall on a secure virtual private network.” Otherwise we couldn’t
speak like this.

“Why didn’t your lips move?” Reggie asked.

Because we don’t have to use our mouths. Secure messaging, uses the
vocal patterns from my brain to replicate the sound of my voice
transmitted into your implant. Try it.

“How?”

Stop moving your vocal cords and just think what you want to say.

Fuck. Reggie did not think it worked, then laughter burst from Trevor’s
mouth.

“Really? You want that to be your first word? Technically your third.”

I guess so. An expression of wonder and amazement flooded over Reggie’s
face. Wait. third word? What did I say before?

‘Get. Out.’ It was when I was thumbing through your stolen comic book
memories.

Damn! Reggie mentally exclaimed.

Ain’t it cool? Trevor grinned. We’ve got little time to play. But you’ve
got to learn on the fly. Quick. Fast. And in a hurry.

###

The connection a User shares with his FILTER is symbiotic. If the User
is fresh and rested the FILTER will respond and give the User an
immersive and texture-rich vision of the program and protocols being
utilized. If the User is tired and requires food or rest, the FILTER
will begin to exhibit lapses in its operation. Like a cellphone with a
low battery, the FILTER will stutter and the image overlays and
simulation may not be as crisp.

To the User’s eyes and brain, the FILTER coats the world around him with
layers of information. Everything to the mundane to the hallucinogenic -
to an extent. For instance, a particular game of football, when engaged
with other Users, displays to each User’s eyes and senses football
uniforms, helmets, and cleats layered over other Users physically
surrounding them, but it also will allow for remote Users to engage with
the physical Users. The football is a simulated object visible to the
Users participating in the game and tactile to their senses - some Users
have even said they can smell the leather of the football, but this has
never been fully confirmed through analysis.

To a bystander, not connected through a FILTER, the Users may look like
a group of people playing a make-believe game of football, but to the
Users they are experiencing every tactile and sensory effect of actually
playing a game.

A popular game for Users of the FILTER is a zombie sniper - the
simulated gun in his hands feels like a real rifle. It recoils when
fired. Its weight feels real in his hands. The User shoots at what are
simulated Zombies, but like any simulation it can be manipulated.

Representative Baker was just one of these simulations overlayed on to
the real world. OpOne designed this protocol himself. It was useful for
particular missions requiring stealth and divorce from any possible
blowback. What would a police detective say to a person saying they did
not know the gun in their hands was real? That they thought they were
playing a game. Vision Systems could easily spin any potential
investigation with a rendering of what a User really sees versus what is
actually occurring.

OpOne studied the JFK Assassination for the basis of this particular
protocol. How else can you create the perfect Patsy? And it did. The
perfect assassin does not know he is killing, nor why he is killing. Not
being able to conjure up a reason for the conspiracy is a simple way to
divorce any suspicion from the act and possible motives. Killing the
Patsy would not have been necessary and there would be no need for any
Jack Rubys in the equation.

Years ago OpOne pitched this application to Clemens and Robinson. They
were skeptical about the possibility for blowback and implication of
Vision Systems and ultimately the U.S. Government. To quell their fears,
OpOne designed a failsafe function to wipe the FILTER from the User.
They agreed to a test-run, somewhere far away from the U.S. mainland.
OpOne pulled up an old report of one of his first test subjects and
began reliving the experience in his Secure-link.

OLD TOWN SQUARE, PRAGUE, CZECH REPUBLIC - Operator Alpha Zero-two.

Perched in a window of an art museum overlooking the Old Town Square, a
User, as instructed by his FILTER Mission was to pay admission to enter
the museum. Walk through the exhibit and find an open window on the
third floor. He did so.

Once at the window, his FILTER displayed instructions to his heads up
display.

Your targets are the infected. They are found in the square. You must
neutralize them before infection spreads.

The User clicked acknowledgment of the mission and began to feel a
sniper rifle materialize in his hands. He raised the rifle to his
shoulder and peered through the scope. Through his FILTER, the infected
glowed green, the uninfected a pale blue.

The User was able to get off six shots before the massive city square
emptied. Six shots - six kills. The game was a success. The User enjoyed
the elation of the immersive experience. The rifle in his hands
deconstructed and slipped back into the FILTER system that had bonded
with him just that morning. He calmly walked out of the museum into the
hail of police gunfire.

The FILTER system’s failsafe triggered at the last heartbeat of the User
and the nanotechnology devoured itself into base elements and dust -
resembling cocaine or some other powdered drug to the naked eye. The
User would be considered some lone wolf madman blasted out of his mind
on some drug. Fifteen minutes of news and it would be dropped as “just
another isolated incident.”

This mission was a success. And further testing was conducted.
Refinements were made. Clemens and Robinson did not like the use of a
projectile. OpOne struggled with this problem for months until he found
a sonic bullet project from the Defense Advanced Research Projects
Agency. DARPA had been the repository for all the seemingly impossible
projects that the military industrial complex wanted and funded to a
nauseating amount.

The sonic bullet, in the form OpOne discovered, was a crude theory of
using a pulse of air to pierce the soft tissue of the target. Per the
records, it was only able to create a bruise, perhaps break a small
bone. It was defunded and the research was archived.

From those ashes OpOne took the basic concepts and with a few
simulations and application of the quantum computer, he had at his
disposal a Vision System able to mix sound and a laser for a body
penetrating “bullet” that was far more effective. The flux of sound and
direction of a laser in any number of applications is constantly used in
various health sciences OpOne just figured out how to use it to kill a
person. It was the perfect weapon - silent and undetectable and wielded
by a person unsuspecting of its potency; a baby with a loaded gun.

Intermission:

‘‘SEC. 702. PROCEDURES FOR TARGETING CERTAIN PERSONS OUTSIDE

THE UNITED STATES OTHER THAN UNITED STATES PERSONS.

‘‘(a)...

(i)(3) ...the Director of National Intelligence may authorize...,the
targeting of persons reasonably believed to be located outside the
United States to acquire foreign intelligence information.

‘‘(b) LIMITATIONS…(1) may not intentionally target any person known at
the time of acquisition to be located in the United States;

‘‘(2) may not intentionally target a person reasonably

believed to be located outside the United States if the purpose of such
acquisition is to target a particular, known person reasonably believed
to be in the United States;

‘‘(3) may not intentionally target a United States person

reasonably believed to be located outside the United States;

‘‘(4) may not intentionally acquire any communication as

to which the sender and all intended recipients are known

at the time of the acquisition to be located in the United

States; and

‘‘(5) shall be conducted in a manner consistent with the

fourth amendment to the Constitution of the United States.

‘‘(c) CONDUCT OF ACQUISITION...

‘‘(2) DETERMINATION.—A determination under this paragraph

and for purposes of subsection (a) is a determination

‘‘(C) the Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Court.

‘‘(g) CERTIFICATION.—

‘‘(1) IN GENERAL.—

‘‘(A) REQUIREMENT...the Attorney General and the Director of National

under oath and under seal, in accordance with this subsection.

‘‘(2) COMPENSATION.—The Government shall compensate,

at the prevailing rate, an electronic communication service

provider for providing information, facilities, or assistance in

‘‘(3) RELEASE FROM LIABILITY.—No cause of action shall

lie in any court against any electronic communication service provider
for providing any information, facilities, or assistance in accordance
with a directive issued pursuant to paragraph (1).”

8 - The Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Court

Judge Farland found his way into law after telling his father he'd
rather fight in Vietnam than run the family business of radial tires.
The elder Mr. Farland was not pleased and ever the haggling salesman
bartered with his son to pay for law school instead of jump school.

Judge Farland took law school after a heavy dose of nightly news and
pictures from the hell that awaited a potential ROTC cadet with an Eagle
Scout badge hanging next to a baseball trophy over his childhood bed.
That pedigree was reprinted word-for-word in obituaries of hundreds of
Sunday newspapers across the fly-over states. Mr. Farland wanted nothing
more than to keep his son's from the printer's press. Future-Judge
Farland would never live down the gnawing regret of not signing his mark
on the volunteer roster.

In time and with the sweat of the jungle far removed from the equation
of his life Farland discovered he did not like the law, but it grew to
love him. Rutgers Law School was a trim fit for the Irish-Catholic from
Queens. It was just far enough away from his family's brownstone to feel
like he was on his own but close enough for long weekends.

Farland spent his time at Rutgers swallowing the law. He joked he hoped
to find somehow the time to digest it over the life of his career. After
publishing a note in the law review focused on the legal justification
of the use of chemical weapons to defoliate the jungle, he found an
internship offer in the form of a letter from Hughes Chemical in
Delaware. This took up his summers and dragged him further from the
borough of Queens. A secret goal of his he carried deep in his heart for
many years.

Farland missed his mother but found the freedom from his father's store
a worthy trade.

The contracts he learned to read were boring. Numbers mixed with
promises over the amount of pure hell being poured all over the jungle
of southeast Asia. He often felt pangs of guilt for not refusing the
Faustian bargain from his father, but then reassured himself with a
pseudo-sense of patriotic purpose in the subject matter of his
monotonous work in his windowless office in Wilmington, Delaware. This
duty station was far from the fire of a war zone; at least he was close
to the beach for the summer, another excuse to avoid a train ride to
Queens.

After law school, the justice department found his name on a list of
"good ones" forwarded by a senior president in the upper echelons of the
chemical company's resource placement division. Soon Farland found the
humidity of Washington, DC comforting and even further removed from the
concrete jungle of New York.

Assistant U.S. Attorney Farland found the new subject matter
invigorating. No more contracts for chemicals and shipping services.
Farland now found people the heart of the case, namely those cowards
that refused to the honor of fighting for their country in a war against
the dark forces of communism. Farland used those exact words in an oral
argument of one unfortunate "draft dodger."

After success in this field Assistant U.S. Attorney Farland was promoted
to more important cases; those involving persons charged with subversion
and active opposition to the sovereignty of the president of the United
States. Hippies. Protestors. Activists. Undesirables.

Within the ranks of the other assistant US Attorneys at the justice
department, Assistant U.S. Attorney Farland found a new nickname:
Hang'Em High Hughie. He did not enjoy the name at first but once a
federal judge winked to Farland after a particularly vigorous
cross-examination of a draft-dodging hippie that ended up finding a new
home at Fort Leavenworth, Kansas.

"That Hippie deserved every minute of that sentence." Judge Farland
reflected sternly during an interview for a Legal Magazine in 2008.
Judge Farland was also fond of saying he'd never retire because his work
will never be done. "I'll be found on a Monday morning. My clerk is
aware this will be the case. He has instructions on whom to call first
and the orders that should be signed with the pen still in my hand."

Judge Farland received his robe by order of President Ronald Reagan in
the twilight of his second term. Farland was so anxious and nervous that
he didn't notice the dribble of drool formed on the President's lower
lip or the misbuttoned shirt. Not to mention the forgetting of Farland's
name during the five minute face-to-face meeting. Judge Farland often
casually said they should have never changed the law after Roosevelt,
“the country needed Reagan's strong hand in fighting those Ruskies."

Ever with the soldier's mentality, Judge Farland never sought a higher
position that District Judge in the District Court for the District of
Columbia Even though the particular court had been a stepping stone
toward the Supreme Court across the First street from Congress. He was
happy with the intrigue and demands of the trial court, and his "hang'em
high" reputation followed and strengthened with the power of the bench.
After twenty years on the bench, the Judge found a new purpose with the
need for certain judges with discretion and firmness to handle certain
national security issues that were beyond the comprehension of his
brothers and sisters in black and inconceivable to the general public.
Judge Farland found his appointment to the Foreign Intelligence and
Security Panel a welcome promotion, that e felt, "returned him to his
original intent when the law drafted him into service." He had been
drafting and redrafting his memoirs since 2006, to be self-published
immediately after his death by operation of his last will and testimony.

The times changed around Judge Farland, he did not. He wore a fedora and
overcoat in the winter and straw hat with his seersucker suit in the
summer. His black Cadillac remained in pristine condition as the day he
paid cash for it in 1984.

Everything had a place in his life, and if there was ever anything out
of place, it was because someone had invaded his world. He was unmarried
and childless - children without a wife would have been a sin to his
roots. His wife was the law and his children where his judgments, upheld
on appeal and ratified by his fellow robe-bearers.

He never struggled with the concepts in his courtroom. If he did not
understand the particulars of how the "internet" functioned then he
would pay deference back toward the young Assistant US Attorney. In his
mind, surely that young man understands these particulars and has the
best in mind for the country! Judge Farland grew comfortable in letting
this argument play out behind his eyes; sadly, that argument never
entered the record drafted by the court reporter.

The Young Assistant US Attorney continued pleading his case for access
to the "phone wires at the junction box at the end of every street that
everyone's phone connected to,."

It was apparent to Judge Farland there was no reason to hogtie the
excellent work of protecting America with worrying over the particulars
of a few private whispers. "If a few fish get caught in Ahab's net when
Moby Dick is the target then we let the fish go." Judge Farland enjoyed
when his exact quotes were used in the appellate court's sealed opinion
affirming his eloquent opinions. He smiled broadly when he reread the
opinions long after the paper had yellowed from years of sunlight and
humidity in his private office at his home in Georgetown.

###

“All rise!” The Clerk sounded the announcement as a Judge William
Farland entered the SCIF-courtroom from a rear entrance.

“As you were,” the Judge announced before he reached his high-back black
leather chair at the bench. “What’s on the agenda today? Mister… Belmer,
is it?”

Assistant United States Attorney Henry Belmer swallowed, cleared his
throat, and approached the podium. “Your Honor. If it please the Court…”

“No need for pleasantries, Mr. Belmer,” Judge Farland interrupted. “We
don’t have opposing counsel here or anything. It’s not an adversarial
hearing.” He motioned to a vacant third table.

“Yes, your Honor,” Belmer said. “We have a series of warrants for your
consideration. Operations in the field are in need of SIGINT. Excuse me,
Your Honor, Signals Intelligence from various locations and seek your
finding of probable cause in order to obtain them from the
telecommunications partners, I mean, sources.”

“Yes, yes.” Farland scanned a sheet of names and bullet pointed lists on
an spreadsheet before him.

”These have all been vetted by your office to a...oh what is the
standard...that we came up with.” Judge Farland snapped his fingers. “Oh
yes, a reasonable degree of certainty?”

“Yes, your Honor.”

A ‘reasonable degree of certainty,’ the term meant so many things in so
many manipulated ways. From scientific evidence to probability, even to
a layman on how sure they were about something that could happen. It was
a fuzzy, ill-focused, and subjective term - a legal fiction ginned up by
lawyers and judges over a hundred years of legal jurisprudence.

Judge Farland’s brow furrowed and he rubbed his clean-shaven and
wrinkled chin.

“These are being predominantly routed through our international
partners, I see.”

“Yes, your Honor.”

“Then how does the constitution apply?” Judge Farland questioned.
“Doesn’t the constitution stop at our borders?”

“Yes and no, your Honor, some of the targets have connections to U.S.
Citizens and the rights travel with the person. We need your approval in
those circumstances.”

“Yes, yes, I know. Transient constitutional protections,” Judge Farland
waved his wrinkled hands in the air. “Perhaps these persons should watch
who they communicate and associate with. Warrants approved, orders to
issue. You have a proposed order for me, Mr. Blemer?” Judge Farland did
not notice his mispronunciation of Belmer’s surname.

Little did Judge Farland understand or had he ever even questioned was
the normal routing of the Internet always sought the path of least
resistance and as such a packet of data can travel in and out - across
and back again - countries and man-made borders, and this enormous
loophole, the mountain of wiretaps were freely approved.

“In your hands, the last pages attached with a binder clip.”

The proposed orders were held together with a large black steel binder
clip, requiring a strong hand to unclip them. Judge Farland’s arthritic
hand struggled to gather the strength to remove it. He plopped the paper
back on his bench.

“I don’t need to read them, the Justice Department is thorough enough
for my needs. Approved en masse. Court is adjourned.” Judge Farland
slammed his gavel on the bench and stood up to exit.

“All rise,” the clerk announced. There were only three people in the
room, but Judge Farland demanded the ceremony.

Mr. Belmer gathered his papers into his black leather portfolio. The
Clerk retrieved the papers from the judge’s bench.

“These will be sent to your attention this afternoon, Mr. Belmer,” the
clerk said.

Mr. Belmer nodded and exited through the opposite end of the room.

As he exited, he retrieved his cellphone from the basket and nodded to
the Marine who snapped to attention. Belmer tapped the screen and called
up a message application, Warrants approved, ops are go. He tapped the
send button and wondered what he was going to do for lunch, perhaps
soup. Cream of something...oh, mushroom, maybe?

9 - The Experience

“You’ve got to relax and just let the implant operate,” Trevor sighed.

“Eighteen hours ago I tore one of these out of my skull because it was
about to explode and kill me. Excuse me if I’m having trouble relaxing
with its cousin twitching in my eye socket,” Reggie’s frustration was
more than evident to Trevor.

Trevor grinned and recalled the video he skimmed from the files ripped
from the broadcast his sister pushed out before she was abducted.

“You masturbate,” Trevor chuckled. “Like, a lot.”

“Fuck you!” Reggie recoiled and slapped an empty paper cup away from
him. “What kind of fucking statement is that?!”

“Dude, just saying. Like all weekend. They watch everything. Every.
Fucking. Thing.”

“You mean…” Reggie blushed.

“Yeah, you stopped fucking my sister and started jerking it hard. Like
it owed you money.”

Laughter erupted between the two of them. First Trevor, then nervously
at first but then a full-throated Reggie joined in.

Trevor had a unique ability to disarm with words. He would find some
random idea or fact and insert it into a tense moment, like pressing a
knotted muscle at exactly the right pressure point and poof the tension
was relieved.

Tense moments came with the nature of Trevor’s work. Ever since he
signed the papers to join the military the tense moments and the need to
disarm them fell to him. Then bombs replaced the moments and Trevor
couldn’t afford mistakes.

The sun was rising. Not that either of them were able to see the sun
from inside the SCIF-basement. Trevor’s implant chimed with the alert
for the sun rising. He kept these alerts on because it was easy to
forget the world outside while he was in the SCIF.

“It’s time to go outside," Trevor said as he stood up and walked toward
the black-out curtains.

“What? No, what if it connects to their system. What if it…” Reggie
pleaded.

“Shut it," Trevor shot his eyes at Reggie. “We don’t have time for
what-if’s and paranoia. Remember Jessica? I have a way to see if she is
still up and going, but I can’t do it from in here. So get up and come
outside,"

Trevor exited through the curtains. Reggie heard a door open and shut
behind the curtains.

“Oh, what the hell," Reggie sighed. “I’m probably dead by sunset
anyways. He stood and wiped his sweating palms on his jeans. Then he
punched the air with an adolescent fit of frustration and thrust through
the curtains and out the door.

“I’ve got a signal. It’s weak, but it’s broadcasting," Trevor sucked his
teeth. “She’s still alive.”

“Or the signal is being faked," Reggie said.

“Possible, but unlikely. It’s on a frequency that is used for normal
broadcasts and mundane things - weather alerts and stock prices - but
also how we communicate," Trevor paused as his implant began process
police band radio signals. “Shit, we’re gone. Get in the car.”

Trevor disappeared through the doorway back into the basement.

“What? I thought you said these were secure!” Reggie shrieked.

Trevor emerged from the SCIF-basement with his jacket on and Reggie’s in
hand.

“Keep it down, my neighbors are light sleepers.”

A dog barked in the distance. A city bus rumbled by on the next street
over.

Trevor entered the driver side of the car, the engine started.

You’ve got ten seconds to get in this car. I can’t be caught.

Reggie’s face flushed. The voice in his head carried a serious tone; the
car engine reved. He sucked his teeth as he threw his jacket around them
and the passenger door of Trevor’s car popped open. Reggie got in and
the car slipped into reverse and slowly pulled from the carport.

Reggie felt the droop of his eyelid over his new appendage. The human
eye is controlled by a set of four muscles: Superior Oblique, Lateral
Rectus, Medial Rectus, and the Inferior Oblique. In layman's terms: Up,
left, right, and down. Popping one's eye from the socket with a soup
spoon can cause a bit of damage to these tender muscles. Thus the droop
of the eyelid. Trevor did a fine job guiding the computer on the
installation of the implant, but tightening the finer cosmetic
accoutrements required a deft surgeon’s scalpel. However the marvels the
implant contained more than outweighed the cosmetic hamper. And at least
Reggie retained his depth perception.

They pulled onto New Hampshire Avenue and headed northwest, Reggie’s
muscles tensed.

“Wait, the laptop,” he called out to Trevor.

“Already committed it to memory," Trevor tapped his temple and continued
to drive. “You got it, too.”

Reggie gasped as his mind raced with the files and data from the laptop
flooding his thoughts and the implant.

“What’s happening?!” Reggie shrieked as the information filled his mind
like water pouring into an empty bucket.

“Sorry, I should've asked you first," Trevor concentrated on the road
ahead as the morning sun glared across the pavement.

“Asked for what first?” Reggie demanded.

“To use your processor," Trevor was calm. Reggie was about to lose
control.

Trevor’s implant released a signal; a burst of a radio frequency that no
organic ear could hear but one that the antenna embedded in their eye
sockets recognized. The signal carried with it a command, as a plane
with bombs to drop. The payload was a command to initiate an individual
protocol and process data in a certain way. This triggered the implant
in Reggie to begin analyzing and sorting the data into a form that he
could understand.

Essentially, every human brain stores and retrieves information in a
unique way; that’s how we are made - we’re all special little
snowflakes. So when we marvel at one person being so incredibly smart
and remark that we’d like to borrow their brains; it would do little for
us because our brains don’t work like anyone else's brain other than our
own. The algorithm triggering in Reggie’s brain right now was learning
the way his brain processes data and information and would then organize
the flood to suit him.

“TO USE MY WHAT?! WHAT THE FUC…” Reggie stopped as his vision and mind
filled with images and reports of every document and file retrieved from
Jessica’s laptop. It was intense and consumed his senses as not only he
saw it occurring but the information invaded and bonded with his
memories.

After a few minutes, Trevor pulled onto Interstate 495 and Reggie was
finally able to speak.

“What. Just. Happened?”

“I’m sorry, I really am,” Trevor apologized. “But I needed to get us out
of the District and you were, well, being a little bit of a bitch.”

“Fuck you, man," Reggie said coldly. “I feel like you just raped my
mind. But I feel like all that is, I don’t know, I can’t explain.”

“Like you experienced it?” Trevor offered.

“Yes! Exactly!” Reggie scoffed still trying to understand new memories
found in his mind carrying weight from years ago and places he knew he
had never trespassed.

“Well, that is because you kinda did. It’s part of the bonding process.”

“The what?” Reggie stopped and held his hands up. “Nevermind, I don’t
want the details.”

“Too late," Trevor grinned, “it’s in your brain already.”

Reggie’s mind suddenly shifted into visions of the development of the
implant. Blueprints and diagrams. Visions from another set of eyes.
Mathematical formulas stretching over multiple chalkboards. Monkeys on
operating tables. Then he was swinging through branches of trees and
eating fruit. Furry clawed hands picking bugs out of the fur of another
monkey. Then the dark nipple of a furry breast coming toward his face
and a sensation of his mouth around a furry nipple and the taste of
monkey breast milk in his mouth.

“Oh God, what is that?! FUCK! WHAT DID YOU DO?!?! WHAT JUS, THE FUCK,
HAPPEN?!”

“Are you to the fruit eating part?”

“No, I got to the MONKEY-TIT BREASTFEEDING PART!” Reggie began sweating.

“Oh yeah, I forgot about that. The implant tends to go at the pace of
the new user. You’re smart, it likes you. So it’s going fast. But
there’s one more part..wait for it,” Trevor counted down on his fingers.

Three.

Two.

One.

Go.

“OWW! Fuck! It all went black!” Reggie cupped his hands to his face, he
expected to see blood on them.

“Yeah,I hate that part,” Trevor said as a distant look washed over his
face, his face briefly flushing white.

A moment passed between them. Icy cold and empty.

“Was that a bolt gun?” Reggie’s breathing was labored.

“Those are the databanks, memories, if you will. The development phases
of the implant that is currently decorating its new home. Your brain.”

Another moment.

“It takes a little while to forget those bits, but you have to keep them
or it throws off the whole A.I.’s Self-Identity or some computer-science
bullshit.

“Shit," Reggie’s mouth was dry. His stomach gurgled with hunger.

“There’s a pack of jerky in the glove box," Trevor suggested. “You’ll
need to keep your calorie intake up. The implant requires a lot of
energy to process at optimal levels. Stay away from sugary drinks. Stick
to protein, there’s less of a crash with protein.”

As Reggie pulled the bag of jerky from the glove box, he asked, “So,
those are my memories now?”

“Yes, and no," Trevor answered. “They are the implants’ data banks and
now the implant is in your brain. So they are your memories and the
implants data. So yes and no.”

“What about my memories?” Reggie’s mind flew back to he and Jessica
laying on the couch last night.

“Don’t worry,” Trevor wiped his hand across the air in front of his
face, dismissing a time and distance alert form his own implant. “Your
spank file is safe. But you’ll probably not remember your piano
teacher.”

“Who?” Reggie said with a mouthful of jerky.

“Exactly," Trevor pushed his hand into the bag of jerky and retrieved a
strip of dried meat after shaking the bag from his fist.

They chewed quietly as the highway stretched out before them. They did
not speak, but their minds were not silent. The morning sky above them
was filled with red, orange, and yellow clouds.

“They’re Watching...6,200

The average number of times that some popular Android smartphone apps
collected device locations of participants in a Carnegie Mellon
University study over a two-week period - an average of once every three
minutes, according to the Wall Street Journal. The study analyzed the
app use of 23 volunteers, the newspaper reported. Researchers earlier
found that apps share location information with advertisers 73 percent
of the time.”

Washington Post Express, March 26, 2015.

10 - Diversion

“In April 2013, a group of unidentified individuals carried out an
attack on a electrical transmission substation in order to disrupt the
electrical grid and supply to the surrounding area, if not to whole
portions of the electrical grid. This mission was successful. They have
never been caught or identified. The investigator's notes indicated the
perpetrators operated with “commando-like military tactics.”

General Robinson paused to take a sip of water from the glass next to
the long stemmed microphone on the table in front of him, in the middle
of the Senate Select Subcommittee for National Defense. Bodies and
camera lenses filled the camera well as the whirl and clipping of the
shutters sounded. The panel of Senators chairs was half filled on either
side with Aides shuttling in and out of the hearing room through the
staff and Senator’s entrance behind the bench. Behind General Robinson,
the audience was silent save for the occasional cough and sniffle. The
Reporters table immediately to the General’s left held captive
journalists from the largest newspapers, website, and cable news
channels across the world. Their hands furiously tapping and click on
the keyboards and screens in front of furrowed brows and teeth clenched
on ink pens and pencils.

“Surveillance video confirms that these men assaulted the substation
presumably attempting to cut local power.” General Robinson read the
report in front of him. “Their hand signals and movement patterns
resembled small teams close quarters training, the sort Navy SEALs and
Army Rangers receive in advanced tactical training schools. Intelligence
analysis indicates these tactics are well known and studied outside of
our armed forces and as such do not suspect homegrown terrorist groups
or other bad actors.”

General Robinson continued to read the report aloud as camera shutters
snapped in rapid succession, television cameras focused on his calm
outward demeanor. In the Cable News Bureaus across town, the
commentators were no doubt reading a one sheet with the General’s
publicly available resume in some attempt to help the audience at home
understand who this man was and why he was saying such scary things. The
Senators either hung on every word of the narrative he described or
spoke in hushed tones to their aides.

“It has been suspect by analysts within the CIA, FBI, and NSA that this
was a practice mission by terrorists to test the vulnerabilities of the
security of the electrical power grid. These men were successful.”

General Robinson continued, “three or four men lowered themselves
underground to cut fiber-optic cables that supplied a portion of the
internet and communications backbone to millions of residents and
citizens. This particular junction was protected only by a heavy manhole
cover; easily pried open with a crowbar. These men also proceeded to
fire well over a 100 rounds from high-powered rifles at nearby
transformers and caused $16 million in damages. The resulting explosions
of the conductive liquids in the transformers provided a very clear and
present danger to the local residents and businesses.”

Always work the threat to the local residents and businesses into the
speech. Rule one of the “How to Make a Spectacle at a Public Hearing
Rulebook.” The General did not write the book, but he was a contributing
editor and acolyte.

Robinson relished these open-door briefings he gave to Congress. It was
moment to rattle cages and scare the appropriations committee into
signing blank checks; the perilous world of national security terrified
Congress.

Robinson continued, as he had every camera of the mass media focused
solely on him, “It is the Special National Security Council’s conclusion
that this is a systematic attempt to take down the electrical grid with
this behavior of disciplined and para-military attack."

Robinson had them exactly where he wanted them.

"We do not believe it is related to any particular group responsible for
previous attempts and attacks on United States interests abroad and
domestic. However, this does reveal a weakness in our domestic national
security policy. Our power grid is susceptible to terrorist attacks, but
we can, with the permission of Congress and the American people, protect
them from these dangers of our modern age.”

Robinson paused and took a sip of water, his pacing was polished from
years of saber-rattling in these halls.

"Terrorism has many definitions and forms. One of them is an extremely
well-trained group of operatives attacking a major piece of
infrastructure in an expertly planned attack. This is a most
sophisticated and focused attack that has occurred on the grid. At least
to the knowledge and information available to the National Security
Council under the laws and regulations we are subject to today."

Robinson turned the sheet of paper he was reading. It was for show.
Aside from the facts in the first few paragraphs the remainder he had
the whole statement memorized but also had it auto-scrolling in his
heads up display via his implant. He also had a ticker scrolling across
with the latest rise and fall of the Dow Jones Industrial average. It
was almost like being a maestro of an orchestra when he ramped up his
inflection and doomsday-speak in the speech the ticker would trend
downward. When he plied Congress with flatterings and examples of
success in this war on Digital-Terrorism the ticker trended higher.

“We have studied the possibilities of attacks of these and the potential
effects of more widespread and coordinated attacks such as the many I
have described and concluded that we have great - potentially
insurmountable-challenges of protecting our infrastructure, from food
and water to cybersecurity to our nation’s potentially devastating
effects to our unprotected power grid.” The ticker of the stock market
dipped into the red.

Another sip of water touched Robinson’s lips. Machiavelli could set his
pocket watch to Robinson’s manner and pattern.

“Power blackouts have plagued the power grid. Leading to numerous
instances of widespread loss of power and potential lawlessness,” He
paused. Down the ticker dove by five’s and one’s.

“However, our successes find relief in the fact that newspapers and
televisions screens are not filled every morning with the success of our
enemies, but rather the absence of those successes and the silence of
their failures.” The ticker shot into the green at a pace of tens and
twenties.

“We have found much success in our focus and to cut these efforts to
disable our great country, if allowed to do by the people, Congress, and
the President.” He paused to the applause of the shutters of news
cameras. The ticker soared into triple digit gains.

“I am happy to answer any questions from the panel. Thank you,” General
Robinson closed his folio and rested his hands one over the other across
the black leather.

The questions were as scattered as the concepts General Robinson faced
every day. From the obscure and opaque far-right theories that the
wavelengths of the technology surrounding everyone had on “the sanity of
these terrorists” to the “tread left on the tires of our constitution”
in facing these digital terrorists wherever they may be. Robinson had a
deft and nimble hand in his responses - the implant also helped as it
triggered on statistics and mined his databases for the exact figures
and reports the Congressmen raised in their awkward questions. One
Senator began his question with a quote from Ben Franklin.

“Those who would give up essential liberty , to purchase a little
temporary Safety, deserve neither Liberty nor Safety.” The Senator
smugly stated and then launched into a long-winded multi-part question.

“Thank you for your question, Senator,” Robinson said. “When Mr.
Franklin wrote those words in 1775 he was speaking on behalf of the
Pennsylvania Assembly concerning the immediate French and Indian War on
the frontier and focused on taxation regarding lands and the
jurisdiction to levy taxes upon them. Further he was speaking not as a
subject of the Government, but rather as an Assembly Member.”

The Senator’s face flushed white with his mouth agape.

“Further, the context of the quote you proffered does not focus on
‘civil liberties’ but rather the right of self-governance of the states
against the collective security provided by all.”

Internet search engines suddenly received increased queries for “Ben
Franklin” and “Liberty” and “Safety” quote. Robinson knew this because
along his head-up display he had continuous tracker of keywords pulled
from his conversations and how the Internet was being queried with them.
Normally, this was only useful at cocktail parties, but in a
Congressional hearing the element of surprise and a deft pivot to keep
your opponents on their heels was imperative.

“To finish my point, the quote does not keep with the subject matter,
but rather I would feel Mr. Franklin would be reassured by the
cooperation between the states and agencies as well as the extent of the
protection provided with his 18th century perspective.”

The use of “ Mr. Franklin” was to put Robinson on his same level. It was
not a gesture of respect, but rather a demotion.

The Committee Chairman gaveled the expiration of the Committee Member’s
time and the next question was called and launched from the opposite
side of the bench.

The questions from the Congressmen were animated yet not directed toward
General Robinson, but rather the faceless violent monsters at the gate
Robinson previously described. The posturing lasted for two hours, and
it was well worth the General’s time as each question further increased
the public budget and in kind the exponentially increasing the black
budget for “off-the-books” projects.

Power outages were the most terrifying aspects of his dire forecast. In
just a hundred years, people had become so dependant on electricity they
had no comprehension on being without it. Every aspect of their lives
revolved around something that required power to operate.

Paramilitary attacks aside. Rouge squads of anarchists didn’t phase the
worried sheep. It was the neutered nakedness of no lights that was the
most terrifying and resonating part of his statement. Bad men blowing up
substations were some amorphic illustration to Mr. and Mrs. America.
Never did the thought cross their imagination that the bomb makers were
next door. Assassins sitting next to them on the commute to work. Nobody
looked anyone in the eye anymore anyways. It was every person and the
cellphone for themselves. Damn the rest of the world to hell if they
couldn’t get that Snapchat posted.

General Robinson understood this. He grasped early in his fear-mongering
career that you can’t scare the public too much, just enough is good
enough to get through the next funding cycle.

This process is like good sex; not fucking. Sex. You never want it to
finish with a huge explosion and cascade of terror. Keep it tantric and
ongoing. Never ending and impossible to defeat, but controllable with
just the right amount of pressure, apprehension, and filthy-filthy
money.

General Robinson understood how to massage money out of Congress with
the willing thumbs up from the taxpayers; skills admired by the most
skilled massage parlor technicians in red light districts the world
over.

Robinson knew the vote count by the end of the hearing. It was the lead
on the evening news, even trended on various social media platforms by
the afternoon. The enemies were most certainly at the gates and they
came in peace with Congressional funding and a mandate from the public
to scour the earth for the evil-doers and keep the American lifestyle
secure and comfortably numb.

11 - Loose Ends

“Miss Powell," The disembodied voice announced.

Jessica’s brain felt fogged and slushy from the drugs flowing through
her system.

She opened her eyes and found herself strapped to a table with her arms
extended in supine position. As her vision cleared she traced the tubes
coming out of her past the leather bands holding her to the table toward
machines beeping rhythmic sounds and flashing lights. Then her eyes
focused on the mirrored glass directly in front of her.

“What?! You don’t want me to see your faces?” She mentally checked her
infrared vision on her enhanced secure-link.

[]

A searing pain shot through her skull and a scream shot from her throat
and she strained against the straps.

“You will want to avoid trying any little tricks that you may have up
your sleeve with your secure-link. We’ve...adjusted the firmware.”

“Fucking, pricks.”

“We can stop all of this if you will just tell us where the data went.”

“What data?”

Her right eye began to feel warm, then hot.

“Where did it go, Ms. Powell?”

Her heart rate sped up. Her eye felt like it was in a searing hot
skillet. The medical machines began to sing in terrifying chorus.

“FUCK! Stop it!”

“Where did you send the data?”

“Up your mother’s ass!”

“Language, Ms. Powell. We expected more from a retired Air Force Officer
and Congressional staffer.”

She grimaced and strained against her bounds.

“I’m recently unemployed,” she growled. “Just haven’t cleaned out my
desk.”

Another shot of pain stabbed her behind her eyes causing her body to
shake uncontrollably. She felt her muscles begin to constrict and relax
uncontrollably. She began to smell urine and taste blood. Then she
understood, she wasn’t sending these signals from her brain, they were
sending them from the implant.

“It can end, in a heartbeat. Just tell us what we need to know.”

The signal shot to the receptors in the implant and triggered electrical
impulses through her nervous system causing uncontrollable twitches and
convulsions to nerves and muscles in places she’d forgotten they
existed.

“Thinking about writing a tell-all memoir,” She coughed and gagged.
“I’ll be sure to send you a courtesy copy.”

“It’s data, Ms. Powell. You know it can be traced.”

“Then fucking, Trace! It!” She spat and spattered blood and saliva on
the mirrored glass.

“You’ve only got a few more tries, Ms. Powell.”

“I’m a tuffy from Jersey. You’ll never pull it out of me.”

Another jolt of heat and searing pain stabbed her. Her screams echoed in
the room.

On the other side of the glass, Clemens grimaced as he shifted his feet
from foot to foot nervously while standing in the back corner of the
room.

“Sir, she is resisting," an operator turned his head to the side and
looked to Clemens for instruction.

“Give her another jolt. I’m tired of her damn mouth.”

Her muffled screams seemed to rattle the one-way glass.

“She’s got a pair of lungs on her," Clemens calmed his nervousness and
smoothed a silver tie against his chest.

“Sir, the secure-link has its limitations," the Operator cautioned.

“We’ve exceeded limitations before. The device is incredibly resilient.
Again," Clemens reached for a cup of coffee and took a sip as Jessica
screamed.

A door to the secure room opened. Robinson stepped through as the
electronic door pulled closed.

“If you kill her, we’re back to square one," Robinson poured himself a
cup of coffee.

“Then we’ll just have to do that,” Clemens nodded to the Operator. “If
it comes to that."

Another set of screams escaped Jessica’s lips.

“We might need a physical touch for this one," Robinson blew on his
coffee.

“Like what?” Clemens asked.

“Did you never learn chess?” Robinson asked. “In this line of work you
have to think steps ahead. Four, five, eight. Depends on the opponent,"
Robinson took a sip. The hot liquid singed his tongue.

“Fuck you and your games. Hit her again," Clemens ordered.

“Hold that order, Operator," Robinson commanded. “She’s no good to us
dead!”

Robinson smoothed his uniform and approached the microphone next to the
Operator. Clemens fumed and took a hard gulp of his hot coffee,
purposefully burning his tongue rather than biting it.

Through the glass Robinson saw Jessica. Her nose and ear bleeding. She
had a wild look in her eyes and her arms strained at the restraints.

“Ms. Powell, we don’t have the time for these games. Nor the patience.
If you are not willing to be helpful, then we shall have to try other
means.”

The voice was distorted, but Jessica did not flinch. Her muscles burned
with lactic acid build-up and strain. Her veins bulging through her
skin. She knew every minute she held out was another minute the data
remained out of their hands. Her torture resistance training in the Air
Force was thorough, but it did not include restricting internal implants
attached to the ocular nerve; one of the most sensitive nerves in the
body.

On the other side of the glass, Robinson watched Jessica pant and
collect her senses.

“Send in a User," He ordered.

12 - Tactical Advantages

Standing tall, a glass building twelve stories high broke the rolling
woodlands of central Maryland. In the morning the traffic on the
Baltimore-Washington Parkway suffered from the glare of the highly
polished and regularly cleaned glass facade. The building was seventeen
and a half miles from Baltimore. Twenty-three miles from the heart of
the District of Columbia. On most buildings the name of the company with
the most square footage proudly emblazons its logo or name, not this
one. There were no signs. Not even a street or block number for a person
to identify it by. It was a glass and steel rectangular block to all
that ever bothered to recognize it. However, it was no ordinary
building.

Vision Systems was one of the largest contractors in the world when it
came to research and development to a Who's Who of fellow government
contractors directly to the governments themselves. It had major
corporate offices in Australia, Canada, New Zealand, and the United
Kingdom - as well as numerous offices, labs, testing sites, and
corporate holdings in the United States. Not to mention the various
subsidiaries and “PO Box Offices” sprinkled around the world placed in
both friendly and unfriendly nations.

Vision Systems has had many names over the course of its thirty-six-year
existence. Its true beginning was mired in a classified mystery and
dogmatic legend. It first began to receive public notice when it
coordinated and developed the foreign surveillance system known as
NOLECHE. Pronounced “No-Le-che” - as in, “no milk.”

NOLECHE was used by various governments around the world to tap the
landline and satellite communication systems around the world through
the undersea cables connecting the continents. There were five countries
that paid Vision Systems for its initial development and maintenance of
this program. Internally, Vision Systems executives and directors called
their best customers “the Five Sisters,” the Australia, Great Britain,
France, Germany, and the United States. Externally, they refused to
release any of the names of its customers due to confidentiality
agreements wrapped in the blanket of national security. For its
confidence and discretion, Vision Systems was paid immensely and in turn
became incredibly profitable. Soon small offices in nondescript office
campuses in far off lands grew to entire wings of mid-sized office
buildings and then tall glass and steel buildings never more than a few
minutes drive to the local embassies or consulates. Vision Systems was
more than a provider of capability it became the next door neighbor in
each of its best customer’s backyards offering exactly what its
customers demanded, on time and under budget.

Vision Systems has never been a “household name.” They are generally
only known by members of the joint-chiefs of staff, a handful of
Washington insiders, and rarely mentioned by journalists agile enough
traverse the black and white jungle of government appropriation bills.

Not until 1996 that a document found its way into the hands of
journalist and news stories about the international eavesdropping
program began to gain traction in the mass media. It was not until 2000
that a former director of one of the United States intelligence agencies
publicly acknowledged its use by the United States. Even with this, its
use was not curtailed nor shuttered. Instead it remains a viable
signals-intelligence gathering mechanism to this day; an “unblinking eye
guarding the horizon” was the sales pitch.

In short, Vision Systems has had the ability to capture every digital
communication in the known world for over twenty years. It has also
continued to improve and develop numerous additional systems whose
existence is vehemently denied.

The FILTER system was an extension of NOLECHE and numerous other
projects and contracts funded by the Five Sisters.

The research and development has largely been ignored by the public. It
has not been without its setbacks or its failures.

In 2001, a gunman walked into a church on a Sunday morning. He unloaded
two clips of 25 rounds of ammunition from his AK-47 rifle before being
taken out by a police sniper. This was determined to be a disgruntled
man with no real motive for the violence. An isolated incident conducted
by a “lone wolf.”

In March of 2004, a man clad in full body armor hijacked a bus of
children as they were visiting a public zoo. The children were freed,
but not until after the man executed their teachers and chaperones on
live television. Again, no motive could be discovered for the man’s
actions. An isolated incident conducted by a “lone wolf.”

A number of other incidents found brief flashes of fame in the public’s
gaze but the germination of Project FILTER went unnoticed by the public.
These have never been linked back to Vision Systems.

###

"This is a nation of 'laws,'" Trevor's hands released the steering wheel
for a moment to air-quote his emphasis then he reclaimed it. "But there
are two laws. The one the people know and understand and believe protect
them from the bad guys."

He paused to switch on the windshield wipers.

"But there is another set of laws. Those are the ones on how those laws
are interpreted. And applied against citizens."

An image flashed across Reggie's heads up display, Then files and photos
flew across his eyes. He felt his mind swell with the information
pouring in.

"What are you showing me?"

"It's the latest batching from the files Jessica got."

"Why does it feel like it's filling my brain?"

"Because it is. Just read, you're already memorizing it. It's not going
anywhere."

Reggie's mind flushed with memories. The reports and accounts became
real to him. He gasped at its depth and detail. It was every black
operation and all the paperwork and records that his country had
authorized over the past twelve years. It pulsed through him as his mind

stuff from the Iranian Revolution and Beirut!" Reggie was dumbfounded.

"Three is more buried in more servers and secure networks," Trevor
sucked his teeth. "Did you know the human brain only uses up to twelve
percent of its capacity? Not processing power, but just capacity. This
is the download folder on the laptop in your skull, borher. Welcome to
the other eighty-eight percent, my friend."

After a moment, Trevor explained that Reggie and he were connected
together by a multi-faceted wireless network encrypted and
point-to-point. They could function separately, but better together.

"You've also got a connection to the part of the Internet that you
normally don’t look at. The Sub-Net or Darknet as the script kiddies
used to call it. Tread lightly, friend."

"What is it?"

"Did you ever wander into a place that shouldn't be? A seedy bar? A back
room of a porno shop? An illegal gambling den?" Trevor paused.
"Chinatown, DC at 3 am on a Wednesday morning?"

“Huh?” Reggie asked.

“You see a lot of the what ‘the wrong’ really is when you wander into
the ‘wrong’ places,” Trevor was fond of using air-quotes when he talked.
Reggie was less fond of every time he did it he took his hands off the
steering wheel. “The Darknet is a lot like that. All the times the other
side of the coin people are resting and relaxing or asleep that’s when
the crazy really gets out. You’ll have to take my word on it, but there

"I'm following you on this one. I guess so."

“So, with that said, you’ll just have to be careful with it. Cause you

much."

"I'm not understanding," Reggie was a baby learning to walk. He'd have
to learn to run before the day was done.

"Just think about it, but don't overthink it. It's not as organized as
the networks and internet you're used to. It's more numbers than words,
but you'll have to get used to it."

Reggie thought about Trevor's words as patches of information began to
wash in his mind. Reggie became frustrated.

"Quit fucking with me, Trevor."

Sometimes we won't be able to speak aloud. So you'll just have to
listen, think, and speak in your mind. The system is intuitive, it knows

Reggie paused and took a breath.

Fuck you, Trevor.

Thataboy! Gets easier the more you do it.

After a moment of silence between the two, Reggie's skin and spine

Is it a...al..always this creepy? Reggie's mind stuttered with a

Pace yourself, it's a marathon. In a few days, it will all be second

Reggie thought, It's not that, it's the actual tech, programs,

Trevor looked at Reggie out of the side or his eyes. It was what your

Trevor grinned, He knew more than what was in the file. He wanted it

Trevor sent an email file to Reggie's heads-up display. It read as a
