It was bigger than you. Above your pay grade, Trevor. Jessica thought

Representative Baker treated Reggie like a son. That was always the root

They took his legs out from under him.

Neither Trevor nor Reggie could tell which of the two of them had said

Trevor chuckled and winked, Jessica was taken when she smuggled this to

Reggie nodded, I know, that's where we are going. Start reading and find

  “Lavabit employed two stages of encryption for its paid subscribers:
  storage encryption and transport encryption. Storage encryption
  protects emails and other data that rests on Lavabit's servers.
  Theoretically, no person other than the email user could access the
  data once it was so encrypted. By using storage encryption, Lavabit
  held a unique market position in the email industry, as many providers
  do not encrypt stored data.”

In re Under Seal, 749 F.3d 276, 279 (4th Cir. 2014)

14 - Isolated Incidents

Henry Jessup worked in international currency trading. He often joked
that buying the yen and selling the crown was the weirdest bread and
butter he ever ate. Not many people got Jessup’s kind of humor. Currency
trading was not the most social of professions.

He spent most of his days behind a bank of computer monitors watching
charts grow and shrink while tapping on a keyboard - buying and selling
dollars, yen, pound sterling, even bitcoin. Each day his goal was just
to break even and keep his customers calling him and emailing the next
day for a fresh set of buys, puts, shorts, and sells.

Jessup, while a small cog in a large machine, found the data that
pointed toward upending a very lucrative merger for two of the
continents largest banks and major gateways for currency trading. He
found terrorist money flowing freely between one banks South American
coffers and into subsidiary bank in Italy. The dollars used for the oil
deals flowed out of the Brazilian bank and into the European bank still
stinking of petrol the conversion to Euro notes and then into Riyals
were simple ledger trades, currency for currency, but the impulses
behind them were unmistakable. The oil pulled from the sovereign ground
of a country whose religious taste favored stonings for bare ankles and
hanging for sodomy. The money paid for that black gold then traveled
through the digital backbone by which the modern world rotates and into
zealot coffers of which a specific and dogmatic percentage per the Holy
Word of God commanded that it be used to drive the infidels out from the
holy lands by jihad - the personal and religious struggle of every
devoted Muslim.

These funds found their ways to bank accounts used to buy food for camps
of men huddled in the gorges and caves of Afghanistan, the farms of
southern Sudan and the deserts of Yemen held nearly uncountable oases of
devoted followers of the Holy Word of God and dutifully carried out its
instructions with violence and terrorism directed at the infidels and
the enemies of the one true God.

Jessup was found dead in a stairwell of his office building three days
after he went missing - broken neck after a three flight fall through
the empty space between the guardrails. He was working on a special
analysis for a client involving currency and interest rate trades by a
major bank in Europe for the past three years in anticipation of a major
acquisition. This bank had been suspected of laundering money and
revenues for international oil and drug cartels along with international
financiers of terrorism.

A typed suicide note was found on Jessup’s desk. This was unusual
because his office and house were littered with handwritten notes. It is
a common theme in suicide investigation that those that commit suicide
want their loved ones to have the personal connection, therefore a
handwritten note is commonly found near the body. Jessup did not have a
girlfriend or wife. His parents died years ago. No siblings. He was
however very popular among his coworkers and neighbors. He often called
them his “adopted family.”

Jessup’s suicide note was cold, short, and not signed in his own hand.

I have done a horrible thing. I cannot live with it any longer. I’m
sorry.

Not even his name was typed. It puzzled the detectives for a moment, but
with a few questionable deposits found in his bank account history
coupled with a browser history of even more questionable subject matter
and lack of discretion, along with a private file on his work computer
filled with dark and extreme pornography gave the detectives had little
reason to investigate further.

The detectives failed to notice the timing of the bank deposits. These
occurred just before his death and in amounts that would likely trigger
money laundering alarms.

Why would a rich man kill himself?

Why would a man so versed in anti-money laundering laws and regulations
allow such obvious amounts of questionable money be deposited into his
personal accounts?

The history browser logs displayed the various websites and downloads
were visited and initiated during times when Jessup was normally asleep
or his calendars indicated he was engaged in conference calls or video
chats with clients.

Finally, the cache of pornography found on Jessup’s work computer hard
drive showed a checksum and creation time of roughly an hour and thirty
minutes after the approximate time of death. It appeared that even in
death, Jessups thirst for the prurient arts was insatiable.

###

Operation successful.

Target suicide investigation was closed by authorities after payloads
discovered.

###

Angie Rainn was a brilliant computer scientist. She developed and
successfully proved a portion of the cryptography used in virtual
currencies. It was so successful that she operated her own virtual
currency exchange website which was highly profitable. It was highly
secure and had never been hacked. It was considered the Fort Knox of
online currency exchanges.

She was found dead in her home in Toronto with a gunshot to head. The
internet, where she and many of her fellow tech-generation lived,
erupted with an outpouring of sadness and condolences. Virtually every
social media website across the world was peppered with these messages
for weeks. News stories flowed out of media outlets.

The density of the technology and its alien terms frustrated police
investigators and news reporters. Angie Rainn’s death was quickly
confirmed as a suicide. There was compelling evidence to prove
otherwise.

Angie Rainn had a heart problem. One reason for her living in Toronto
was the low cost and often free healthcare that she received. Her heart
problem was normalized and under control with a simple compound
supplement of a handful of drugs for circulation and mineral
deficiencies. A week before her “suicide,” she filled the prescription
via her online pharmacy for a six-month supply.

The gunshot wound to her head was at an angle which was more unusual
than most suicides. The wound angled downwards through the front of the
skull and temporal lobe. Generally when a person wants to die by
gunshot, she avoids the head or at least the face, when she does they

What had been lost in the inventory of Angie’s apartment and her home

James McClandry was the chief of staff for Senior Senator Herald DeGault
of Louisiana. McClandry had been chief of staff for DeGault for six
years. He was unmarried, not an uncommon trait in the busy political
world of Capitol Hill - but also a closet homosexual.

His sexual orientation remained in the closet due to the Senator's
personal opinions as well as the prevaling opinion that homosexuallity
was a “sin against God.” In Louisiana, it was preferred that if a man
felt those feelings that he should ignore them and attempt to prove them
wrong.

This burdened McClandry and he had planned on resigning once he was able
to secure a lobbying position with a K Street firm. He was interviewing,
although quietly and without the permission of the Senator. He was also
thinking of leaving his lover when he found the courage to finally leave
the Senator’s staff. Unfortunately, this would have to occur at the same
time as his departure from Capitol Hill because the Senator’s personal
security guard, Alex Thompson was McClandry’s lover.

Senator DeGault in the last election thundered against the “gays” and
their “assault on the moral fabric of Louisianian's across the globe.”
DeGault won his re-election by a narrow margin of half a percentage
point, but he was the victor nonetheless.

McClandry bit his tongue every day while on the campaign trail with the
Senator. In addition to traveling with a bigot running for office,
McClandry was tasked with keeping DeGault “up to speed” on the oversight
committee’s latest investigation into U.S. Government and law
enforcement surveillance against private citizens. This required
McClandry to constantly be glued to his smartphone, secure-link, and
various computers he carried in a forty-pound satchel. Alex was often
kind enough to carry the dufflebag when the Senator was not looking.

One evening, a file was emailed to McClandry’s office email. It was a
draft of the committee’s investigation report. The thousand page
document began to expose the vast network and multi-layered system of
surveillance against private citizens and numerous violations of the
fourth amendment’s protections against warrantless searches and seizures
conducted by government agencies and military contractors around the
globe.

DeGault was hoping to use the report - with his signature on it - to
springboard his next term as Senator into a larger set of offices at
1600 Pennsylvania Avenue.

McClandry was just hoping to survive the endless tide of drafts and
red-line edits being passed back and forth amongst the various staffers
of fellow Senators, all with their own objectives and internal
motivations for the public release of the damning report.

Once the final draft of the report loaded onto McClandry’s laptop he
began to read it, but the long day sprinting from event to event and the
coolness of the air conditioning of his hotel room made his eyes heavy
and soon the laptop was the only thing in the room switched on.

McClandry awoke the next morning to a pounding on the door and a gruff
authoritative voice shouting for him to open the door. He stumbled out
of the bed wearing nothing but a white t-shirt and his boxers. He had
his hand on the doorknob when he heard the one phrase he was not
expecting to hear, “We have a warrant to search this hotel room.”

The hotel room door burst open with the thrust of a hand-held battering
ram behind it. McClandry was slammed against the wall and pinned behind
the door. An FBI swat team flooded his hotel room with submachine guns
and nine-millimeter handguns drawn.

“Freeze! Hands in the air! Face down!” The commands were repeated in
rapid-fire succession from almost every agent’s throat. McClandry was
quaking in fear with his hands behind his head.

“Sir, I have the device," one officer held the laptop in his hands.

“Does it have the material on it? As the informant said it would?” the
commanding officer asked.

“Yes, there are tons of it," the officer confessed. “It’s disgusting.”

McClandry was still shaking the sleep from his mind.

“James McClandry, you are hereby under arrest. You have the right to
remain...” the commanding officer continued to read McClandry his
rights.

“What am I charged with? What’s going on here?” McClandry pleaded.

“Possession of illegal contraband," the commanding officer spoke
sternly. “Child pornography.”

McClandry was dumbfounded and aghast. He could not verbally respond. His
skin flushed a ghostly white. “I...I...don’t...I don’t ha...have...any.”

“Show him.”

The officer holding McClandry’s laptop spun it around to display a
series of horrific images on the screen.

McClandry’s knees buckled and he began to drop to the floor. Two
officers grabbed him by the shoulders and snapped cuffs around his
wrists and dragged him out of the hotel room.

McClandry was immediately dismissed from his position. His attorney
posted his bond and McClandry retreated to his Washington, D.C. condo
for a week, not even leaving it for food.

His body was discovered by D.C. Metro Police after a neighbor smelt a
decomposing body from the hallway. He was found in his bathtub with his
wrists slashed and two empty bottles of prescription medicine for a
sleeping disorder he developed during the Senator’s last election
campaign.

Outside, Reggie stood watching the sun light receed toward the tree

“I’ve never been sure of it, but I’ve always known there was something

Ted Withman was a national security report for the Washington Bulletin.
Previously he served in the United States Army as a Public Relations
Officer in the Pentagon. When he finished his term with the Army, he
dusted off his journalism degree and began to write stories for a small
magazine focused on the defense industry. His clips grew and in a few
years he had a stringer job at the Bulletin. After he exposed a defense
contractor’s invoice padding scheme that cost the Army millions, Withman
received a desk, a phone, and a computer. Since then his writing focused
on government oversight and waste. Withman was well respected in the
media industry.

Reggie read Withman’s writing and had the chance to deny him a quote
when the Air Base in Representative Baker’s district was found polluting
the water table.

The story was about Vision Systems.

###

The headline was stark and sobering. It rumbled through Washington, D.C.
and gurgled through broadcast media. To Withman’s credit, he did call
Vision Systems Public Relations office for a comment before publishing.
They declined and denied any knowledge about what he “alleged” - their
choice of words.

The talk shows and news channels raced to cover the revelations.
Producers and even on-air anchors peppered Withman's cellphone. So much
that he pulled the battery. One anchor even tried calling him via skype,
live on the air.

Withman was not ducking their inquires. He was sitting in a meeting, now
in its fourth hour at the Bulletin. Half of the board room filled to
standing room only with Assistant United States Attorneys from the
Department of Justice. One of them was doing all the speaking, which
after the first hour elevated to more shouting threats than a
discussion.

Withman had been advised by the papers' general counsel to assert his
fifth amendment rights. Withman corrected him and asserted his first
amendment rights instead.

Withman's editor, Charles Glass, sat next to him. He had offered to
order sandwiches for the representatives of the Justice Department, but
this was refused. Glass marveled that half of them had not fallen out
after standing for so long. He wondered if their briefcases even had
paper in them.

"Gentlemen, we've endured enough of these questions," Glass said calmly
interrupting the AUSA. "If you have further questions, please submit
them in writing through the paper’s legal counsel. My publisher wants us
to get tomorrow's paper on the racks."

He stood up and buttoned his sport coat and left the room. Withman stood
gathered his notepad and pen and exited behind Glass. The silence in the
room was deafening.

Once in Glass's office, with the door closed, they chuckled. "Whatever
you have next needs to be airtight."

"I know," Withman pulled his cellphone and battery out of his pocket and
held them up for Glass to see.

Glass nodded and pulled his cellphone from his pocket and pulled the
battery. He then unplugged his desk phone. And pulled the webcam and
microphone from his desktop computer.

Withman scanned the room looking for any other electronics, he found
none.

"We should get a faraday cage made for the newsroom," Withman said.

"A what?" Glass asked.

"It's a room lined with copper mesh, laid out to block electronic
transmissions from going in or out. The president travels with a
portable one. Secret Service sets it up next to his hotel room every
where he goes. It's like a portable SCIF room. Also, it needs to be
without windows. They have really cool little laser scoped microphones
that can listen through the vibrations against the glass of a window."

Withman pointed behind Glass to a large window that overlooked 15th
street.

"We have to have a space out of sight and away from their ears. That
includes the network. It has to be cold storage."

"What's the next story?" Glass asked.

"I'm going to need two reporters to handle follow up and comment pieces.
That should buy us at least a week," Withman spoke softly, in a hushed
tone.

"I know that," Glass leaned forward over his desk. "I meant the next
story."

"I've only got the first and small follow up on a program they use here
in the states."

"You only have one?" Glass almost shouted, but he held back.

"They are going to come in pieces, it's not an information dump. This
shit goes deep and my source has to keep himself alive. The information
is doing that and we can't push it."

Glass sighed and slumped back in his chair. "Take over some space down
in the archive. Make enough space for four desks."

Withman nodded, stood up, and left Glass's office.

Glass swung his chair around and looked out the window at the grey sky
outside. "I'm going to miss my window."

###

The White House Press Room was percolating about the front page story in
the Washington Bulletin. Withman was not in the room, he was currently
in a conference room up 15th Avenue having a Department of Justice
Attorney gnash his teeth at him for the past hour.

Reporter James Conway sat in the Washington Bulletin’s reserved chair.
He was allowed to see the copy of the story before it was published,
late last night. He knew the press briefing would be chaotic as his
colleagues would be scrambling to scoop up any extra bits they could
find. Conway had been the White House Correspondent for the Bulletin for
the past three years. This morning his eyes were bagged with
sleeplessness. The story he read last night had kept him from enjoying
any sense of rest.

A hush washed over the room as the Press Secretary for the President
entered the room. Alex Dunnigan was recently appointed Press Secretary
after his predecessor accepted a position with a major communications
corporation and was currently advising a number of large corporations on
public relations and policy.

“Good Morning, all,” Dunnigan began. “First off no announcements.
Associated press?”

“The reports in the Bulletin this morning have left a lot of people
wondering exactly how much of these processes the White House knew of
and how much was condoned by the President. Can you comment?”

The bags under Dunnigan’s eyes resembled the ones under Conway’s.

“The White House has no comment at this time on the reports published by
the Post. Next, Reuters.”

“So the White House is denying involvement or denying comment?”

“The White House has no comment at this time.”

Conway raised his hand, like every reporter in the room. Dunnigan locked
eyes with him and glanced away.

“Next, Gazette?”

Everyone was surprised even the correspondent from the Gazette, Charles
Price - a twelve year veteran of the Washington D.C. news industry.
Dunnigan never called on the correspondent from the Washington Gazette.
It was funded by the leading anti-thesis to the current President’s
political party and virtually every core policy that the President stood
for.

“Did the President read the paper today?” The question hushed the
briefing room.

Dunnigan’s eyes flashed. He had been instructed to freeze out the
Bulletin's correspondent and to try and detract by going to the Gazette
early in the briefing. The strategy was that the Gazette of all papers
would be so focused on its own agenda that it would distract the
briefing and then the news cycle from the revelations in the Bulletin.
Right now all of that strategy had been washed away.

“The President receives a briefing from National Security every morning
and then…”

“Did the President read the Bulletin? This morning?” Price interrupted
Dunnigan - another shocking event to the room. “There was a shocking
story covered on the frontpage.”

He held up the paper for dramatic effect.

“I am sure the public would like to know if the revelations in the
Bulletin were as shocking to the President.”

Dunnigan was an Irish name. In the Navy he was a boxer. He was also
quite good at ducking and weaving away from the fists of an opponent,
but this was a seasoned and precise blow to the mid-section.

Price locked eyes with Conway for a brief moment immediately after
dropping his copy of the Bulletin to his lap. A knowing nod was
exchanged between them.

“The President is fully briefed on the situational reports from every
important aspect of the interests of the United States.”

“Did he call the FBI? NSA? CIA? It appears there is someone out there
with some very damning information and at least one paper willing to
spend the ink on it.” Price was lining up sound-bytes for his round of
afternoon interviews that he was going to get after making this show.

Dunnigan paused.

“After receiving these reports, the President has spoken with the heads
of certain affected departments and programs and asked that an internal
investigation and review be conducted in order to ascertain the scope of
this breach.”

Dunnigan reached for a glass of water from a table to the side of the
podium.

“So, there has been a breach?” Price continued before any of the rest of
the reporters could respond.

“The President has asked members of his administration to investigate
and review. And he is simply not going to comment on an ongoing
investigation.”

Dunnigan had tried to prepare himself for the roasting he was about to
receive, but nothing prepares for a roasting in front of the Press, when
they smell blood in the water. It was agonizing for Dunnigan. The
President and his chief of staff watched from the Oval Office, both
obvilous to even the existence of Vision Systems.

###

Reports and misinformation circulated on the internet and media
denouncing and discrediting the story in the Bulletin, a countermeasure
set in place by Vision Systems. Reggie being the source was never
identified and kept secret by Withman and Glass at the Bulletin, one of
the broadcast networks had uncovered some footage of a security camera
from Vision Systems headquarters near Fort Meade, Maryland of Reggie and
an unknown accomplice entering the building.

When reached for comment on the footage obtained, Vision Systems Senior
Spokesperson, Samantha Browser, stated that the man seen in the footage
was at the headquarters of Vision Systems and there was a theft that
occurred from one of its highly-secured and classified data banks.

Ms. Browser also assured, via satellite link, “the FBI, CIA, and Defense
Intelligence Agency had been contacted as well as local state and
federal police and an investigation was being conducted.”

Conveniently, a spokesperson for the Federal Bureau of Investigation was
available for a live interview with the broadcast network immediately
after Ms. Browser was allowed to make her comments.

“The Bureau is taking these occurrences very seriously and we are
conducting a full-scale forensic investigation into this individual and
the accomplice he was seen with in the video footage,“ the spokesperson
said. It was a script he knew by heart.

“But what about the recent revelations in the Washington Bulletin? Is
this man the person behind them?”

The spokesperson snapped with a canned answer, “The Bureau is
investigating all leads and possibilities, but is certain that this man
has something to do with the recent unauthorized disclosures.”

The broadcast anchor asked another question, “Is the identity of this
man known at this time?”

“His name is Reggie Watts. Is is an individual of great interest to the
Bureau and this investigation. He should not be approached by the public
and his whereabouts should be reported to the Bureau’s Crimetip
Hotline.”

###

The internet and the media outlets buzzed with revelations and rumors
about Reggie Watts. Being the former Chief of Staff to the late
Representative Baker was the first shocking headline to crawl along the
bottom of the television screens and email alerts. There were numerous
questions about whether he was an Israeli spy and if Rep. Baker was

the public lapping it up by the spoonful.

All of the countermeasures worked perfectly, the Operators slipped in
small bits of suspicion and scandal into various forums and chat rooms
across the underbelly of the Internet. They took root and bubbled up to
the surface. Then once the bubbles caught the attention of the
information-starved public, it spread wide and far, like a woodlands
fire in a drought.

To many he was a a digital hero fighting back against the long-supposed
disregard for the fourth amendment protections. Various technology and
social rights groups began to champion his name. He was vilified by the
other side of the coin as a traitor and spy working for a long list of
monsters and enemies of the United States. His credibility is
questioned, reputation forever destroyed.

Withman’s cell phone buzzed, on the screen a string of numbers. He
flipped open his specially formatted and encrypted laptop prepared by
the tech staff at the Bulletin and typed in the string of numbers and
tapped the enter key.

I’ve seen the news. A message appeared in a chat window.

Withman began to type, I know, we expected this to happen. This isn’t a
puff-piece.

It’s only going to get deeper. Did you take the precautions I
recommended?

Yes, Withman replied. Got the cage setting up now.

Good, Reggie sat in a motel room that was time-warped directly from the
seventies - shag carpet included. They will continue to attack me at
every turn. But I’m not the only one.

Withman scratched his forearm and returned his hands to his laptop. I’m
going to pad the releases with comment pieces to paint them into
corners, but I’m going to need another drop soon. Do you have something
for me?

Downloading now. Going to an encrypted synced file on your hard drive. A
file labeled Baker.

Withman tapped a command and the file populated half the screen - he
returned to the chat window. Your poetic license is not yet revoked, but
this isn’t much.

I know. It will have to come in drops. Not much bandwith on this
network, but there is a lot more. Start with the finance file. VS has
been capitalizing on the markets after their operations. Follow the
money. Reggie was hesitant to type the last sentence, because it was so
clichē, but it was so appropriate.

Two days after the first expose about Vision Systems was published, a
second story appeared on the front page of the Washington Bulletin.

Vision Systems Profits Subsidized With Secret Stock Trades.

The public outcry was deafening silence.

It would take too long for Withman to sift through the files and get to

16 - Damage Control

“Please tell me that this isn’t true,” Robinson faced a copy of the
Washington Bulletin spread across his desk. After a beat of silence,
“Clemens?”

The soft static of dead air sounded in his ear and he heard a long sigh.

“Yes and no.” Clemens had a copy of the Bulletin in front of him. “I
placed some future trades, but they were supposed to be small and
discreet.”

“Well,” the blood in Robinson’s neck pulsed. “IT DID NOT FUCKING WORK,
CLEMENS!” He wiped away the phone call from his secure-link and thrust
the papers from his desk. They rained and floated down on Robinson’s
office floor.

He took a breath and called up a message protocol in his secure-link.
Activate the Clean-up Protocol. His stomach gurgled and twisted in his
abdomen as it had not been a pleasant 72 hours.

The message pinged in OpOne’s secure-link. He grinned and initiated the
requested protocol.

###

An Operator’s FILTER pinged with a message. Enter the room you are
guarding and eliminate the target.

The Operator was assigned to the private security of a high level Vision
Systems Executive. For security purposes the FILTER system was always
activated and the Executive’s face was digitally blurred to the senses
of the Operator - he only knew it was his assignment by a digital
signature protocol through his FILTER. He never knew exactly who he was
guarding, but he knew that who he protected was in the office he was
guarding.

Your mission is to eliminate the target. Comply or the operation is
canceled.

The Operator gripped his Sig Sauer 9mm pistol and twisted the doorknob.
The thought flashed in the Operator’s mind, is this worth it?

He opened the door and saw his target glowing red - all over; the face
was not digitally blurred anymore. He recognized the face.

The target must be eliminated.

A gun materialized in Clemens’ hand to the eyes of the Operator. In
reality, it was a pencil he nervously tapped on his desk.

The Operator crossed the room and stood in front of the desk his target
was sitting behind.

“What the hell do you want?” Clemens shouted as his hand gripped the
pencil tighter.

The Operator drew his weapon from its holster - pointing down toward the
ground - and gripped it with both hands.

“What the hell? Put that away, Operator!” Clemens breathes drew short
and quick.

Eliminate the target, Operator.

The Operator raised his weapon and fired three shots to Clemens’ glowing
red chest. Ropes of blood erupted from Clemens’ chest as his lungs and
body cavity filled with blood.

Clemens gasped and slumped from his high back leather desk chair onto
the floor under his desk.

Objective complete, Operator. Excellent work.

A surge of energy erupted from the FILTER and through the Operator’s
nervous system causing a heart attack and violent muscle spasms. His
fingers rapidly clenched and released emptying the ammunition from its
clip into the wood paneling of Clemens’ office. Then the Operator’s free
hand clutched his chest and he dropped to the floor.

Operation completed, successfully. Robinson confirmed receipt of the
message and wiped away the message. A hundred secure messages populated
his secure-link from various Senators and Representatives in Congress.
All of them wanted answers to the story they just read in the Bulletin.

Robinson wished he had a hundred more Operators.

“In their present form, the seven paragraphs of subsection 1030(a)
outlaw

• computer trespassing (e.g., hacking) in a government computer, 18
U.S.C.

1030(a)(3);

• computer trespassing (e.g., hacking) resulting in exposure to certain

governmental, credit, financial, or computer-housed information, 18

1030(a)(2);

• damaging a government computer, a bank computer, or a computer used
in, or

affecting, interstate or foreign commerce (e.g., a worm, computer virus,
Trojan

horse, time bomb, a denial of service attack, and other forms of cyber
attack,

cyber crime, or cyber terrorism), 18 U.S.C. 1030(a)(5);

• committing fraud an integral part of which involves unauthorized
access to a

government computer, a bank computer, or a computer used in, or
affecting,

interstate or foreign commerce, 18 U.S.C. 1030(a)(4);

• threatening to damage a government computer, a bank computer, or a
computer

used in, or affecting, interstate or foreign commerce, 18 U.S.C.
1030(a)(7);

• trafficking in passwords for a government computer, or when the
trafficking

affects interstate or foreign commerce, 18 U.S.C. 1030(a)(6); and

• accessing a computer to commit espionage, 18 U.S.C. 1030(a)(1).”

17 - No Man’s Land

This morning, I woke up in a new country. Reggie had begun recording
small sentences and phrases that popped into his head. Anything to help
him grasp what he was doing. These recordings saved in his implant,
presumably locked away in his mind’s eye.

Reggie had not interacted with anyone in over a week. The limited bursts
of communication with Withman did not amount to real contact to him.

The world looks different when you’re turned on your ear.

He could not venture to a store or gas station.

Reggie was underground. 45 feet to be exact. Into the side of a
mountain. Tucked behind the facade of a plain nature reserve.

Only touching a private mountain home community mostly filled with
snowbirds form the north that found a way down into the applications and
ultimately a small crossroad town called Saphaire, North Carolina.

He was held up in his late-grandparent’s cabin in the mountains of North
Carolina - a fifteen hour drive from Washington, DC. He was beginning to
go stir-crazy. The over-the-air television carried stories about the
news published in the Bulletin and recovered and counter-reported by
other agencies. But largely the impact Reggie had hoped for was
dwindling. The concepts of an unblinking eye suveiling every aspect of
one’s daily life was far to grand for the common Man too gasp, it
seemed. Some privacy rights groups had pounced on the revelations as
justification of their Cassandra warnings. Many more sought donations
for lobbying and civil action. Others found weaponization in the reveal
of a deep-state operation that effectively monitored the world.

But that was all over head.

What many failed to see was the willingingness of everyone to sign away
the right to privacy. Reggie recorded his thoughts as he thought about
the news. OUr inalienable rights has been dashed and sold off for access
to free “things.” The thirteenth amendment forbids slavery, but there is
nothing in our body of laws that prevent someone from contracting away
their freedoms. These things and services; these “free lunches.”
Remember, there is no free lunch and when you are eating one; you’re the
lunch.

At this moment, Reggie’s name was more recognizable than the
President’s, that is why he could not set foot back into the real world.
The mountains offered some minor sense of camouflage. And like Thoreau’s
experiment at Walden Pond, Reggie was beginning to regret his self-exile
away for the world. But Thoreau did not have the military industrial
complex wanting the files and secret in his head. Nobody cared about
Walden Pond; everyone wanted Reggie’s mind.

But Reggie was moderately safe here in this tiny mountain town with only
one lighted intersection. People here did not pour over news like in DC.
They often did not hear of any news until they turned on the evening
news or spent a Saturday reading the past week’s local papers. This town
was an island in the lake of information - from which it did not drink
often.

Reggie pre-arranged with Jessica and Trevor to turn on their implants
long range communications into a special secure chat room only for them
at certain randomized times; not to communicate, but rather just to
listen for a distress call - their “coffee-breaks.” It was now one of
those times, Reggie poured a cup and unlocked his implants
communications. It was quiet at first, then the a message appeared.

Foreign press wants in on the dispatches like the Bulletin. Trevor was
the first to break the silence.

Which writer? Jessica questioned. Reggie had now idea if she was there
until she sent the message.

A German paper, the whole paper. They have been pinging a special drop
account I set up. It’s virtually the whole paper and editorial staff.
They want to churn out the information. Trevor was not excited. It
sounded too brash to all three of them.

I don’t like dealing with a whole paper. It’s too many eyes and ears.
Even through small drops of information. Reggie sipped his coffee.

Tell them to pick one. One writer. One Editor. Jessica offered. If they
look good to us, then we can treat the two of them like the Bulletin. We
are not shotgun blasting this information out there. It’s too dangerous
and needs to have sober recollection on what is being released. It can’t
be a puppy-mill.

Well said, Reggie missed Jessica. He did not know where she was, and
that was by design. He suspected Canada, hopefully Quebec - her language
skills could get her safely to France or Africa if they had to leave the
US. Trevor had let slip in a previous session that he was in Europe -
when that happened both Jessica and Reggie immediately broke
communication to avoid tracing or further disclosure.

So what do we do? It was late in the evening as Trevor sat in a dark
room of a hostel in Prague. He had been hopscotching through Eastern
Europe after clearing customs in Berlin. He needed to find a more secure
location soon.

Don’t pursue them. I don’t like the scale. Jessica answered. She looked
out the window at the long grey landscape of Iceland at dusk and hugged
herself, waiting for Reggie to weigh in.

No more contact with unsolicited inquiries. We only go to those we
originally discussed. Reggie poured the rest of his coffee into the
sink. We’ve got twenty years of data stored in our brains. Enough to
kill Baker for it and they’ll kill us too. We must kill them first, but
with a controlled burn. Understood?

Affirmative, will update as needed. Sign off. Trevor clicked off from
the communication link and lit a cigarette as he leaned out the window
and stared at the old-town architecture and city-scape of Prague.

Confirmed, sign off. Jessica clicked off and shut down all her
communication links. The sun never really set in Iceland this time of
year, it just wobbled in the sky and played with the shadows surrounding
her.

Reggie stood in silence in his late-grandparent’s cabin. Alone.
Disconnected. He loved this cabin. It was the only thing he had left of
his grandparents. But it was almost like a Pharaoh's tomb. Nothing had
changed since his grandparents passed away, not even the clothes in the
closets.

They raised him since his mother and father divorced when he was twelve.
His mother dying shortly after thereafter from liver cancer and his
father of lung cancer. One enjoyed alcohol too much and the other worked
too long in an asbestos factory. They spent their summers in the cool
shade of the tall pines. He learned to drive the winding and twisting
mountain roads. He played football and baseball in a small school near
Brevard, North Carolina. His first girlfriend's name was Rhonda - it was
carved into a tree exactly halfway between this cabin and her father’s
house.

All of this were distant memories. Life was no longer simple and Reggie
knew this. He sucked his teeth and stuffed his clothes into his duffle
bag. He had been still too long. His face was too well-known and he was
going to have to go deeper into seclusion. He knew he could not come
back to this cabin. He hoped one day he might be able to, but doubted
the possibility very seriously. He had to leave and drive all night to
get to his next haven.

###

Data never lies. It is a mirror. It tells what is going on where is it
happening and who is creating it.

It notes the time and place when you sneak a few minutes of porn while
in the bathroom at work on your private cellphone. It records your path
when you detour by an old lover’s apartment on you way to pick up the
children from karate class. It even remembers when you looked up
symptoms for gonorrhea in college.

The streams of encrypted communications did not appear to have a real
pattern at first glance. OpOne had always had a fascination with raw
data, he watched it constantly as his operators completed missions and
the world rotated on its magnetic axis. Now, it was more important than
ever to watch the data. There were three people in a sea of seven and
half billion others that had information that could black out OpOne’s
world. He had not slept for two days.

“It’s going to be drops of water in oceans data,” OpOne muttered to
himself. The two other Operators in the control room snored softly in
their chairs. “Short bursts, random times.”

OpOne wiped his hands in the air and cleared away patches and blobs of
data streaming across his eyes that did not match his theory. It fed
into him from the servers and data banks of virtually every corporation
in the world. Cell phone companies. Internet providers. Banks.
Libraries. Cable companies.

Every human being in the world was feeding OpOne with all the
information about virtually every aspect of their lives. Their thoughts
as they tap them into a word document on their laptop. The grocery list
they emailed to their partner. The shows they watch. When their birth
control was about to be refilled. When they last had a prescription for
antibiotics. Their chats via webcam, phonecalls, text messages,
everything was a buffet of data to OpOne. And from the data buffet
patterns emerge. OpOne with predictive algorithms he wrote could even
predict what targets might do.

The cruel joke was not lost on OpOne; these people paid every one of
these corporations to generate this data on them as customers. Willingly
they forked over mounds of cash for the product or service. In return
they opened up the never-blinking eye of convenience and as a
consequence its twin - surveillance. They paid for a service, the
corporation merely noticed then sold it wholesale to the government
which wanted to tap into these pools for intelligence. And the companies
profited handsomely in return.

It was a matter of finding the data he wanted pluck from the vines
flourishing before him.

“It’s going to be longer than text messages and smaller than video
chats. They wouldn’t be stupid enough to put their faces into the data.”
Another layer of data dissolved from his eyes.

“Great, that leaves only half the data.” OpOne sighed and dragged his
hands through his shaggy and unwashed hair. His eyes were red and
bloodshot with fatigue. His mind screamed for rest.

“Randomized. They wouldn’t stick to a regular schedule and they would
only communicate if they had something to say. One would have to have
contacted someone at the Bulletin. Had to be randomized secure chats in
the internet relay chat network, it was the only one that still remained
somewhat encrypted.”

The massive server farms struggled with the loads OpOne was placing on
them. He was also tapping into the processing farms of half of the
Defense industry. It was an immense amount of data to compute.

“Ignore the Middle East. They wouldn’t be that stupid.” OpOne wiped away
a section of the globe spinning before his eyes. “Africa doesn’t have
the digital resources.” Another chunk of the world dropped away.

OpOne stood quietly staring into the rendering of the world’s
communication data.

“Sleep schedules!” OpOne clapped his hands and adjusted a timer
algorithm to the data stream. The other Operators jolted awake from the
sound.

“Short bursts, secured networks, randomized sleep schedules of three
different people on three different connections between roughly a third
of the world.”

The algorithm churned through the data in real-time. This required
power, immense amounts of tremendous power. As it did, the power loads
on the server farms strained the power grids of half the country.
Brownouts were reported across the Northeast and parts of the Midwest -
OpOne wiped away these warning messages in his secure-links with a flick
of his hand as he massaged his temples, trying to stave off another
migraine.

The algorithm returned its analysis and probabilities.

OpOne’s yellowing teeth split his chapped lips as the areas of data
narrowed.

“North Carolina! Western! Near Asheville!”

Robinson watched and heard the report muffle its way through the glass
window of the observation room above the Operating Room. He dispatched a
small team of Operators - it would take them just a few hours to get to
Asheville.

Redraft

Usually when Robinson met with members of Congress the meetings were
discreet and never attended by members of the press. This was not the
case for this meeting. Reporters and congressional staff members crowded
into the biggest hearing room in the Capitol. The whirl of camera
shutters sounded like a chorus of rain sticks as each member spoke and
lamented their outrage and disbelief focused on the recent disclosures
in the press.

Robinson’s medals and brass glinted in the flash of the cameras as he
answered heated questions with answers citing “National Security,”
"Terrorism," and “Defense of Democracy.” It was an unpleasant event and
it was witnessed by more viewers and online streams than any
Congressional event ever broadcast before.

“General Robinson,” Senator Ammerstand leaned forward in his high back
leather chair.

“Lieutenant General, Senator,” Robinson was beginning to show signs of
stress from the public exposure of the hearing.

“What, oh, yes,” Ammerstand’s eyes narrowed. “Lieutenant General
Robinson, where in the scope of National Security do you see the excuse
for invading the homes and privacy of normal everyday persons?”

“Every investigation and…” Robinson began to answer.

“‘Investigation’? Is that what you call it?” Ammerstand interrupted.

“Yes, every investigation conducted by these programs and services were
sanctioned by a specially appointed and authorized judge in a court
proceeding to verify probable cause that these measures were needed in
the interest of national…” Robinson was cut off.

“Yes, yes ‘National Security.’” Spittle began to form at the corners of
Ammerstand’s mouth with veins beginning to bulge out of his forehead.

“But the recent disclosures depict something that is severely more
alarming. And I quote, ‘Project AllSIGHT contains the capability of
remotely controlling web cameras, cellular microphones, and recording
technology built into common household items such as televisions,
electronic devices, printers, and alarm clocks. General, I find it hard
to believe that ‘National Security’ concerns require the U.S.
Government’s Intelligence Services wholesale command and control of
virtually every American’s alarm clock. You, sir, are at the helm of a
wildly out of control ship steering toward a very steep cliff. I hope we
can right this ship and avoid a terrible cost to the constitutional
rights and privileges that have been so viciously attacked by your
command.

“Section 702 of your authorizing statute, General Robinson, states...And
I am quoting. An authorized acquisition authorized under subsection
(a) - (1) may not intentionally target any person known at the time of
acquisition to be located in the United States;

“(2) may not intentionally target a person reasonably believed to be
located outside the United States if the purpose of such acquisition is
to target a particular, known person reasonably believed to be in the
United States;

“(3) may not intentionally target a United States person reasonably
believed to be located outside the United States;

“(4) may not intentionally acquire any communication as to which the
sender and all intended recipients are known at the time of the
acquisition to be located in the United States; and

“(5) shall be conducted in a manner consistent with the fourth amendment
to the Constitution of the United States.”

A dead silence fell across the room.

“General, I ask you, have you ever read the fourth amendment? Much less
these restrictions in the statute? Mr. Chairman, I see that my time has
expired. I yield.”

The hearing ended shortly after that with Chairman Burchart dismissing
Robinson and pounding his gavel against the roar of applause from the
public audience. They refused to stop cheering Senator Ammerstand’s
statement until Robinson was ushered out of the room by a security
detail.

This was just the morning session of a full day of hearings to be held.
As the room cleared and the Senators retreated to their private offices,
Robinson rushed Senator Burchart into a SCIF room.

“Richard, this farce cannot be extended,” Robinson was boiling in his
own skin with rage.

“I know, Jack. I know,” Senator Burchart answered. “But we can’t simply
call off the afternoon session. It’s impossible right now. We’ve got
every news agency and known faction of civil rights group pounding on
the Capitol doors out there. They’ve been protesting up and down
Constitution and Independence Avenue all week!”

“Senator,” Robinson began to calm down, he sensed he was regaining
control of Burchart. “If this is allowed to continue, we will seriously
jeopardize ongoing investigations and operations throughout the world
and seriously put at risk the security of our armed forces.”

“Jack,” Burchart answered. “You can’t expect me to walk out there and
put my face on that pile that you are spouting right now. It’s been said
before and now it has taken root. We shoveled the manure on this plant
long ago. It's sprouting.”

Robinson and Burchart stood face to face in the SCIF room no one could
even hear one of them scream if he did.

Robinson’s arm shot from his side and clutched Burchart by the collar
and throat.

“Listen here, Senator,” Robinson jerked Burchart nose to nose. “You
can’t call off the afternoon hearing, but you can close it. Don’t make
me drop the poison pill it will go sideways faster than you can shit
yourself after a free late-night breakfast buffet at a cheap titty bar.

“We know every dirty trick and skeleton in your heart of hearts. The
‘massage’ sessions you get in your office. The trips on the jets of all
those fat cats whose businesses’ names are on the receiving ends of
those military contracts you push through appropriations.”

Robinson paused and bared his teeth.

“The blowjobs. The hookers. And I’m not just talking about yours. Your
staff is just as dirty. You don’t spend much time in this filthy city
before you end up with dirt on your soul.”

Robinson released Burchart’s collar and jacket from his grasp. Burchart
collapsed into a chair. His skin a pale shade of it’s normal rosy
complexion.

“Close the afternoon session,” Robinson adjusted his uniform. “And put a
muzzle on your minority leader.”

An hour later, the audience from the morning session began to shuffle
into the Capitol only to discover Capitol Hill Police guarding the
entrances into the hearing room. A placard read, “Hearing Classified.
Closed to Public.”

“What do you mean, you can’t locate him!” Robinson screamed into the
empty back seat of his town car. His driver tapped a button from the
front seat and raised a privacy barrier. Robinson’s towncar was a mobile
SCIF, with only a highly secured uplink directly back to Vision Systems.

“I mean, he’s gone.” OpOne had sincerely hoped to get more than two
hours of sleep after narrowing down the only stateside location of the
three targets. “They must have a pattern or rule about moving once they
communicate. They have to know we will try to track them. I mean they
have our fucking files! Our Playbook!”

“Shut up and don’t give me excuses,” Robinson gulped a glass of scotch
down. “I don’t give you full access to the infrastructure of this
country and virtually all the processing power of the Intelligence
Community just for a team of Operators to show up to an empty cabin in
North Carolina with their dicks in their hands!”

He could hear OpOne sigh on the other end of the connection.

“Find him and alert our foreign friends to the last known whereabouts of
his fellow traitors. Catching one of those fish will draw out the
leader.”

Reggie heard the news and knew what it meant to have a scheduled public
hearing suddenly closed or relabeled as classified. He was currently
pulled over in a truck stop in Indiana near Fort Wayne.

He flipped open his cellphone after snapping the battery into it. Three
messages appeared. All from the same number and all requesting a chat
session. He responded with a string of numbers to a new secure chat
session and flip up his laptop.

After a moment of waiting, Withman’s unique identifier enter the
chatroom.

You heard about the hearing today? Withman asked.

Yeah, from AM Radio, but, yeah, I got the gist of it after filtering out
the conservative spin against the protesters. Reggie answered.

Well, it’s not over with. They are still protesting the Capitol. In
fact, it’s spreading across the town. K Street is virtually impassable.
If you do have something for me, my editor is going to send me out to
cover the White House protests.

Don’t leave just yet. Downloading now. Vision kept secret files on every
member of Congress and their staff. I’m sending you the files on the
members of the committee from today. I’m also sending you Rep. Baker’s
file.

Receiving now. Withman toggled to the download screen and saw the files
filling his hard drive.

I am leaving what is publishable to your discretion. I am not going to
control the information. It was put in my lap for protection not
censorship. Even Baker’s File.

Withman clicked on Representative Baker’s file. His eyes widened at the
information populating his screen.

This is pretty damaging stuff. Withman typed into the chat window.

You can’t defame a dead man. Reggie answered and waited for Withman to
respond.

Yeah, but you’re not dead. There’s stuff on you in here. I mean it’s not
give your mother a heart attack stuff, but it’s not something you want
every Tom, Dick, and Harry to know about. You sure you want this in the
wild? They are going to use it against you.

After a short pause, Reggie’s fingers began to tap the keyboard. My
mother is dead. Dad too. No living relatives. No one to disappoint.
You’ve got two stories there to consider.

One, what Vision is doing to members of Congress to intimidate them into
compliance. And Two, what they had on a member of Congress who had long
suspected they were breaking the law and what they did to silence him. I
leave the discretion up to you.

Withman sighed and responded. That doesn’t cover what’s in here about
you. This isn’t you got kicked out of the boy scouts kind of stuff. You
probably shouldn’t have had a security clearance in the first place.

Reggie answered almost immediately. I know, but I did have a security
clearance and Baker believed in a young kid from a fourth-tier law
school who showed up on his doorstep with nothing but a law degree, a
checkered past, and mountain of student loan debt. That man was a father
to me. He believed in me and I believed in him. He wants this told and
exposed. He deserves to have his last will and testament read publicly.

Withman watched the cursor blink on his screen for a couple of seconds
to make sure Reggie was finished. I understand. But I have one problem
with it all...I’m supposed to be the writer. And yes, I’m going to steal
that last line.

Reggie chuckled as rain began to tap the windshield of his car. You can
borrow it. I don’t even care if you quote me on it.

"Sometimes chaos is the best strategy for the long game," Reggie lined
up his shot on the pool table and projected the cue ball toward a
striped ball.

"Chaos only gets you so far," OpOne took a sip of beer.

Pool balls ricocheted off each other shattering into multiple random
angles, bouncing off of bumpers and fellow variables.

Cigarette smoke hung in the air.

"We don't need to ignore the common ground between us," Reggie studied
the pool table.

"No, no we don't," OpOne freshened the chalk on his cue.

"We both have shit in our heads that could crumble the very ground under
our feet." Reggie paused to sip from his beer.

"Yeah, and they know it." OpOne resisted the urge to analyze the angles
of the balls on the table with his implants.

"We don't need to advertise it either," Reggie raised an eyebrow. "I
sincerely hope you aren't broadcasting anything."

"I ain't that stupid, Reggie."

“So, where does this end?” Reggie paused and leaned on his pool cue.

“Depends,” OpOne shrugged. “Where do we want it to end?”

“So we both agree for whatever purpose this shit was designed, it has
gone too far.”

“Agreed,” OpOne turned to his beer and gulped down a mouthful.

“So we pool our resources,” Reggie joined OpOne at the high-top table
and slurped his beer.

“I’ve got the access, do you have the horsepower?”

“I’ve got virtually every script-kiddie and hacker in the known world
trying to ping me on every channel. I turned on my comms for a minute
and just barely shook off the migraine that ensued.”

OpOne paused as his eyes narrowed. “First comes the flood. Then the
Fire. Then the Ashes.”

“Scorched earth policy?” Reggie was hesitant. He knew the whole of the
world’s superpowers would turn on them once it began.

“There is no other way to take it all down. So people will have to live
without a few things for a while. But we just might be able to wake them
all up.” OpOne sucked his teeth.

“They’ve all been asleep for too long,” Reggie sighed. “Most of all our
leaders.”

“If we do it right, they won’t be our leaders anymore. They’ve been
asleep at the wheel for far too long.” OpOne’s head sunk between his
shoulders. “I’ve seen it, nurtured it, capitalized on it.”

“Don’t apologize for genius.” Reggie sipped his half-full beer and wiped
some foam from his newborn beard. “We should recognize that we are
trying to do something right.”

They paused their conversation. Reggie returned to the pool table and
took a shot at the 7-ball. It deflected wildly and bounced off two balls
and a bumper.

“We open the floodgates. Release codes and access points,” OpOne eyed
the balls on the table.

“But we don’t attack the things that society needs. Power grids.
Hospitals. Traffic systems.”

OpOne look at Reggie incredulously. “Of course.”

The room around Reggie and OpOne stuttered and blinked to a halt. The
pool table dissolved into squared bits resembling sand between them. The
music sputtered and then stopped.

“We should really do this again, I had fun,” OpOne said as his pool cue
disappeared in his hand.

“Definitely not like the pool halls I usually trespass,” Reggie said as
his image vanished.

OpOne flickered and disappeared.

After a moment of peaceful silence, Reggie sighed and stood up. His bar
stool flickered and dematerialized.

“What do you think?” Reggie asked aloud.

The moment paused as the question flew across fiber optic cable into the
Jessica’s and Trevor’s implants.

but the body count.”

Trevro was at his most vulnerable when he talked about Afganistan. He
lost more than friends, brother’s, even a lover by chance of encourtere,
but it was the dishonor he saw on thebattlefield and in the War rooms,
th eplanning, matrix, the computer simulations, the abject reduction of
human conflict into a madness of simulated potentials of gunship battles
versus bombarmentd and the exposure of certain potliucal senticvies to
late new coverage of massive mutilmillion dollar missiles being cast
like pennies at the moutains that thwarted, yeased, and welped
Alexander, “the Great.”

“Trevor, we can’t fight this from the outside. We need a fifth column.”
Jessica hated the words as she spoke them from her secured location. The
dark room was small, more coffin than closet. “We need him on the inside
as we attack the four walls with the hacker groups, data leaks, and
political pressure. No one else can feed the information out to us or
undermine what they do internally. And yes, I know he built the shit in
our heads. Trust me I’d love to be able to tear this out of my head and
live the rest of my existence half-blind and ignorant, but that isn’t
the case. We’ve been given the gift of fire. That bell is never going to
stop ringing in my brain.”

“Fine! He can eat your fucking liver! Because he’s a goddamn vulture
opportunist who is going to stab us all in the back and fuck us long,
slow, deep, and hard.” Trevor clicked off the channel.

“Well, we have a majority, I guess.” Reggie sighed.

Jessica laughed, “this isn’t a democracy, Reggie. This never has been. I
got this shit implanted in my head out of necessity. It was part of my
mission. I was to track, record, surveil, and report. Counter-Operations
was never my mission. I went rogue. Trevor did too. I didn’t know he did
until I got the ping from him during a sensory deprivation exercise.”

Reggie did not dare interrupt Jessica right now, he knew she had to get
this out of her if there was to be any progress for this conversation.

“They buried me, Reggie. In the goddamn ground naked with a tank of
oxygen and fucking gas mask strapped to my head. They turned off every
sensor and communication I had via this thing wrapped around my ocular
nerve and shut me off for three days.

“Right when I had just about enough of it and thought about trying to
claw my way out through eight feet of earth on top of me, I heard
Trevor’s voice. Clear as if he was sitting next to me. He was sobbing
and mumbling curses and last names. Then the images flickered into the
backs of my eyes. I saw the aftermath of exactly what he just described
to you. That really happened to him and his unit in Afghanistan and this
country through the very assholes we are shaking hands with right now
did it to him.

“These demagogues are playing both sides, just like when they sent boxes
of cash to Iraq while bombing the Kurds in the north. I saw the blood of
my brother’s best friends staining his hands and saw his vision blur
with his own tears. These monsters can’t be tamed, but they can be
killed.”

Reggie dared not speak or try to respond.

“I don’t like it. But fuck you for making me do it.” Jessica wiped her
eyes and gasped alone in the blackness. “I’ll kill this fucker if he
dares double-cross us. Do it.”

The transmission clicked off. Reggie’s vision focused back to the
spartan cabin he found himself alone, so connected but alone.

###

“What is going on?” An Operator's eye screens flashed and whirred with
data and alerts. Lights dimmed in the large orb-room. The observation
decks looming over the Operator station flickered. The climate controls
clicked on and off randomly. Access pads flashed red-green-red-green -
stuck in a chaotic cycle.

“We’re under attack,” Robinson’s knuckles whitened under his dark skin.
“Fucking hackers and terrorists.”

This was not an attack that you see in movies. It was not artillery
being fired in from a ship in the channel. It was not the taking of
Omaha beach. No bullets zipping by soldier ears. No gore and carnage
blasting up from the sand. It was a digital attack. Focused on
motherboards and server farms, while overheating fiber-optic wiring with
repeated commands meant to drive up CPU cycles to melt the wiring render
the machines inert and useless, effectively destroying hundreds of
millions of dollars in computer networks and data. This was in every
aspect the biggest and most comprehensive denial of service attack ever
attempted. A denial of service - or DDOS - attack attempts to overload
the capacity of a computer several or network by randomly sending
requests and and information. The capacity of the network and ability of
the servers to process the information and renders the machines
unresponsive and useless for the education for the attack.

“We can’t isolate the sources of these attacks. It’s everything in the
book. Denial of Service attacks. IP pings. Power grid fluctuations. It’s
everything and all things in and out of the networks.” An Operator
shouted toward Robinson from the floor of the Operator’s room.

In his ears, Robinson heard a muffled and shouted version of the
Operator; one from his secure-link and the other through the glass.

Robinson was a pillar of stone, but his mind raced with probabilities
and strategies. It’s every angle. It’s every option in an out. They are
throwing every play in the book at us with infinite players. It’s a new
steel. A game changer. His mind flashed to an old history lesson from
college. A new kind of steel emerged in Scandinavia. Forged at higher
temperatures and creating a steel that dominated the battlefield until
the Industrial Revolution. Ulfberht swords. Named for the inscription
along the blade. It was branded there and made a daunting impression on
the adversaries of its holder.

Another lesson streaked across Robinson’s mind, amid the chaos
surrounding him. When faced with an insurmountable force or out matched
weapon, it is often the best option to withdraw and vanish.

“Operator,” Robinson calmly spoke to his chief Operator whose eyes were
wild with anxiety. “Shut it down. Take it offline. All of it.”

The Operator stopped dead in his tracks and shot his eyes toward
Robinson from the Operator’s Room.

“That’s an order, Operator,” Robinson eased his stance, relaxed his
shoulders, and cupped his hands together behind his back - in an at-ease
military stance.

The Operator charged out of the Operator’s Room and into the hallway. He
broke into a run toward the central elevator axis. Once there, he
pressed the down button repeatedly. The elevator door opened and then
shut, then opened halfway and shut again. He pressed through the
malfunctioning elevator doors and slammed through the doors to the the
emergency stairs. He descended down toward the server farms and into a
secured room because of the hacks the electronic door was locking and
unlocking rapidly. Timing a shoulder push in-between the locks locking
and unlocking the operator found his way to the center of the servers
farm room with it's long aisles of tall cabinet-sized servers.

He yanked the door opened hard to find a terrified security guard with
his sidearm shaking in his right hand.

With his hands up, the Operator shouted, “Don’t shoot! Orders from
Command!”

Suddenly, a shrieking signal shot through their secure-links. They are
in the secure-links. Fuck!

The security guard dropped his sidearm to the floor, grabbed his head
and blood began to trickle from his nose.

Despite the shrieking pain in his head, The Operator rushed toward a
long tube glowing blue with heat and electricity. It was three feet in
diameter. The backbone of the globally connected world. It was the
doorway from and to everywhere Vision Systems’ networks reached. If it
could not be fail-safed it had to be cut - physically.

A hand scanner blinked green-red-green-red next to the control panel for
the glowing tube. The monitor blinked and displayed garbled text and
pornographic images.

Another jolt of the mind-wrenching signal shot through his secure-link.
No other option.

The Operator grasped a lever alongside the glowing tube, bit his lip,
and yanked hard.

Two rings of explosives around both ends of the tube fired off in series
of quick small and controlled explosions. The glow ceased with sparks
and fizzles.

Immeadiately, half the world went dark.

Street lights stopped, flashed caution and reset.

Trains sputtered on their tracks and then resumed the travel.

Radar screens of airports flickered - switched off like old televisions
and snapped back on.

Power grids flickered and rebooted.

For a brief moment, the world was deathly quiet.

Blood dripped from the Operator’s nose and tapped his shoe. The smell of
sulfur and copper hung in the air - like a ghost.

For a brief moment, the Operator’s mind was quiet and still. There was
no buzzing message in the corner of his mind. His heart beat in his
chest. He felt the pressure of his weight on the pads of his feet inside
his shoes. The weight of his arms pulling on his shoulders. It was a
brief reprieve from the electric pulse of his world.

Vision System’s computers and internal networks began to come back
online. The internal communications triggered and his secure-link
flooded with messages from other Operators and Robinson on the isolated
internal networks.

Tears began to roll down his cheeks as the deafness of disconnection
left his senses.

###

The electronic stock boards flickered and sputtered as the quotes
displayed in garbled and strange symbols. Cryptic lettering replaced
numerals. The floor traders froze paused from their normal frenzy of
buying and selling based on the rise and tumble of the boards quotes and
trends.

Suddenly the lights blacked out and red emergency flood lights
illuminated the exits.

A few shrieks escaped weak lips and throats. But the glow of handheld
datapads allowed the traders to see in the dark.

What was truly terrifying was the data. It was stopped. Frozen. Broken.
In the stock market, data is money. Without it, there is no wealth.

The servers froze. Halted. A virus inserted in a single trade infected
the backbone of the stock market. It was successful. In an instant,
trillions of dollars in wealth disappeared.

The datapads in the hands of the floor traders were little more than
overpriced flashlights.

Panic flooded the stock building. Filling it to the brim,sucking the
oxygen out of the room like a vacuum.

The panic shot out of the room through the news cameras filming their
midday market reports.

The attack was perfectly timed. It's effect was maximized with every
breath taken while surrounded by darkness. The world shrugged and
sputtered.

Just as the news anchors caught their bearing on what was occurring.
Their producers shouted into their earpieces about the effects of the
blackout and rumors of the virus attacking the world’s leading stock
markets.

The reports of the network crash and the tremendous loss of wealth
flashed across almost every television and computer screen in the world.

For fifteen minutes, the reporters and anchors repeated the keywords fed
into their ears by the little clear wires snaking up from their shirt
collars.

After ten minutes, reports of bodies falling from skyscrapers in
Manhattan began to spark on social media. Pictures followed shortly
thereafter. Bloody pools surrounding crumped shattered shells of flesh,
bone, and meat.

Gunshots sounded in normally quiet upscale neighborhoods tucked into the
most expensive zip codes of the United States. Scenes of desperation
fueled by blind reaction awaited the next unfortunate person to enter
those rooms.

Fires burst out of car windows as glass store fronts shattered from the
impact of bricks thrown through them. The goods lining the shelves were
soon missing and likely found for a severely reduced sale price on a
darker market.

Less than 144 lines of computer code inserted through a routine stock
trade succeeded in setting the world on fire.

Then, the lights came back on in the trading room. The servers roared
back to life. The wealth - thought lost - filled the digital account
books across the world.

The "glitch" was widely reported. It was blamed for the widespread panic
that covered the world. The "glitch" was blamed for the suicides,
rioting, and looting. It was not a glitch.

In a bunker, tucked behind yards of concrete and steel, a series of
monitors filled with code and signal quietly watched the impact of the
virus. The effects catalogued and indexed for an after-operation report.
One monitor did not change, but it's code remained stagnant. This code
was the virus. This room was the launchpad from which this disaster
sprang.

"The after report should take about an hour to compile," an Operator
leaning back into his chair said into his headset.

"Confirmed," a voice answered in his ear. "Continue monitoring and
supplement as needed."

Robinson blinked away the communications link. He called up a national
security transmittal he had prepared prior to launching this operation.
It was filled with responses meant to paint hackers and terrorists
responsible for the attack on the stock market. It was well received
because it was easy to accept. The news networks repeated, nearly
verbatim.

Soon the world was terrified of the black-faced hackers and bloodthirsty
terrorists that caused the stock market to crash, and were to blame for
all the horribles that followed.

The outcry was deafening across every avenue of expression available.
Congress had banded together on the steps of the Capitol earlier that
afternoon and sung the National Anthem. At nine that evening, the
President issued an executive order from the Oval Office while sitting
at his desk. The country's highest elected official stared into the
television camera with his graying hair and tired, but steady eyes and
wiped away every constitutional right held by every person under the
protection of the Constitution.

He issued an executive order declaring martial law within 50 miles of
every national security facility under control of the United States.
This effectively opened an umbrella over the heads of virtually every
corner of the world.

His calm voice washed over the world. The vast majority of the hearts
and minds of his people felt reassured. They felt calm at the end of a
truly violent and horrible day. He did not promise one hundred
protection, he only promised it would not happen again under his watch.

The world thanked him as tired eyes eased into restful sleep.

They did not know their lives were no longer theirs. Their homes no
longer secure against intrusion. Their bank accounts no longer respected
as private. Their minds and thoughts no longer their own. Virtually
every particulate of their lives were now opened fresh and unprotected
from the unblinking stare of what was no longer their government but
rather their worst enemy.

The world awoke the next morning to tanks at street corners and the
freedoms it once held became like sand in the grip of a clenched fist.
