###

Senator Ammerstand had not left his office since the President's decree.
He and his staff had been working around the clock and sleeping in
shifts. They had been researching and fact-checking the documents and
reports in the press and released by the mysterious whistleblower.

The offices were covered with paper and filled with the roar of
typewriters. Mountains of legal pads littered the desks along with empty
cups of coffee.

A packet of documents had been delivered to Senator Ammerstand late last
night. With a clear note handed written on the outside: “Organic Eyes
Only - No Secure-link.”

The documents detailed a number of classified operations involving
Vision Systems. Operation reports filled with details that turned the
Senator’s blood cold.

The Senator was beginning to realize all the years of classified
briefings were half-truths filled with omissions. The information was
true but the application was never fleshed out. A dark world unfolded
before him in the reports with tentacles wrapped around the throats of
nearly every person, corporation, and government entity around the
globe.

"What did they do wrong?" The Senator muttered at his desk. His aide
paused from his note taking. It had been three days since martial law
had been ordered. "Tim? What did they do wrong? What rule did they
break?"

The Senator paused and looked at his befuddled aide. His eyes bloodshot.
His collar unbuttoned; tie loosened.

"The law." Tim's brain was not wired to work on less than four hours
sleep taken on an army surplus cot in a supply room.

"It's not broken if the law allowed the conduct," the Senator a lifted a
copy of the United States Code from his desk and tossed it to an empty
space between him and Tim, landing with a heavy thud.

"Was it wrong? Yes. Was it allowed? Yes. We allowed the conduct. Gave
them carte blanche to train every available intelligence weapon against
the citizenry and persons they swore an oath to protect, all in the
guise of protecting those from themselves. They even put a goddamn bug
in the brains and eyes of Congress." The Senator pointed to his own
right eye.

The morning the newspapers reported the surveillance leaks, the Senator
had his staffs' Secure-links disabled by their tech officer. Then the
computers were unplugged and typewriters were brought up from storage.
Only a handful of computers were allowed to remain on and only one
connected to the Internet; all had any web camera lens blocked with an
opaque post-it note. A small sneaker-network sprang up in the Senator’s
office and while many of his Staffers and Aides agonized over the
technological reversion in the office, but from the information they
read, studied, and researched their blood ran cold and stomachs twist as
they realized their own eyes had been made to spy on their every move.

"It shall be the right of the people to be secure in their person,
places, papers, and effects," the Senator said. "And no warrants shall
issue, but upon probable cause." He finished the half quote of the
Fourth Amendment. Tim nodded and noticed his desk copy of the
Declaration of Independence and Constitution teetering on the edge of
his desk.

"The Founding Fathers thought this was important. They put it forth on
their list of things to keep sacred," the Senator stood from his desk
chair and straightened his tie.

The roar of work in the senator’s office dulled as a hush settled as the
Senator rushed out into the hallway toward the exit of the building.

"Wait! Senator!" Tim chased after Senator Ammerstand. "They may arrest
you!"

Outside the Senate office building an army tank sat in the intersection
of First Street and Constitution Avenue. Soldiers with assault rifles
stood at the ready circled around the tank. Fifty feet from the steps of
the Senate Office building a corral was set up and held reporters with
their cameras.

Senator Ammerstand burst out of the double doors of the building. The
soldiers startled to attention. The audible sound of safeties clicking
off was heard. A gasp registered from the throats of some of the press.
Cameras powered up, focused on the gated Senator, and began to record.

The Senator approached the soldiers. His shoes tapping on the concrete
in the stillness of the afternoon.

"Good afternoon, Gentlemen. Where y'all from?" The Senator stopped
twenty feet from the soldiers surrounding the tank.

"Ohio," a lone voice answered.

"Cincy or Cleveland?" the Senator asked.

"Go Browns!" Another voice chimed in and a chuckle eased over the
soldiers.

"Can we talk candidly, Gentlemen?" The Senator unbuttoned his suit
jacket and tucked a hand into his pant pocket while gesturing with his
free hand. "I understand why you are here. I understand the orders you
are following. I served.” He tapped a lapel pin with the Marine Corps
Eagle, Globe, and Anchor with his free hand over his heart and just
below his Senate lapel pin. “I know the oath you took. I took a similar
oath. And added onto that one with the one I took in honor of my current
position. Not to mention to serve and protect our common employer."

He paused and gestured toward the television reporters and cameras.

"Now, I understand we’re under orders right now. You have your’s from
your Commanding Officer, who got them from his Commanding Officer, and
so on up the chain. I have my orders too.”

Senator Ammerstand softened the glare he shot from his eyes and eased a
kind smile across his face.

“I'm going to ask you to ignore those orders for the next twenty
minutes. Will you do that for me, not as a Senator, not as a Marine, not
even as a Patriot, but as a fellow citizen?"

Silence shrouded the group.

"Gentlemen, I will take that as a ‘yes,’"

The Senator turned from the soldiers and approached the corral of
reporters.

Shouts of questions began to pepper the Senator. He ignored them, raised
a hand to quiet the shouts, and pulled open part of the corral allowing
the reporters to move about freely.

"Please follow me," the Senator asked the reporters.

He crossed in front of the soldiers. "Gentlemen, would you also join us?
I’m not giving you any orders. I don’t hold that power. I’m just asking
for company. Please walk with me."

The Senator walked toward the east face of the Capitol. His aides and
staff had crowded at the entrance of the office building.

A corporal flanked the Senator to his left. A reporter from the Bulletin
paced them on the Senator’s right side.

"Son, what is your name?" the Senator asked as he walked toward the
Capitol.

"Jackson, Vance Jackson," the soldier answered. The reporter was
furiously scribbling notes as he kept pace. "My friends and family call
me Van."

"May I call you Van?" the Senator asked.

"Yes, sir."

"Please call me, Chuck."

Van nodded affirmingly and smiled.

They continued until the Capitol was less than 75 yards in front of the
Senator. It was a good backdrop for the cameras. The sun was low in the
sky and the golden hour was just beginning. The marble steps of the
United States Capitol raised the tall Senator above the crowd. He
stopped his advance and spun around. To one side the reporters had
followed. To the other flank the soldiers had followed.

The Senator paused and smiled.

"I speak to you today not as a Senator..."

The broadcast carried over the news networks. The whole march had been
picked up as it proceeded. In direct violation of the order imposing
martial law issued three days ago the national news networks were
broadcasting. It was currently being watched by more than ten million
viewers; rebroadcast on both the Internet and via Virtual Private
Networks and recast on countless websites and media platforms. .

"Our country is at a crossroads. We are facing an unprecedented
challenge that we cannot ignore. I am not here to lay blame at the
threshold of the Whitehouse. Nor am I here to accept blame on behalf of
the Senate and the House. All are equally to blame for the state of this
great nation at this moment."

He paused, Senator Ammerstand was previously not only a Corporal in the
Marine Corps, but also a trial lawyer and understood the importance of
timing when giving an argument.

"As your leaders we are shouldered with the burden and honor of
safeguarding the rights and liberties of persons protected by the
Constitution. We have failed in that responsibility. This is a dreadful
failure in the course of my service, under my command, and the blame
falling squarely on my shoulders."

"The protection of this republic is one that is a great burden, worthy
of only the most honorable of our neighbors. Corporal Vance Jackson is
one of those honorable men. He and I shared a walk this afternoon and
I'm proud to consider him a friend."

The Corporal nervously smiled as the Senator patted him on the back.

"But that's not why I'm here today, nor is it the reason he is here
today. We stand here this afternoon because of an order issued through
the office of the President of the United States. To that order, I, nor
he, challenged the authenticity of the order or the veracity of the
threats to our great nation which warranted the extreme action buried
within that order. But we should challenge the need of this drastic
measure when there is compelling evidence that we have hobbled this
nation by our own design."

Camera shutters whirred with the pause of the Senator’s speech.

"We have allowed one of the sentinels of this nation too much slack on
its leash and it has encircled us. Every person hearing my words today
is subject to the unlawful search and seizure of their person, papers,
places, and effects. This long honored principle has been embedded in
the very DNA of our country since its inception. 'No warrants shall
issue, but upon probable cause.' Frankly, we have tossed away even the
need for warrants. We scrubbed away those words when we as your leaders
ignored the bill of rights and offered the vile of perceived safety to
you, the public. We passed the operative acts and laws allowing for this
intrusion of your privacy whole cloth and without question. We promised
to safeguard the Constitution when we accepted the duties of our
service. For that I apologize. For that dereliction of duty, I am
ashamed. For the willful ignorance of the common mission we all share in
providing for and sheltering the inalienable rights given by our
creators, I have failed you, my fellow Americans."

He paused and turned to the Corporal to his right.

"Van? I owe you an apology and ask forgiveness." The Senator’s right
hand lowered from the Corporal’s shoulder and opened seeking redemption.
The Corporal's right hand loosened from the grip of his rifle and took
the Senator's hand.

The Senator’s eyes focused on the faces that followed down the line
behind the Corporals' and seemed to request forgiveness from each
soldier. His gaze stopped when he locked onto the lenses of the video
cameras.

"Finally, I ask my fellow Americans for their forgiveness. I refuse to
continue not serving my fellow Americans as I have over the past 72
hours. I ask that my fellow Congressmen and Congresswomen join me in the
immediate repeal the current order imposing martial law and not stop
until we root out the terrible violations of our Constitution that have
occurred thus far. Thank you."

The Corporal's hand slipped from the Senator's as he spun on his heels
and walked toward the Capitol steps. He slipped between a barricade and
past more soldiers and tactical police. He proceeded up the eastern
steps of the Capitol and entered the building.

The news networks carried every minute of the impromptu news conference
and apology of the Senator.

Tim waited for the Senator at the entrance to the underground subway
between the Capitol and the Senate office buildings.

The Senator was visibly pale and his collar was unbuttoned.

"I can't believe you did that, Sir!" Tim was dumbfounded.

"It had to be done. You break up a log jam with a stick and pressure."

"How did you know that would work?" Tim ushered the Senator on to the
automated subway car.

"I didn't. I just hoped everyone still remembered they were American."

The subway car clicked and trundled through the tunnel toward the Senate
office building.

"Was it only for show?" The question shot out of Tim's mouth.

"No, that was the easy part. Now we have to marshal our friends and
enemies to do the right thing."

###

Life slowly returned to Washington, D.C. For three days tanks and
soldiers blocked every avenue of ingress and egress throughout the city.
The Army and National Guard receded back to their bases. Slowly the news
reported everyday online and broadcast that the world was getting back
to normal.

Robinson tapped his fingers on the tabletop as the coffee steamed in a
cup before him.

The meeting was set through unconventional channels. Senator Ammerstand
was a half hour late, on purpose.

A folder lay on the table with a seal marked "Top Secret."

Ammerstand crossed the windows and entered the front door of the diner.
It was known as the Silver Dollar. Known for greasy patty melts and
crispy hash browns for nearly forty years. Posters of classic movies
lined the walls.

Robinson didn't acknowledge the Senator but continued to stare out the
windows.

"General? I trust you haven't ordered yet?" Ammerstand tried to make
small talk.

"Just coffee, Senator," Robinson patted the folder. "I asked to speak
with you because I wanted to let you know about a little information
that I discovered. I wanted to make you aware it was out there in case
it is revealed to harm your efforts."

"You suck at blackmail."

Robinson scoffed as he sipped his coffee. "Why do you say that?"

"Because I know my life, General. I know what your prop there, has on
me. Nice effect with the seal, by the way. Looks good and official."

"So you don't want to see it?" Robinson growled.

"I know what it says. I know that my wife had an abortion while we were
in college together. We were kids, we weren't ready to have children.
She knows I slept with my best friend’s girlfriend during a time that my
wife and I were separated. It was a moment of weakness. I've discussed
this with my wife, we went to counseling. We have promised ourselves to
each other and renewed our lives together. Oh, I smoked a lot of pot in
high school. I mean A LOT! Even owned a VHS of a Cheech and CHong movie.
Wore that thing out, I smoked and watched it so much. Anything else?"

Robinson slashed the seal on the folder open with a butter knife and
opened the folder.

"What about Timothy Rogers?" Robinson asked.

"He's my personal aide and has worked for me since I first practiced
law. I'm sure he has secrets. Those are his own. Those are what makes
him human. I've alerted my whole staff to clean their closets and go to
confession. And yes, I know Tim's gay. I also know he was dishonorably
discharged from the Air Force for striking an officer. What's missing
from your little folder there is the officer was his lover and the fight
was about whether to re-up their commissions together and at the same
base.

"But all of that did not and has not impeded his ability to work in my
law office and he has not failed in his duties during the course of his
service to this country while I have been a Senator.

"He's also very good with kids. My kids call him, Uncle Timmy."

Robinson closed his folder and took a sip from his coffee. His stare was
cold.

"We have a great opportunity for us to bury the hatchet," Robinson said.

"That's where the problem lies, General." Ammerstand shifted in his seat
as he adjusted his jacket. "There should have never been a hatchet. We
are supposed to work together, but you wanted something more. That pesky
bill of rights just got in the way. So, with a little smoke and fire
along with some clever orchestrations of actual terrorism you supplanted
the very string of rights and protections right out from under all of
us. Hell, we helped you wrap the body in towels and rugs as you dragged
it out the back door."

"Senator, the wild language isn't needed," Robinson waived off the
waitress from refilling his coffee and to keep their conversation quiet.

"Don't you dare tell me what is and isn't needed. I'm telling you,
General. You've got three days to resign. Three. I'm not going to unpack
a folder of your personal life in front of a committee hearing or on the
news, web, or telegraph!"

Ammerstand realized his voice was rising and reset his tone.

"I don't care what you do, but it will never be in relation to a
government operation for surveillance, data, National Security. You'll
retire. You'll be comfortable. Learn fly fishing. Go to your cabin in
Michigan. You haven't been there in a while."

Robinson's eyebrow raised, he did not expect Ammerstand to know about
his cabin.

"You've served, General. You've served your time and you'll be honored
by the public. We don't want a bloody coup. We don't want legacy to be
tarnished any further. The public doesn't need that and we need to move
forward. But we need to remember the mistakes. There won't be blame."

Robinson motioned for the waitress. Her footsteps tapped on the linoleum
floor.

"Miss, thank you, but my friend and I have to cut our brunch short
today. What do I owe you for the coffee?"

"Not a problem, Sir. Here's your check," she ripped it from her pad and
placed it on the table.

Her footsteps retreated.

"I suppose to make sure this couldn't be considered a bribe, I should
pay for my own coffee," Robinson pulled his wallet out and flipped a
five dollar bill on to the check. "Senator? I wish you a good day."

Ammerstand sighed then sucked his teeth.

Robinson stood and whipped his jacket around his shoulders.

"Oh and Senator, so that there is no misunderstanding. I will not go
quietly into the night. And you should reconsider your stock portfolio,"
Robinson leaned closer to Ammerstand's ear. "It's not just the true lies
we know about, but also the lies we create, command, and control."

Robinson stood up straight. "Give my best to your wife. She is looking
better since rehab."

Senator Burchart was sitting down when his secure-link chimed with a
download link from Robinson. Or who he thought was Robinson.

It began with a relatively friendly note.

Dear Senator, we hope this finds you well. We also hope that this may
help you fully comprehend the terror ordinary people feel everyday.
Sincerely, the other side of the pillow.

The documents fluttered into Burchart’s secure-link. A parade of
horribles unfolded.

A note at the end was a video file with a digitally scrambled voice. It
auto-played.

Senator, thank you for attending this episode of "This is Your Shit." We
hope it has been enlightening. We understand that your are not the
devil. We understand you are human, as are we. We trust you are thinking
right now. This information was not cobbled together by us. But rather
it was taken from a close friend of yours. It was part of a poison pill
program that would have released to the public upon a number of
different events.

The video paused.

We trust these events will occur so we have taken the liberty to replace
this payload with something less damaging. We are not asking for favors,
we are asking for your re-dedication to the oath you took when you chose
public service and became a Senator. We trust you to do the right thing.
We trust you will still be able to recognize the right thing. This
package has been backed up just in case you struggle with your morality.

The video shuddered and reset. The shape of the figure changed and the
voice pitch returned higher.

Senator, you are now in possession of an additional selection of
documents.

A document package pinged in Burchart’s secure-link.

This was cobbled together from black files and countermeasure protocols
that monitored your office and committees. There are numerous emails and
communications. But more importantly, there are drafts of a special
report of intelligence oversight and one investigation regarding
interrogation tactics engaged in by black-ops under the direction of the
CIA. This was a set of documents that was of particular interest to
General Robinson. Also included are transmissions and download logs from
when he allowed other arms of the intelligence community to access your
networks and computers.

We trust you will recognize the importance of these documents and the
moral conundrum it places your office in. We trust you will make the
right choice, Senator Burchart.

The video clicked off and the documents unfolded in Burchart’s heads up
display. The dirty laundry of his whole life polluted his vision.

The choice was difficult, but not difficult to make.

21 - Contact

Jessica had not heard from either Reggie or Trevor in days. But she had
seen the world melting down in the news. The stock market hacks were of
particular interest. The media continued to blame the nefarious elements
in the digital underworld.

"It's easy to blame the mute," Jessica said as she curled up on a small
couch watching the news from the Pirate Political Party's Headquarters
in Iceland.

The news droned further with talking heads metaphorically attempting to
pour oil over the barricades against the barbarians at the gate.

A ping alerted in Jessica's secure-link. Nobody should be able to ping
her. She had reprogrammed her link.

Again, the ping triggered behind her eye.

It couldn't be Reggie. Trevor would know better than to try in this
form.

Ping.

She returned the ping.

A request for a comm-link opened. She opened a link after bouncing the
connection through a number of firewalls and across a handful of
continents.

"That wasn't necessary," the voice on the other end of the connection
said.

"I'll be the judge of that," Jessica answered.

"I pinged you. I knew where you were before you knew who pinged you."

"Okay, you've made your point," Jessica sighed, "What do you want?"

"I'm hoping to help you out. Reggie and I have talked and I think you
are in the best position to release the information we want out there."

"How about all of it?" Jessica said. "I don't think any of this needs to
be be kept from the world."

"I agree," said the voice. "But some information resembles drops of
water and some are nuclear bombs."

"I think the bombs need to be handled delicately," Jessica answered.
"The water should flow, regardless."

"I want to help," the voice said.

"Think of this as a job interview," Jessica said. "Let's begin to
interview."

"You're watching the international broadcast feed of World News Network.
The room you are sitting in has four wired-appliances. The blanket you
have pulled over your lap is the one thing you allowed yourself to bring
from Washington when you went underground and left the country. You had
it in your townhouse. It was hardly used and usually hung on the back of
your couch."

"Stop it," Jessica hissed. "You've made your point. You're an insider.
You've got access to some good hardware and skills to use it. I bet you
have more information that you would like to release. More than what we
know about right now."

A pause crept between them.

"I don't know," the voice announced. "I don't know if I want to get into
bed with you."

"We haven't even had dinner yet," Jessica replied.

"Drinks neither," the voice chuckled.

"Listen," Jessica threw the blanket off her lap and leaned forward. "I
know you’re watching right now. I know I'm a target. My face has been on
the fucking news. Yours hasn't. You're still anonymous and hungry to get
this shit off your chest. Out of your mind. It's a relief. Trust me. I
was tired of keeping it bottled up in my brain."

"I built it," the voice sighed. "God. That is a relief just to admit to
someone."

Jessica's skin and blood flashed cold, as if an icy wind suddenly
engulfed her.

"What do you mean?" Jessica asked.

"I built it. Designed it. The whole system. Everything from the back
doors to the front doors. The piggy-backing into wired components and
webcams, all of it. To tell you the truth I was just looking for a new
perspective when I was studying technology. It wasn't easy, but it was
fun. It was just the beginning."

Jessica realized this wasn't just a hacker looking to piss on big
brother. He was big brother.

"I didn't intend all of this," the voice cracked. "I was just taking my
theories and designs to their logical levels and fleshing them out. I
didn't think I'd get into every home in the world and peek into every
pocket of every person."

"It's okay," Jessica said.

"See, that's just it. I don't think it is okay," the voice said. "It
shouldn't be okay. Oppenheimer said it best after the first successful
test of the nuclear bomb, '...now I have become Death, the destroyer of
worlds.'"

"It's not the end," Jessica tried to comfort him. "There is always a new
angle. A new beginning."

"There is no putting this genie back in the bottle, Jessica."

He used her real name, causing chills down her spine.

"It won't stop unless we rewind time."

"Stopping it doesn't need a time machine," Jessica cautioned.

"No, it's like giving a bottle to an alcoholic. They won't just not
drink it. They'll use it and slurp it down. The headache the next
morning will just be the price of victory...over the bottle the night
before."

"So how do we do it?" Jessica dared to push the conversation along.

"They don't just give it up," the voice said. "They won't. They can't.
You keep a heroin addict from injecting themselves by breaking their
arms. The cast keeps them from touching the vein. That's the only way."

"You make it sound so final and desperate." The tone in his tone was
beginning to scare Jessica.

"It is, because it is. I've lived this as my life," the voice said.
"Jessica, I'm sending you some files. Let them lose. They'll catch on
like wildfire. I'll begin to dismantle and limit from my end, but not
before Rome burns."

The connection clicked off. Files filled Jessica's secure-link.
